Optimal Estimation of Schatten Norms of a rectangular Matrix
Solène Thépaut and Nicolas Verzelen

Abstract
We consider the twin problems of estimating the effective rank and the Schatten norms ‖ 𝐀 ‖ s \|\mathbf{A}\|_{s} of a rectangular p × q p\times q matrix 𝐀 \mathbf{A} from noisy observations. When s s is an even integer, we introduce a polynomial-time estimator of ‖ 𝐀 ‖ s \|\mathbf{A}\|_{s} that achieves the minimax rate ( p ​ q ) 1 / 4 (pq)^{1/4} . Interestingly, this optimal rate does not depend on the underlying rank of the matrix 𝐀 \mathbf{A} . When s s is not an even integer, the optimal rate is much slower. A simple thresholding estimator of the singular values achieves the rate ( q ∧ p ) ​ ( p ​ q ) 1 / 4 (q\wedge p)(pq)^{1/4} , which turns out to be optimal up to a logarithmic multiplicative term. The tight minimax rate is achieved by a more involved polynomial approximation method. This allows us to build estimators for a class of effective rank indices. As a byproduct, we also characterize the minimax rate for estimating the sequence of singular values of a matrix.



keywords
[class=MSC]
Primary 62H12
; secondary 62C20

keywords

schatten norm, effective rank , matrix estimation , random matrix
, polynomial approximation

1
Introduction

In many modern problems, scientists are faced with a large data matrix
\mathbf{Y}
, which is often assumed to be sum of a signal matrix
\mathbf{A}
and a background noise. Under some structural assumptions on the signal such as small rank, it is possible to precisely recover the signal matrix
\mathbf{A}
, for instance with singular value thresholding methods (see e.g.
[
8
,
14
]
). Low-rank assumptions are at the heart of unsupervised learning methods, including clustering or biclustering. In this work, we focus our attention on estimating specific functionals of the matrix
\mathbf{A}
that are connection to its rank. On the one hand, the rank or the effective rank of the signal help to assess the relevance of low-rank based procedures. On the other hand, evaluating the rank (or the effective rank) of
\mathbf{A}
may also be an objective per se as a characterization of the complexity of the signal matrix
\mathbf{A}
. We argue below that inferring the effective rank of a matrix
\mathbf{A}
mostly boils down to estimating Schatten norms of
\mathbf{A}
. This manuscript is dedicated to the latter estimation problem.

To be more specific, we consider the signal plus noise model

\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{E}\ ,

(1)

where
\mathbf{A}
is the unobserved signal and
\mathbf{E}
is a
p\times q
noise matrix with independent entries following a standard normal distribution. Extension to non-Gaussian noise are dealt with at the end of the manuscript.
Without loss of generality the noise variance is set to one. Also, we assume throughout the manuscript that
p\geq q
.

Given a
p\times q
matrix
\mathbf{A}
, we write
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\geq\sigma_{2}(\mathbf{A})\ldots\geq\sigma_{q}(\mathbf{A})\geq 0
for its ordered sequence of singular values.
The rank of a matrix
\mathbf{A}
corresponds to its number of positive singular values.
For any
s\geq 1
, the
s
-Schatten norm of
\mathbf{A}
is defined as the
l_{s}
norm of its sequence of singular values, that is

\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sigma^{s}_{i}(\mathbf{A})\ .

(2)

For
s=\infty
, we define
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}=\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})
as the operator norm of
\mathbf{A}
.
More generally, given a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we define the functional
f_{\sigma}(A)
by
f_{\sigma}(\mathbf{A})=\sum_{i=1}^{q}f(\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A}))
. For instance,
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}^{s}=f_{\sigma}(\mathbf{A})
when one takes
f:x\mapsto x^{s}
. This manuscript is dedicated to the problem of estimating the Schatten norms of
\mathbf{A}
from a single noisy observation
\mathbf{Y}
. Before describing our contribution, we explain how Schatten norms are related to effective rank measures.

1.1
Effective Rank of a matrix

Since the rank of a matrix is very sensitive to small perturbations, it is difficult to estimate it from
\mathbf{Y}
. Furthermore, a large rank matrix
\mathbf{A}
may have only few large singular values together with many small singular values. For such a matrix, the rank is poorly informative on the structure of
\mathbf{A}
.
As an alternative, various notions of effective ranks have been introduced. In particular, some of these notions of effective rank are at the heart of high-dimensional or infinite-dimensional probabilistic results. For instance, in
[
20
]
, Koltchinskii and Lounici consider, for a non-negative symmetric matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
, the indicator
\tr[{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}]/\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{\infty}=\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{1}/\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{\infty}
. For a rectangular matrix
\mathbf{A}
, one can think of two extensions of this index, depending on whether we work directly with the singular values of
\mathbf{A}
or with the singular values of the square matrix
\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}
.

\mathrm{ER}_{1,\infty}(\mathbf{A})=\frac{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}}\,\quad;\quad\quad\quad\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})=\mathrm{ER}_{1,\infty}(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})=\frac{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2}_{\infty}}\ .

(3)

Other notions of effective ranks are based on entropy measures. For instance Roy and Veterlli
[
33
]
introduce the Shannon effective rank:

\mathrm{ER}_{1,1}(\mathbf{A})=\exp\left[-\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}}\log\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}}\right)\right]\ ,

(4)

which corresponds to the Shannon effective number of the distribution induced by the probability vector (
\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})/\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}
)
i=1,\ldots,q
. More generally, one extends any effective number index based either on probability vector associated to the singular values of
\mathbf{A}
, that is the vector (
\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})/\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}
),
i=1,\ldots,q
or to the vector (
\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{A})/\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2}_{2}
),
i=1,\ldots,q
. For the purpose of biodiversity analysis, a large class of diversity measures are considered in
[
18
]
. In particular, for any positive
s>0
and different from 1, the Hill’s effective number
[
18
]
which derived from Renyi entropy straightforwardly straightforwardly extends to the following effective rank indices

\mathrm{ER}_{1,s}(\mathbf{A})=\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}}\right)^{s/(1-s)}\,;\quad\quad\mathrm{ER}_{2,s}(\mathbf{A})=\mathrm{ER}_{1,s}(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})=\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2s}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}}\right)^{2s/(1-s)}\ .

(5)

When all the non-zero singular values of
\mathbf{A}
are equal, all these effective rank indices are equal to the true rank of
\mathbf{A}
. However, these measures differ in the way they treat heterogeneous values for the singular values. In short, smaller values of
s
in
\mathrm{ER}_{1,s}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathrm{ER}_{2,s}(\mathbf{A})
are more prone to take into account smaller singular values in the effective rank. See
[
18
]
and references therein for further discussions.

Most work around effective ranks consider noiseless setting
[
33
]
, but the matrix
\mathbf{A}
is sometimes allowed to be only partially observed (e.g
[
2
]
). In this work, we tackle the general problem of estimating the effective rank of
\mathbf{A}
relying on a single noisy observation
\mathbf{Y}
of
\mathbf{A}
. To the exception of the Shannon effective rank (
4
), all the other effective rank measures are ratio of Schatten norms of
\mathbf{A}
, so that evaluating the former amounts to estimating well the latter. Besides, assessing the intrinsic difficulty of estimating Schatten norms
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}
can lead the statistician to favor some specific effective rank measures, that are based on Schatten norm
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}
that are easier to estimate.

1.2
Our Contribution

In most of the manuscript, we focus our attention to the Gaussian signal plus noise matrix model (
1
). As a warm-up, we consider the Frobenius norm
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}
and we prove that the simple quadratic estimator
(\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{2}^{2}-pq)^{1/2}_{+}
, where
x_{+}=\max(x,0)
achieves the optimal risk
(pq)^{1/4}
. Interestingly, this risk
(pq)^{1/4}
cannot be improved by any estimator even if the matrix
\mathbf{A}
is additionally known to be of rank at most one. Then, we establish that a non-linear transformation of
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{Y})
estimates
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}=\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})
with the same optimal error rates
(pq)^{1/4}
.

Regarding general even norms
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}
where
k
is any integer, we first remark that
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}=tr[(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})^{2k}]
is a polynomial with respect to the entries of
\mathbf{A}
. This allows us to build an unbiased estimator
U_{k}
of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}
based on Hermite polynomials. Relying on the invariance of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}
by left and right orthogonal transformations, we establish that this estimator has a simple expression as an algebraic combination of monomials of the form
tr[(\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{Y})^{l}]
so that the estimator
U_{k}
can be efficiently computed. One of our main result is general variance upper bound for
U_{k}
, which allows us to prove that the estimator
(U_{k})_{+}^{1/(2k)}
achieves the optimal risk
(pq)^{1/4}
uniformly over all matrices
\mathbf{A}
.

Regarding general norms
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}
where
s\geq 1
is not an even integer, we first exhibit a simple plug-in estimator based on a linear transformation of the empirical singular values (
\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{Y})
) that achieves a much higher error of the order
q^{1/s}(pq)^{1/4}
(compare with
(pq)^{1/4}
for even Schatten norms). Our second main result is a minimax lower bound of order
\frac{q^{1/s}}{\log^{s}(q)}(pq)^{1/4}
stating that it is impossible to estimate non-even Schatten norms at a much faster rate. Using polynomial approximation techniques that approximate
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}
by a linear combination of even Schatten norms
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}
, we are able to partially close this logarithmic gap between our upper and lower minimax bounds.

As a first application of our estimator
U_{2k}
, we apply a moment matching method introduced by
[
21
]
in a covariance estimation model to estimate the whole sequence of singular values
\mathbf{A}
at the minimax rate
\tfrac{q}{\log(q)}(pq)^{1/4}
. As a second application of our findings, we build an estimator of the effective rank
\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}
and control its error in probability. Besides, we argue why Effective ranks measures
\mathrm{ER}_{2,s}
where
s\geq 2
is an integer are easier to estimate than other definitions (
3
,
4
,
5
).

Finally, we extend our analysis to a signal plus noise model with a general subGaussian noise distribution. Our third main result is an error control of the estimator
(U_{k})_{+}^{1/(2k)}
for even Schatten norm
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}
.
Quite surprisingly, we establish that, while the definition of
U_{k}
heavily depends on the sequence of moments of the normal distribution,
(U_{k})_{+}^{1/(2k)}
achieves the same convergence rate
(qp)^{1/4}
as for Gaussian noise. However, the analysis turns out to be much more involved in comparison to Gaussian case.

1.3
Related Literature

Low rank Matrix Estimation and Detection.

There is a long line of work regarding the estimation of the matrix
\mathbf{A}
when
\mathbf{A}
is assumed to be low-rank or approximately low-rank. Procedures based on singular value thresholding procedures
[
14
,
8
]
, that is estimating
\mathbf{A}
by setting small singular values of
\mathbf{Y}
to 0, have been proved to achieve near optimal performances. Donoho and Gavish
[
12
]
have assessed the asymptotic risk of singular value soft-thresholding procedures in an asymptotic framework where the rank is proportional to
q
and
p
is proportional to
q
.
[
34
,
27
]
have introduced other non-linear singular values shrinkage estimators that turn out to achieve smaller risks (the constant in front of the rates is slightly better), at least in an asymptotic framework where
p/q\rightarrow c\in(0,\infty)
and
\mathbf{A}
has finite rank low-rank. As explained in Section
2.2
, some of our Schatten norm estimators are reminiscent of those non linear shrinkage functions.

Asymptotic estimation of the singular values in the asymptotic
p/q\rightarrow c
.

In asymptotic matrix analysis, most work have been devoted to the setting where
p/q\rightarrow c\in(0,\infty)
. In that literature, the model (
1
) is coined as the information plus noise model
[
10
]
. Given the symmetric matrix
\frac{1}{p}\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}
with ordered eigenvalue
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{q}
, define the spectral measure as
\mu_{\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}}:=\frac{1}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\delta_{\lambda_{i}}
. In the specific case where
\mathbf{A}=0
, it has been established
[
1
]
that
\mu_{\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}}
converges weakly towards the Marchenko-Pastur distribution. More generally, when the spectral measure of
\frac{1}{p}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}
converges to a probability measure,
[
38
]
have characterized the limit of
\mu_{\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}/p}
through its Stieljes transform. Given that characterization, one may try to invert the mapping from the limit of
\mu_{\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}/p}
to the limit of
\mu_{\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}/p}
to estimate some functional of the spectrum of
\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}
from
\mathbf{Y}
. In some specific case, where
\mathbf{A}
has asymptotically a finite number
k
of distinct singular values, consistency of these singular values has been derived. See
[
10
, Ch.8]
and references therein for more details. Given a consistent estimation of the spectral measure, on may plug it to estimate the Schatten norms. However, results in this line of work are not comparable to ours as they are restricted to very specific settings.

Matrix Sketching.

In computer science, there is an active line of research around the problem of matrix sketching which can be defined as follows: the practitioner has access to some entries of the matrix
\mathbf{A}
or to some linear combination of the entries of the matrix
\mathbf{A}
. In this noiseless problem, one aims at recovering functionals of
\mathbf{A}
such as a Schatten norm or the rank using the smallest possible budget. See
[
26
,
19
,
2
]
. As in our noisy framework,
[
26
]
emphasizes that estimating even Schatten norms using sketches seems easier than estimating non-even Schatten norms. Apart from this, the techniques for both the lower and upper bounds highly differ from our settings.

Estimation of non linear functionals.

Schatten norms are non-linear functionals of the entries of
\mathbf{A}
. Vectorizing the matrices
\mathbf{Y}
,
\mathbf{A}
, and
\mathbf{E}
, we can rewrite our problem in the Gaussian sequence model. In particular, estimating the 2-Schatten norm is equivalent to estimating the
l_{2}
norm of a vector in the Gaussian sequence model. See
[
13
,
9
]
and references therein for an account of work of quadratic functional estimation. More generally, the estimation of the
l_{r}
norm of a vector has been addressed in
[
24
,
7
,
15
]
. However, apart from the specific case
s=2
,
s
-Schatten norms do no correspond to
l_{r}
norm and the methodology developed in the Gaussian sequence model does not extend to our setting.
In discrete distribution estimation, optimal rates of convergence for Shannon entropy, Renyi entropy have been respectively established in
[
40
]
and
[
17
]
(see also
[
16
]
) and the polynomial approximation techniques in Section
4
are inspired from this literature.

Closer to our settings, Kong and Valiant
[
21
]
have considered the problem of estimating even Schatten norms of a covariance matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
given a
n
-sample of a mean zero random vector with covariance matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.
Interestingly, their estimator exhibit a low computational complexity and is unbiased for general noise distributions.
Also, they rely on these Schatten norms to estimate the whole sequence of eigenvalues of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
. Our application to estimating the sequence of singular values is directly inspired from their work.
However, their setting much differs from ours as they have access to
n
independent observations so that they can easily build an unbiased estimator of any polynomials in
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
. In contrast, we only have a single observation of the matrix
\mathbf{Y}
so that higher-degree polynomials in
\mathbf{A}
are more difficult to estimate for non-Gaussian noise distribution.
Also, Kong and Valiant
[
21
]
do not assess at all the optimality of their rates. In contrast, we provide matching minimax lower bound in our information plus noise model. As an aside, our proof approach could perhaps extend to their covariance framework.

1.4
Notation

In this work,
c
,
c_{1}
,
c^{\prime}
denote numerical positive constants that may vary from line to line. We write that two quantities
u
and
v
satisfy
u\lesssim v
if there exists a numerical constant
c
such that
u\leq cv
.
We recall that norm of the form
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}
for
(s\in[1,\infty]
) correspond to the
s
-Schatten norms.
In contrast, for
s\in[1,\infty]
, we write
|\mathbf{A}|_{s}
and
|u|_{s}
for entry-wise
l_{s}
norms of the matrix
\mathbf{A}
and the vector
u
.

Section
2
is devoted to the simple case of Frobenius norm estimation and provides both minimax upper and lower bounds. In Section
2.2
, we consider the problem of estimating the operator norm
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}
. General even norms are addressed in Section
3
, whereas non-even norms are studied in Section
4
. In Section
6
, we extend our results to general noise distributions.
Finally, we come back to the problem of effective estimation together with some open questions in the discussion Section. Proofs are postponed to the appendix.

2
Frobenius and Operator Norm

2.1
Frobenius Norms

We first estimate
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i,j}\mathbf{A}_{ij}^{2}
and then we deduce an estimator of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}
. Remark that
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}
can be interpreted as a square
l_{2}
norm in a Gaussian sequence model. The latter problem is classical – see e.g.
[
9
]
and our simple estimator
U_{1}
for
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}
has been already analyzed in the Gaussian sequence model. Still, we provide here a self-contained analysis as a warming-up for general even Schatten norms.
Since
\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{2}^{2}
follows a non-central
\chi^{2}
distribution, we derive from standard computations for the normal random variables that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{2}^{2}]=\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}+pq
. This leads us to the following unbiased estimator

U_{1}=\|\mathbf{\mathbf{Y}}\|_{2}^{2}-pq\ .

(6)

Proposition 2.1
.

For any
p\times q
matrix
\mathbf{A}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|U_{1}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2pq}+2\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|(U_{1})_{+}^{1/2}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3(pq)^{\frac{1}{4}}\kern 5.0pt.

Remark
: Since estimating
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}
amounts to estimating the square
l_{2}
norm (resp.
l_{2}
norm) of a noisy vector of size
pq
, this problem has been thoroughly studied in the literature. See
[
9
]
and references therein. In particular, the counterparts of
U_{1}
and
(U_{1})_{+}^{1/2}
in the vector setting were already analyzed in
[
9
]
. Still, we provide proofs of the above proposition for the sake of completeness. Next, we assess the optimality of Proposition
2.1
by providing a minimax lower bound.

Proposition 2.2
.

There exists a numerical constant
c>0
such that

\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\ \rank(\mathbf{A})\leq 1}\mathbb{E}\left[|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}|\right]\geq c(pq)^{1/4}\ .

One cannot estimate
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}
uniformly over all matrices
\mathbf{A}
at a rate faster than
(pq)^{1/4}
. More importantly, even when one restricts itself to the much simpler class of matrices of rank at most one, it is impossible to achieve a faster rate.
This theorem is proved using Le Cam’s approach by constructing a discrete prior distribution on the space of rank-one matrix – see the proof for more details.

Since, for a rank-one matrix
\mathbf{A}
, all Schatten norms are equal to
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})
, we straightforwardly deduce a minimax lower bound for all Schatten norms.

Corollary 2.3
.

For any real number
s\geq 1
, we have

\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}}\mathbb{E}\left[|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}|\right]\geq\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\ \rank(\mathbf{A})\leq 1}\mathbb{E}\left[|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}|\right]\geq c(pq)^{1/4}\ ,

where
c
is as in Proposition
2.2
.

As proved later in the manuscript, this minimax lower bound turns out to be tight when
s
is an even integer.

2.2
Operator Norm
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}

In this subsection, we address the case
s=\infty
which amounts to estimating
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}=\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})
. In view of Corollary
2.3
, the optimal estimation risk is at least of the order of
(pq)^{1/4}
for general matrices
\mathbf{A}
.
From the decomposition
\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{E}
, we may be tempted to use plug-in estimators of the form
\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}
.

Lemma 2.4
.

For any matrix
\mathbf{A}
, we have

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}|]\leq\sqrt{p}+\sqrt{q}\ .

Conversely, there exist two positive numerical constants
c
and
c^{\prime}
such that, for
p
large enough,

\sup_{\mathbf{A},\,\rank(\mathbf{A})\leq 1}\ \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}|]\geq c^{\prime}\sqrt{p}\ .

When
p
is of the same order as
q
, that is
\mathbf{A}
is almost a square matrix,
\sqrt{p}
is of the same order as
(pq)^{1/4}
and these simple plug-in estimators turn out to be optimal. However, for highly rectangular matrices, these estimators achieve a much slower rate than the minimax lower bound. To improve the rate from
\sqrt{p}
to
(pq)^{1/4}
, we start by estimating the operator norm
\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}=\sigma_{1}^{2}(\mathbf{A})
. Define the
q\times q
matrix
\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}
where
\mathbf{I}_{q}
is the identity matrix. Simple calculations lead to
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{W}]=\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}
. This motivates us to to estimate
\sigma_{1}^{2}(\mathbf{A})
by
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{W})=\sigma_{1}^{2}(\mathbf{Y})-p
.

Proposition 2.5
.

There exists a numerical constant
c>0
such that, for any matrix
\mathbf{A}
, one has

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\sigma_{1}^{2}(\mathbf{Y})-p-\sigma_{1}^{2}(\mathbf{A})|\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3\sqrt{pq}+4\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\sqrt{q}+8e^{-p/4}+\sqrt{64\pi p}\ ;

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\left(\sigma_{1}^{2}(\mathbf{Y})-p\right)_{+}^{1/2}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}|\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(pq\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\ .

(7)

This new estimator
\left(\sigma_{1}^{2}(\mathbf{Y})-p\right)_{+}^{1/2}
achieves the optimal rate
(pq)^{1/4}
uniformly over all possible matrices
\mathbf{A}
. As for the Frobenius norm, a low-rank assumption on
\mathbf{A}
does not ease the operator norm estimation problem.

From asymptotic considerations where
p/n\rightarrow c^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)
and
\mathbf{A}
has finite rank
r
, Shabalin and Nobel
[
34
]
have introduced an estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
of
\mathbf{A}
satisfying

\sigma^{2}_{i}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}})=\frac{1}{2}\left[\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{Y})-p-q+\sqrt{[\sigma^{2}_{i}(\mathbf{Y})-p-q]^{2}-4pq}\right]\ ,

when
\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{Y})
is large enough. When
q
is small compared to
p
, then the quantity
\sigma_{1}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}})
does not much differ from our estimator
\sqrt{(\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{Y})-p)_{+}}
. In fact, it is not hard to prove that
\sigma_{1}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}})
also estimates
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}
at the optimal rate
(pq)^{1/4}
in analogy to (
7
).

3
Estimation of General even norms

We now turn to the case of general even Schatten norms. In this section,
k
stands for a positive integer and we are interested in estimating
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}=[\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sigma^{2k}_{i}(\mathbf{A})]^{2k}
. We will show in this section that, by carefully studying an unbiased estimator of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}
, it is possible to estimate
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}
at the desired rate
(pq)^{1/4}
. Recall that, when
k
is an integer, we have the following identity

\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}=\tr[(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})^{k}]=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{p}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}=1}^{q}\prod_{t=1}^{k}\mathbf{A}_{i_{t}j_{t}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{t}j_{t+1}}\ ,

with the convention
j_{k+1}=j_{1}
. Obviously,
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}
is a polynomial of order
2k
with respect to the entries of
\mathbf{A}
. Since
\mathbf{Y}_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}_{ij},1)
, one can unbiasedly estimates each monomial using polynomials in the entries of
\mathbf{Y}
.

Write
\phi(y)=e^{-y^{2}/2}(2\pi)^{-1/2}
for the density of the standard normal random variables. For a positive integer
r
, we define the Hermite polynomial of degree
r
by the equation

\frac{d^{r}}{dy^{r}}\phi(y)=(-1)^{r}H_{r}(y)\phi(y)

(8)

It is well known (see e.g.
[
7
]
) that, for
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(x,1)
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{r}(Z)]=x^{r}
. Given two sequences
i=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
and
j=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k})
, we denote
N_{rs}(ij)
the number of occurrences of the
2
-tuple
(r,s)
in the sequences
(i_{t},j_{t})
and
(i_{t},j_{t+1})
with
t=1,\ldots,k
(recall that, by convention
i_{k+1}=i_{1}
). Then, the estimator

U_{k}=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{p}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}=1}^{q}\prod_{r=1}^{p}\prod_{s=1}^{q}H_{N_{rs}(i,j)}(\mathbf{Y}_{rs})

(9)

obviously satisfies
\mathbb{E}[U_{k}]=\tr[(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})^{k}]=\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}
.

Although it is easy to deduce the expectation
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[U_{k}]
from (
9
), this definition is not convenient from a practical perspective as it may suggest that
O(p^{k}q^{k})
operations are needed to evaluate
U_{k}
. In fact, it turns out that
U_{k}
simplifies as an algebraic combination of Schatten norms of
\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}
– see the next proposition.

Given a positive integer
l
, we write
\mathcal{S}[l]
for the collection of integer-valued sequences
s
whose entries are nondecreasing and that sum to
l
. In other words, any vector
s\in\mathcal{S}[l]
satisfies
1\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq\ldots\leq s_{|s|}
and
\sum_{i}s_{i}=l
.

Proposition 3.1
.

There exist coefficients
\alpha_{s}
(depending only on
p
and
q
) such that

U_{k}=\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{k}]+\alpha_{0}+\sum_{l=1}^{k-1}\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}[l]}\alpha_{s}\prod_{i=1}^{|s|}\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{s_{i}}]\ .

(10)

The proof of this proposition relies on the orthogonal invariance of the Gaussian distribution and on the representation of symmetric polynomials by newton sums. As a consequence of this result, the estimator
U_{k}
can be computed in
O((p+k)q^{2}+e^{c\sqrt{k}})
operations. Indeed, we first evaluate and store all the
2l
Schatten norms of
Y
for
l=1,\ldots,k
and then we compute
\sum_{l=1}^{k-1}|\mathcal{S}[l]|
combinations of these norms. Since
|\mathcal{S}[l]|
is the number of partition of the integer
l
, we know from Hardy and Ramanujan’s asymptotic formula that
\log(|S[l]|)\sim\pi\sqrt{2l/3}
.

Remark
: In the specific case
k=1
, we have already established in the previous section that
U_{1}=\tr[\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}]-pq
. In general, the proof of Proposition (
3.1
) only ensures the existence of the coefficients in (
10
). Still, by computing the expectation of

\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{s}]
, one can recursively debias
\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{k}]
to work out the expression of
U_{k}
. As an example, the following proposition provides an explicit expression for
U_{2}
and
U_{3}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

For any
q\leq p
, we have

\displaystyle U_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{2}]-2(p+q+1)\tr[\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}]+pq(1+p+q)\ ;

\displaystyle U_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{3}]-3(p+q+1)\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{2}]+3[p^{2}+q^{2}+pq+p+q-2]\tr[\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}]

\displaystyle+pq\left[-p^{2}-q^{2}+5\right]\ .

As another consequence of Proposition
3.1
, the distribution of
U_{k}
is invariant by left and right orthogonal transformations of
\mathbf{Y}
. Hence, we can assume henceforth that
\mathbf{A}
is null outside its diagonal and that
\mathbf{A}_{ii}=\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})
for
i=1,\ldots,q
. This observation allows us to work out the variance of
U_{k}
in the next theorem.

Theorem 3.3
.

For any integer
k\geq 2
, and any
p\geq q
, we have

\operatorname{Var}{(U_{k})}\leq c^{k}\left[(pq)^{k}+p\|\mathbf{A}\|_{4k-4}^{4k-4}+\|\mathbf{A}\|_{4k-2}^{4k-2}+p^{k}k^{3k}+k^{6k}\right]\ ,

where
c
is a numerical constant.

The proof is based on some combinatorial arguments: starting from the Hermite definition (
9
) of
U_{k}
, we first establish necessary conditions on sequences
(i_{1},j_{1})
,…,
(i_{k},j_{k})
and
(i^{\prime}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1})
,…,
(i^{\prime}_{k},j^{\prime}_{k})
so that the corresponding covariance is non-zero. Then, we count the remaining sequences building on earlier ideas on random matrix analysis
[
1
]
.
From this theorem, we deduce a risk bound for the estimator
(U_{k})_{+}^{1/2k}
of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}
.

Corollary 3.4
.

For any positive integer
k
and any
p\times q
matrix
\mathbf{A}
one has

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[|(U_{k})_{+}^{1/(2k)}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}|\right]\leq c^{k}\left[(pq)^{1/4}+p^{1/4}k^{3/4}+k^{3/2}\right]\ ,

(11)

where
c
is a positive numerical constant.

Interestingly, the estimator
(U_{k})_{+}^{1/(2k)}
achieves the optimal rate
(pq)^{1/4}
for any even norm. This implies that, as for Frobenius norm estimation, estimating the
2k
-Schatten norm of a small rank matrix
\mathbf{A}
is as difficult as estimating a full rank matrix.

4
Estimation of general non-even norms

4.1
Simple estimators

In contrast to even Schatten norms, one first observes that it is impossible to devise unbiased estimators of non-even norms because such functionals are not smooth with respect to
\mathbf{A}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

For all square integrable estimators
\varphi(\mathbf{Y})
and all
r>0
and, there exists a matrix
\mathbf{A}
such that
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}\leq r
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\varphi(\mathbf{Y})]\neq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}
.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

The proof arguments are classical. For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, consider the matrix
\mathbf{A}_{t}
such that
(\mathbf{A}_{t})_{1,1}=t
and
(\mathbf{A}_{t})_{i,j}=0
otherwise. Obviously
\|\mathbf{A}_{t}\|_{1}=|t|
. The function
t\mapsto\|\mathbf{A}_{t}\|_{1}
is not differentiable at
0
. In contrast, the function
t\mapsto\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\mathbf{Y})]=(2\pi)^{-pq/2}\int\varphi(\mathbf{Y})e^{-\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{2}^{2}/2-t^{2}/2+t\mathbf{Y}_{11}}d\mathbf{Y}
is differentiable by Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem. As a consequence, both functions cannot match in any neighborhood of
0
.
∎

Consider any real number
s\geq 1
. As a starting point and to assess the quality of more refined procedure, we consider the plug-in estimator
\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{s}
that simply evaluates the
s
-Schatten norm of the observed matrix
\mathbf{Y}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For any matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
and any
s\geq 1
, we have
\mathbb{E}\left[|\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}|\right]\leq 2q^{1/s}\sqrt{p}
.

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

From the triangular inequality, we deduce that
\mathbb{E}\left[|\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}|\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbf{E}\|_{s}\right]
. Since
\|\mathbf{E}\|_{s}\leq q^{1/s}\|\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}
, we simply need to bound the expected operator norm of
\mathbf{E}
. By Lemma
A.1
in the appendix, we have
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\|\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}]\leq\sqrt{p}+\sqrt{q}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark
: The risk bound achieved by
\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{s}
is much larger than what has been obtained by unbiased estimators of the even norm. Even for square matrices
(p=q)
, Lemma
4.2
enforces a rate of the order
p^{1/s}\sqrt{p}
whereas even Schatten norms can be estimated at the much faster rate
\sqrt{p}
.

Remark
: As in Section
2.2
, it turns out that the risk upper bound
q^{1/s}\sqrt{p}
cannot be significantly improved for this specific estimator
\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{s}
. Indeed, consider the specific case
\mathbf{A}=0
. For any
p\geq 4q
, we have
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}|]\geq cq^{1/s}\sqrt{p}
. Indeed,
\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{s}\geq q^{1/s}\sigma_{q}(\mathbf{Y})
, and we deduce from Lemma
A.2
, that with probability higher than
1-e^{-q/8}
,
\sigma_{q}(\mathbf{Y})\geq\sqrt{p}-3/2\sqrt{q}\geq\sqrt{p}/4
. The result follows.

Next, we improve over the rate
p^{1/s}\sqrt{p}
by relying on a correction of the empirical singular values in the same spirit as what we did for estimating the operator norm
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}
. Consider the centered Gram matrix
\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}
and observe that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{W}]=\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}]=\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\ .

As a consequence,
\mathbf{W}
is an unbiased estimator
\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}
, which could suggest that the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{W}
are close to that of
\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}
, which in turn are the square of the singular values of
\mathbf{A}
. Since the
i
-th eigenvalues of
\mathbf{W}
equals
\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{Y})-p
, this leads us to considering the following estimator of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}

T_{s}=\left[\sum_{i=1}^{q}[(\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{Y})-p)_{+}]^{s/2}\right]^{1/s}\ .

(12)

Proposition 4.3
.

For any matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
and any
s\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|T^{s}_{s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}|]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c^{s}\left[q[(p\vee s)(q\vee s)]^{s/4}+\sqrt{q}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s-1}^{s-1}+{\mathbf{1}}_{s\geq 2}\sqrt{pq}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s-2}^{s-2}\right]\ ;

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|T_{s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}|]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c^{s}q^{1/s}[(p\vee s)(q\vee s)]^{1/4}\ ,

(13)

for some positive numerical constant
c>0
.

The proof of this result relies on interlacing inequalities for eigenvalues (Corollary III. 1.5 in
[
5
]
).
In comparison to the naive plug-in estimator
\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{s}
, the
q^{1/s}\sqrt{p}
factor has been replaced by
q^{1/s}(pq)^{1/4}
. For highly rectangular matrices, there is therefore a significant benefit to correcting the singular values. Still,
this risk bound is still
q^{1/s}
times higher than the optimal risk bound for even Schatten norms.

In the remainder of this section, we establish that, at least when
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}
is not too high this rate can be improved by a logarithmic factor by approximating
\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}
by a suitable linear combination of even Schatten norms
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}
. Then, we show that this new rate turns out to be minimax optimal.

4.2
Improved risk bounds by polynomial approximation

Introducing the function
\psi_{s}:x\mapsto x^{s/2}
for
x>0
, we observe that
\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sigma^{s}_{i}(\mathbf{A})=\sum_{i=1}^{q}\psi_{s}(\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}))
. Following ideas pioneered in Lepski et al.
[
24
,
7
,
15
]
for estimating the
l_{q}
norm of a vector in the Gaussian sequence model, we suggest to approximate the function
\psi_{s}
by some polynomial function
P=\sum_{k=0}^{K}a_{k}x^{k}
and then to build an unbiased estimator of
\sum_{i=1}^{q}P(\sigma^{2}_{i}(\mathbf{A}))
as a proxy for
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}^{s}
.
Indeed,
\sum_{i=1}^{q}P(\sigma^{2}_{i}(\mathbf{A}))=a_{0}q+\sum_{k=1}^{\deg(P)}a_{k}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}
is a weighted sum of even Schatten norms
\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2k}_{2k}
with
k
ranging from one to the degree of
P
and we can rely on the results of Section
3
to build an unbiased estimator of this quantity.

Given a polynomial real polynomial
P
of degree
K
and a tuning parameter
M>1
, we therefore define the estimator

G_{s}[P;\mathbf{Y}]=M^{s}(pq)^{s/4}\left[a_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}a_{k}\frac{U_{k}}{(M(pq)^{1/4})^{2k}}\right]\ ,

(14)

where
U_{k}
is the unbiased estimator of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}
defined in (
9
).

For a continuous function
g
defined on an interval
I
, we denote
|g|_{\infty,I}=\sup_{x\in I}|g(x)|
its supremum norm. The following proposition bounds the risk of
G_{s}[P;\mathbf{Y}]
in terms of its approximation error to the function
\psi_{s}
and of a variance-like expression growing super-exponentially with the degree of the polynomial.

Proposition 4.4
.

Consider any polynomial
P
of degree
K\leq q^{1/3}
. Assuming that
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\leq M(pq)^{1/4}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[|G_{s}[P;\mathbf{Y}]-\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}|\right]\leq M^{s}(pq)^{s/4}\left[q|\psi_{s}-P|_{\infty,[0,1]}+\frac{c^{K}}{M^{2}\wedge[M(p/q)^{1/4}]}|a|_{\infty}\right]\ .

(15)

In order to optimize the bound in (
15
), we want to choose
P
to minimize the approximation error
|\psi_{s}-P|_{\infty,[0,1]}
while having coefficients
|a|_{\infty}
not too large.

4.2.1
Nuclear Norm
(s=1)

To ease the presentation, we first focus on the nuclear norm, the general case being handled below.
In general, devising the best polynomial approximation of a function on a compact is a difficult problem. Fortunately, studying the best polynomial approximation of the square root function over
[0,1]
boils down to studying the best polynomial approximation of the absolute function on
[-1,1]
, the latter problem being well studied in the approximation theory literature
[
32
]
. Indeed, given a polynomial
Q
, and any
x\in[-1,1]
, we have
||x|-Q(x^{2})|=|\sqrt{x^{2}}-Q(x^{2})|
so that
\sup_{x\in[-1,1]}||x|-Q(x^{2})|=\sup_{x\in[0,1]}|\sqrt{x}-Q(x)|
.

In particular, an explicit and nearly optimal approximation of the absolute function by a degree
K
Polynomial is given by its Chebychev’s expansion.
The Chebychev polynomial (of the first kind) of degree
k
is defined by

L_{k}(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}(-1)^{j}\frac{k}{k-j}\binom{j}{k-j}2^{k-2j-1}x^{k-2j}\ .

The degree
2K
expansion of the absolute function in Chebychev basis is
B_{K}(x)=\frac{2}{\pi}L_{0}(x)+\frac{4}{\pi}\sum_{k=1}^{K}(-1)^{k+1}\frac{L_{2k}(x)}{4k^{2}-1}
which is a symmetric polynomial of degree
2K
.
Rewriting this polynomial in the canonical form as
B_{K}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{K}a^{*}_{k}x^{2k}
, we build the polynomial
P^{*}_{K}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{K}a^{*}_{k}x^{k}
so that
P^{*}_{K}(x^{2})=B_{K}(x)
. Relying on Bernstein’s
[
3
]
approximation results, we then obtain the following.

In the sequel, we write
\mathcal{P}_{n}
for the space of polynomials of degree at most
n
. Given a collection
\mathcal{F}
of functions, an interval
I
and a function
g:I\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, we denote

E_{\mathcal{F}}[g,I]=\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|f-g|_{\infty,I}\ ,

(16)

for the best uniform approximation of
g
by
\mathcal{F}
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Consider any positive integer
K
. The modified Chebychev polynomial
P^{*}_{K}
satisfies

|\psi_{1}-P^{*}_{K}|_{\infty,[0,1]}\leq\frac{2}{\pi(2K+1)}\ ,

and
|a^{*}|_{\infty}\leq 2^{3K}
. Besides, no degree
K
polynomial is able to achieve a much lower error.

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}2nE_{\mathcal{P}_{n}}\left(\psi_{1};[0,1]\right)=\beta^{*}\ ,

where
\beta^{*}\approx 0.28
is the Bernstein constant.

Plugging this bound in Proposition
4.4
and choosing well the degree
K
leads to the following result.

Proposition 4.6
.

Fix any
M>1
. Choose
K^{*}=\lfloor c\log(q)\rfloor
for a suitable numerical constant
c
. If
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\leq M(pq)^{1/4}
, then the estimator
G_{1}[P^{*}_{K^{*}};\mathbf{Y}]
satisfies

\mathbb{E}\left[|G_{1}[P^{*}_{K^{*}};\mathbf{Y}]-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}|\right]\lesssim M\frac{q}{\log(q)}(pq)^{1/4}\ .

(17)

Let us compare this risk bound with that of the plug-in estimator
T_{1}
(Proposition
4.3
). When
M
is considered as a constant, the risk in (
17
) improves by a logarithmic factor
\log(q)
the bound for
T_{1}
. As will be established in Section
4.3
below, this rate
q(pq)^{1/4}/\log(q)
turns out to be optimal.

Besides, it is possible to empirically check the hypothesis
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\leq M(pq)^{1/4}
using the estimator
U_{\infty}=[\sigma^{2}_{1}(\mathbf{Y})-p]^{1/2}_{+}
of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}
since
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|U_{\infty}-\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})|]\lesssim(pq)^{1/4}
. Hence, if
U_{\infty}
is too large, then the modified plug-in estimator
T_{1}
is perhaps preferable to
G_{1}[P^{*}_{K^{*}};\mathbf{Y}]
.

4.2.2
General case
(s\geq 1)

We turn to the general case
s\geq 1
. In view of the discussion for the nuclear norm, we first have to quantify the best polynomial approximation on
[-1,1]
of the functions
x\mapsto x^{s}
. The following result dates from Bernstein
[
4
]
and can also be found in
[
36
, Sec.7.2.1]
.

Lemma 4.7
.

There exists a function
\mu:\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\{0\}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that, for any
s>0
, which is not an even integer, we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{s}E_{\mathcal{P}_{n}}\left(|x|^{s};[-1,1]\right)=\mu(s)>0\ .

Given an integer
K
, write
P^{*}_{K;s}
a polynomial such that
P^{*}_{K;s}(x^{2})
achieves
E_{\mathcal{P}_{2K}}\left(|x|^{s};[-1,1]\right)
. As argued above in the specific case
s=1
,
P^{*}_{K,s}
approximates well the function
x^{s/2}
on
[0,1]
. This leads us to the following result.

Proposition 4.8
.

Fix any
M>1
and any
s\geq 1
. Choose
K^{*}=\lfloor c\log(q)\rfloor
for a suitable numerical constant
c
. If
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\leq M(pq)^{1/4}
, then the polynomial
G_{s}[P^{*}_{K^{*};s};\mathbf{Y}]
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|G_{s}[P^{*}_{K^{*};s};\mathbf{Y}]-\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}|\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle M^{s}\frac{q}{\log^{s}(q)}(pq)^{s/4}\ ;

(18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|G_{s}[P^{*}_{K^{*};s};\mathbf{Y}]^{1/s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}|\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle M\frac{q^{1/s}}{\log(q)}(pq)^{1/4}\ .

(19)

As for
s=1
, the estimator
G_{s}[P^{*}_{K^{*};s};\mathbf{Y}]
improves the rate by a factor
1/\log^{s}(q)
when the
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})
is not too large.
Building the estimator
G_{s}[P^{*}_{K^{*};s};\mathbf{Y}]
requires to compute the best approximation polynomial for the function
x\mapsto|x|^{s}
. Still, this can be computed offline and once and for all using the algorithm of Remez
[
31
]
. See also the discussion in
[
28
]
and in
[
17
]
.

4.3
Minimax lower bound

We now prove that the rate
\frac{q}{\log^{s}(q)\vee 1}(pq)^{s/4}
is optimal for estimating
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}^{s}
as long as
s\geq 1
is not an even integer. Since the pioneering work of Lepski et al.
[
24
]
and Cai and Low
[
7
]
, Le Cam’s method with fuzzy hypotheses has turned out to be fruitful for establishing tight minimax lower bounds for non-smooth functional estimation problems. See for instance the discussion in
[
29
]
and the survey
[
41
]
.
Although we follow the same general approach for Schatten norm estimation, the computation of the
\chi^{2}
distance between the corresponding distributions turns out to be quite challenging. Indeed, we consider the singular value decomposition
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{U}^{T}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}
of
\mathbf{A}
and fix uniform prior distribution on
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
together with some suitable prior distributions on
\mathbf{D}
. The resulting
\chi^{2}
distance involves complex spherical integrals that we are unable to work out. For this reason, we introduce a sequence of priors and use some data-processing inequalities to come back to a functional estimation problem for a rank-one matrix which turns out to be tractable.

In the sequel,
\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{k}
denote the space of the symmetric polynomials of degree less or equal to
k
.

Theorem 4.9
.

There exists a numerical constant
c>0
such that the following holds for all positive integers
4\leq q\leq p
and for all continuous functions
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
. Writing
I_{0}=[0,0.125(pq)^{1/4}]
and
k^{*}=\lceil\log(4\lceil q/2\rceil)\rceil
, we have

\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\ \|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq 0.125(pq)^{1/4}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\big|\widehat{T}-f_{\sigma}(\mathbf{A})\big|\right]\geq c\left[qE_{\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{2k^{*}}}[f;I_{0}]]-q^{1/2}\|f\|_{\infty,I_{0}}\right]_{+}\ ,

(20)

where we recall that
f_{\sigma}(\mathbf{A})=\sum_{i=1}^{q}f(\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A}))
.

The above minimax lower bound is valid for all functionals of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{q}f(\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A}))
, although the bound uninformative if
f
is a symmetric polynomial of degree less than
\log(q)
. Next, we apply this result to the functional
\psi_{s}:x\mapsto|x|^{s}
.

Corollary 4.10
.

For any
s\geq 1
, which is not an even integer, there exist constants
c_{s}>0
and
c^{\prime}_{s}>0
such that for all positive integers
q\leq p
, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\ \|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq 0.125(pq)^{1/4}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\big|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}\big|\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle c_{s}\frac{q}{\log^{s}(q)\vee 1}(pq)^{s/4}\ ;

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\ \|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq 0.125(pq)^{1/4}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\big|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}\big|\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle c^{\prime}_{s}\frac{q^{1/s}}{\log^{s}(q)\vee 1}(pq)^{1/4}\ ;

(21)

In view of Propositions
4.8
and Corollary
4.10
, the optimal rates for estimating
\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}
is of the order of
q(pq)^{s/4}/\log^{s}(q)
. Regarding the
s
-Schatten norm
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}
, there is a small logarithmic gap between the upper and lower bound except in the case where
s=1
.

5
Estimating the whole sequence of singular values

In this section, we are interested in reconstructing the sequence of singular values
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})
, …,
\sigma_{q}(\mathbf{A})
in
l_{1}
distance. In other words, we aims at building an estimator
\widehat{\sigma}_{1}
,…,
\widehat{\sigma}_{q}
such that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\sum_{i=1}^{q}|\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})-\widehat{\sigma}_{i}(\mathbf{A})|]
is as small as possible.

First, we study the simple corrected plug-in estimator
\widehat{\sigma}_{i}=[(\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{Y})-p)_{+}]^{1/2}
introduced in Section
4
. Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
4.3
, we show the following result.

Proposition 5.1
.

For any integers
p\geq q
and any
p\times q
matrix
\mathbf{A}
, the estimator
\widehat{\sigma}_{i}=[(\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{Y})-p)_{+}]^{1/2}
satisfies

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{q}|\widehat{\sigma}_{i}-\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})|\right]\lesssim q(pq)^{1/4}\kern 5.0pt.

(22)

Conversely, by triangular inequality, any estimator (
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}
) satisfies

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{q}|\tilde{\sigma}_{i}-\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})|\right]\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[|\sum_{i=1}^{q}\widehat{\sigma}_{i}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}|\right]\ ,

which, in light of Corollary
4.10
is higher than
cq(pq)^{1/4}/\log(q)
, at least for some matrices
\mathbf{A}
with
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\in[0,0.125(pq)^{1/4}]
. Hence, one can improve the rate in (
22
) at best by a factor
1/\log(q)
. We explain how to do this in the remainder of this section by extending the moment matching method of Kong and Valiant
[
21
]
to our setting. As in the previous section, we shall assume henceforth that the singular values of
\mathbf{A}
are bounded by
M(pq)^{1/4}
for some known
M>1
.

Write
\delta_{x}
for the Dirac measure at
x
.
Given a vector
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{q})
, let
\mu_{\sigma}=q^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\delta_{\sigma_{i}}
where
\delta_{x}
denote the associated probability measure. For two measures
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}
with cumulative distribution functions
F_{\mu_{1}}
and
F_{\mu_{2}}
, the Wasserstein distance
W(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
is defined as

W(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|F_{\mu_{1}}(t)-F_{\mu_{2}}(t)|dt\ .

It was previously observed (e.g. in
[
21
]
) that the
l_{1}
distance between two ordered vectors is proportional to the Wasserstein distance between the two corresponding measure.

\sum_{i=1}^{q}|\sigma_{i}-\sigma^{\prime}_{i}|=qW(\mu_{\sigma};\mu_{\sigma^{\prime}})\ .

(23)

Indeed,
qW(\mu_{\sigma};\mu_{\sigma^{\prime}})=q\int|F_{\mu_{\sigma}}(t)-F_{\mu_{\sigma^{\prime}}}(t)|dt=\int\big|\sum_{i=1}^{q}({\mathbf{1}}_{\sigma_{i}\leq t}-{\mathbf{1}}_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\leq t})\big|dt
. For a fixed
t
, all
q
differences
({\mathbf{1}}_{\sigma_{i}\leq t}-{\mathbf{1}}_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\leq t})
are either
0
or share the same sign. As a consequence
qW(\mu_{\sigma};\mu_{\sigma^{\prime}})=\int\sum_{i=1}^{q}\big|{\mathbf{1}}_{\sigma_{i}\leq t}-{\mathbf{1}}_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\leq t}\big|dt=\sum_{i=1}^{q}|\sigma_{i}-\sigma^{\prime}_{i}|
and our problem is equivalent to estimating the measure
\mu_{\sigma(\mathbf{A})}
in Wasserstein distance.

In
[
21
]
, Kong and Valiant bound the Wasserstein distance between two compactly distributed probability measures in terms of their sequence of
k
first moments (see Lemma
8.16
below). This led them to first estimate the
k
-first moment of a measure and then pick a measure whose moments are closest to the estimated moments. See Algorithm 1 in
[
21
]
. In our setting, we are only able to estimate efficiently the even moments. Still, we can adapt their approach by nearly matching the first even moments instead of all the first moments.

Let
K
be a tuning parameter. Denote the size
K
vector
\widehat{m}
by
\widehat{m}_{k}=q^{-1}[M(pq)^{1/4}]^{-2k}U_{k}
where
U_{k}
defined in (
9
) is our estimator of the
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}
. Fix
\zeta=1/(M^{2}q)
and consider a fine regular grid
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})
on
[0,b(pq)^{1/4}]
with
x_{i}=(i-1)\zeta
and
d=\lceil M(pq)^{1/4}/\zeta\rceil
.
Define also the
K\times d
matrix
\mathbf{V}
such that
\mathbf{V}_{ij}=x_{j}^{2i}
. We consider the following two step estimator of the singular values vector.

1.

Let
p^{+}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the solution to the following linear program, which we will regard
as a distribution consisting of point masses at values
x

\mathrm{minimize}_{p}|\mathbf{V}p-\widehat{U}|_{1}\text{ subject to }1^{T}p=1\text{ and }p>0

(24)

2.

Return the singular values
\widehat{\sigma}_{i}
by taking the rescaled
i
-th
(q+1)
st quantile of the distribution corresponding to
p^{+}
, that is
\widehat{\sigma}_{i}=M(qp)^{1/4}\min\{x_{j}:\sum_{l\leq j}p_{l}^{+}\geq\frac{i}{q+1}\}
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Fix any
M>1
and choose
K=\lfloor c\log(q)\rfloor
for a suitable numerical constant
M>1
. If
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\leq M(pq)^{1/4}
, then the estimator
\widehat{\sigma}_{i}
defined above satisfies

W\left(\mu_{\widehat{\sigma}},\mu_{\sigma(\mathbf{A})}\right)\lesssim\frac{M}{\log(q)}(pq)^{1/4}\ .

(25)

In particular, this implies that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{q}|\widehat{\sigma}_{i}-\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})|\right]\lesssim\frac{M}{\log(q)}q(pq)^{1/4}\ .

As a simple corollary, we observe that the plug-in estimator
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\widehat{\sigma}_{i}
estimates the nuclear norm
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}
at a rate similar to that of (
17
).
More generally, the dual representation of Wasserstein distance entails that for any Lipschitz function with Lipschitz constant
L
on
[0,M(pq)^{1/4}]
, we have

\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{q}f(\widehat{\sigma}_{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{q}f(\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A}))\Big|=q\big|\inf f(d\mu_{\widehat{\sigma}}-d\mu_{\sigma(\mathbf{A})})\big|\lesssim LM\frac{q}{\log(q)}(pq)^{1/4}\ .

Hence, the plug-in estimator achieves the rate
Mq(pq)^{1/4}/\log(q)
uniformly over all Lipschitz functional of the singular values.
See
[
16
]
for related discussions in discrete settings. Regarding the nuclear norm, the slight downside of this plug-in approach is that it requires to solve the linear program (
24
), while Bernstein polynomials are more explicit.

6
Extension to general noise distributions

Until now, we assumed that the noise components
(\mathbf{E}_{ij})
follow a standard normal distribution. In this section, we extend our results to a non-Gaussian model
\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{E}
where the entries
(\mathbf{E}_{i,j})
of
\mathbf{E}
are independent, and identically distributed as a sub-Gaussian random variable
E
satisfying
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E]=0
and
\operatorname{Var}{(E)}=1
. In the sequel, we write
\|E\|_{\psi_{2}}
for its sub-Gaussian norm – see e.g.
[
39
]
for a definition. As in the first part of the manuscript, we start with the Frobenius and operator norms before turning to general even norms and to non-even norms. The estimator
U_{1}=tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})]-qp
of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{F}^{2}
defined in (
6
) is a second degree polynomial and we easily work out its variance.

\mathbb{E}\left[|U_{1}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}|^{2}\right]=\operatorname{Var}\left(E^{2}\right)pq+4\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2}_{2}+4\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E^{3}]\sum_{i,j}\mathbf{A}_{ij}\leq 3\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E^{4}][pq+2\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}]\ ,

where we used Young’s and Cauchy Schwarz inequality. Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
2.1
, we arrive at

\mathbb{E}\left[|(U_{1})_{+}^{1/2}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}|\right]\leq 3\sqrt{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E^{4}]}(pq)^{1/4}\ .

The proof is omitted. Similarly, we can extend the analysis of the estimator
U_{\infty}=(\sigma_{1}^{2}(\mathbf{Y})-p)_{+}^{1/2}
of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}
or the estimator
T_{s}=[\sum_{i=1}^{q}[(\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{Y})-p)_{+}]^{s/2}]^{1/s}
of the
s
-th Schatten norm
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}
to obtain similar bounds to the Gaussian case.

Proposition 6.1
.

For any matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[|\left(\sigma_{1}^{2}(\mathbf{Y})-p\right)_{+}^{1/2}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}|\right]\leq c_{\|E\|_{\psi_{2}}}\left(pq\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\ ,

(26)

for a positive numerical quantity
c_{\|E\|_{\psi_{2}}}
that only depends on
\|E\|_{\psi_{2}}
.

Proposition 6.2
.

For any matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
and any
s\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|T_{s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}|]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c^{s}_{\|E\|_{\psi_{2}}}q^{1/s}[(p\vee s)(q\vee s)]^{1/4}\ ,

(27)

for some positive quantity
c_{\|E\|_{\psi_{2}}}
that only depends on
\|E\|_{\psi_{2}}
.

In contrast, it is much less clear to what extent the
2k
-Schatten norms
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}
can be estimated at the rate
(pq)^{1/4}
for general sub-Gaussian noise distribution. Indeed, the definition our estimator
U_{k}
introduced in Section
3
delicately depends on the sequence of moments of the standard normal distribution through the use of Hermite polynomials. Since
only the two first moments of
E
match those of the standard normal distribution, the estimator
U_{k}
is therefore biased for all
k>1
. Still, we are able to control its risk in the next theorem. The arguments significantly differ from the Gaussian case. Indeed, we could assume in the proof of Theorem
3.3
that
\mathbf{A}
is diagonal by distribution invariance under orthogonal transformations. This is not the case here and we need to carefully enumerate all the terms in
U_{k}
using generalized Möbius formula for partitions and graph enumeration arguments.

Theorem 6.3
.

For any positive integer
k\geq 1
and any
p\geq q
, we have

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(U_{k}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}\right)^{2}\right]\leq(c\|E\|_{\psi_{2}}k)^{c^{\prime}k}\left[(pq)^{k}+pq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-4}+q\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-2}\right]\ ,

(28)

where
c
and
c^{\prime}
are numerical constants.

In contrast to Theorem
3.3
for Gaussian noise, the Schatten norms
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{4k}^{4k-4}
and
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{4k}^{4k-2}
in the risk upper bounds have been respectively replaced by the looser bounds
q\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-4}
and
q\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-2}
. Still, this mild modifications does not affect the rates for estimating
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}
as stated in the next corollary.

Corollary 6.4
.

For any positive integer
k
, and any
p\times q
matrix
\mathbf{A}
one has

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[|(U_{k})_{+}^{1/(2k)}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}|\right]\leq(c\|E\|_{\psi_{2}}k)^{c^{\prime}k}(pq)^{1/4}\ ,

(29)

where
c
and
c^{\prime}
are positive numerical constants.

As in the Gaussian case, we achieve a risk bound of the order of
(pq)^{1/4}
for any even Schatten norm. Looking more closely at the risk bound (
28
), we observe that the risk bound is super-exponential with respect to
k
, whereas the dependency of the risk bound in the Gaussian case is only exponential with respect to
k
.

Now we can rely on Theorem
6.3
to analyze the polynomial approximation estimators of general Schatten norms
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}
as in Section
4
, the main difference being that the degree
K^{*}
of the polynomial should be now set to be of the order of
\frac{\log(q)}{\log\log(q)}
to account for the super exponential dependency with respect to
k
in (
28
). This results in additional
\log\log(q)
terms in the rates. For instance, the nuclear norm can now be estimator up to the rate
Mq\frac{\log\log(q)}{\log(q)}(pq)^{1/4}
instead of the rate
M\frac{q}{\log(q)}(pq)^{1/4}
. In summary, up to these
\log\log(q)
possible loss, all the results extend to a subGaussian noise distribution.

7
Discussion

7.1
Application to Effective rank estimation

Throughout this subsection, we assume that
q\geq 2
, otherwise the problem of effective rank estimation simply corresponds to that of testing the nullity of a vector.
Coming back to the problem of estimating the Effective rank of a noisy matrix, we deduce from the previous sections that one should favor ratio of even Schatten norms as these are much easier to estimate. Let us further focus on the specific choice

\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})=\frac{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2}_{\infty}}\ .

(30)

In light of our analysis in Section
2
of Frobenius and operator norms, we consider the estimator

\widehat{\mathrm{ER}}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})=\max\left[\frac{\tr[\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}]}{\|\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|_{\infty}},1\right]\ .

(31)

We cannot simply plug Propositions
2.1
and
2.5
to control this estimator as we only proved expectation bounds. The following result pushes the analysis slightly further to derive high probability deviation for the effective rank.

Proposition 7.1
.

There exist numerical constants
c
–
c_{3}
such that the following holds for any matrix
\mathbf{A}
and for any
t>0
. Provided that

\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}\geq c(\sqrt{pqt}+t)\ ,\text{ and }\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\geq c\left[\sqrt{t}+(pq)^{1/4}+(pt)^{1/4}\right]\ ,

(32)

then, with probability higher than
1-c_{3}e^{-t}
, it holds that

\frac{\big|\widehat{\mathrm{ER}}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})-\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})\big|}{\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})}\leq c^{\prime}\left[\frac{\sqrt{pqt}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{pt}+\sqrt{pq}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2}_{\infty}}+\frac{\sqrt{q}+\sqrt{t}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}}\right]\ .

(33)

If
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})=\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}
is large compared to
(pq)^{1/4}
, the above proposition implies that the effective rank is well estimated.
Condition (
32
) is unavoidable for consistent effective risk estimation. Indeed, we established in the proof of Proposition
2.2
that it is impossible to distinguish
\mathbf{A}
from the null matrix when
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})
is less than
(pq)^{1/4}
. It turns out that the plug-in estimator (
31
) is optimal as stated by the next proposition.

Proposition 7.2
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
. If
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\geq c_{\alpha}(pq)^{1/4}
, then, with probability higher than
1-\alpha
, we have

\frac{\big|\widehat{\mathrm{ER}}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})-\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})\big|}{\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})}\leq c^{\prime}_{\alpha}\left[\frac{\sqrt{pq}}{\sigma_{1}^{2}(\mathbf{A})}+\frac{\sqrt{q}}{\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})}\right]\ .

(34)

Conversely, for any
r\geq(pq)^{1/4}
, we have the following lower bound on the error

\inf_{\tilde{R}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\,\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\geq r}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{|\widehat{R}-\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})\big|}{\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})}\geq c\left(\frac{\sqrt{pq}}{r^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{q}}{r}\right)\right]\geq 0.2\ .

(35)

The optimal convergence rate of the effective rank is of the order of
\frac{\sqrt{pq}}{\sigma^{2}_{1}(\mathbf{A})}
when
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})
is in
[(pq)^{1/4};\sqrt{p}]
and of the order of
\frac{\sqrt{q}}{\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})}
for
r\geq\sqrt{p}
.

7.2
Low-Rank matrices

The optimal risk of estimating even Schatten norms does not depend on the rank of
\mathbf{A}
. In other words, estimating
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}
for a rank-one matrix is as difficult as for a full rank matrix. The situation seems quite different for general Schatten norms as we have obtained the much slower convergence rates
q^{1/s}(pq)^{1/4}
for
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}
when
s
is not even. In the extreme case of rank-one matrix, this rates can be drastically improved to
(pq)^{1/4}
as
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}=\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}
so that we can simply use
(U_{1})_{+}^{1/2}
. More generally, we could hope to derive faster rates for
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}
when
\|\mathbf{A}\|
is a small rank (or approximately a small rank matrix). This is an interesting direction for future research.

7.3
Estimating general
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}
without any constraint on
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}

In Section
4
, we have introduced a simple modified plug-in estimator
T_{1}
for
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}
up to an an error
q(pq)^{1/4}
. Then, assuming that
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq M(pq)^{1/4}
, for some
M>0
, we have analyzed a moment-based estimator that achieves the rate
q(pq)^{1/4}/\log(q)
. This rate turns out to be minimax optimal, at least for bounded
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}
. For other functional estimation problems in the Gaussian vector model
[
7
]
or in discrete distribution
[
17
,
16
]
, the authors introduce an unified estimator by considering separately observations corresponding to parameters in the smooth part of the functional and observations corresponding to the non-smooth part of the functional. In our setting, this could amount to estimating separately the large singular values
\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})
and then use a polynomial approximation method to estimate the part
\sum_{i=s+1}^{q}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})
of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}
arising from the bulk of the singular values. Unfortunately, this cannot be easily done in our matrix setting because of the intertwined dependencies between the singular values of
{\bf Y}
.

8
Proofs

8.1
Frobenius and operator norm

Proof of Proposition
2.1
.

The difference
U_{1}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}
decomposes as

U_{1}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\mathbf{\mathbf{Y}}\|_{2}^{2}-pq-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}=\left(\|\mathbf{E}\|_{2}^{2}-pq\right)+2\langle\mathbf{A},\mathbf{E}\rangle,

where
\langle\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}\rangle=\tr(\mathbf{B}^{T}\mathbf{C})
. First,
\|\mathbf{E}\|_{2}^{2}
follows a
\chi^{2}
distribution with
pq
degrees of freedom. Hence, we derive from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that
\mathbb{E}\left[|\|\mathbf{E}\|_{2}^{2}-pq|\right]\leq\mathrm{var}^{1/2}(\|\mathbf{E}\|_{2}^{2})=\sqrt{2pq}
. Since
\mathbf{A}
is deterministic,
\langle\mathbf{A},\mathbf{E}\rangle/\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}
follows a standard normal distribution and
\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\mathbf{A},\mathbf{E}\rangle|\right]\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}
. The first result follows.
Since
|\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b}|\leq\sqrt{|a-b|}
, we derive from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the first result that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|(U_{1})_{+}^{1/2}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left[\mathbb{E}\left(|(U_{1})_{+}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}|\right)\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left[\mathbb{E}\left(|U_{1}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}|\right)\right]^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{(2pq)^{1/2}+2\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}}\ \ ,

which is smaller than
2(pq)^{1/4}
as long as
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{pq}
. Now assume that
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}\geq\sqrt{pq}
. Since
(x-y)=(x^{2}-y^{2})/(x+y)
, it follows again from the first result that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|(U_{1})_{+}^{1/2}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}|\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{|U_{1}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}|}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}}\right]\leq\frac{\sqrt{2pq}+2\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}}\leq 2+\sqrt{2}\leq 3(pq)^{1/4}\ .

This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.4
.

By triangular inequality, we have
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}|]\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\|\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}]
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\|\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}]-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}|]\leq 2\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\|\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}]
. By Lemma
A.1
, the expectation of the operator norm of
\mathbf{E}
is less or equal to
\sqrt{p}+\sqrt{q}
. This concludes the first part of the proof.
Let us now lower bound the risk of
\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}
and
\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}\right]
.
Choose
\mathbf{A}
to be the null matrix. The risk of
\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}
is then
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\|\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}]
. Consider the size
q
vector
e_{1}=(1,0,\ldots,0)
. Then,

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}|]\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|\mathbf{E}e_{1}|_{2}]\ .

Since
|\mathbf{E}e_{1}|_{2}
is the norm of a standard Gaussian vector of dimension
p
, the expectation
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|\mathbf{E}e_{1}|_{2}]
is that of the norm of a standard Gaussian vector. Relying on deviation inequalities for
\chi^{2}
random variables, we deduce that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|\mathbf{E}e_{1}|_{2}]\geq\sqrt{\frac{p}{3}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}[|\mathbf{E}e_{1}|^{2}_{2}\geq p/3]\geq c\sqrt{p}(1-e^{-p/32})\geq c^{\prime}\sqrt{p}\ ,

for a constant
c>0
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.5
.

It follows from the triangular inequality that

\|\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-p-\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}+\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}-p\ .

Conversely, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{u,|u|_{2}=1}\left(u^{T}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}u+2u^{T}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}u+u^{T}\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}u\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sup_{u,|u|_{2}=1}\left(u^{T}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}u\right)-2\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}+\sigma_{q}(\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}-2\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}+\sigma_{q}(\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E})\ .

This allows us to bound the error

\displaystyle\big|\|\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-p-\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}+[\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E})-p]\vee[\sigma_{q}(\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E})-p]

(36)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}+\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}\|_{\infty}\ .

Then, the first risk bound is a straightforward consequence of the two following lemmas whose proof is given below.

Lemma 8.1
.

For all matrices
\mathbf{A}
, we have
\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}\right]\leq 2\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\sqrt{q}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}\|_{\infty}\right]\leq 3\sqrt{pq}+8e^{-p/4}+\sqrt{64\pi p}.

As in the proof of Proposition
2.1
, we deal differently with small and large values of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}
. Since
\sqrt{x}-\sqrt{y}\leq\sqrt{|x-y|}
, we obtain by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|(\|\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-p)_{+}^{1/2}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}|\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\sqrt{(\|\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-p)_{+}}-\sqrt{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{2}}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{|(\|\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-p)_{+}-\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}|}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{E}\left[|\|\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-p-\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}|\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(3\sqrt{pq}+4\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\sqrt{q}+c^{\prime}(\sqrt{p}+1)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

If we assume that
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq(pq)^{1/4}
, this leads us to

\mathbb{E}\left[|(\|\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-p)_{+}^{1/2}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}|\right]\leq c(pq)^{1/4}\ .

Now assume that
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\geq(pq)^{1/4}
, which is equivalent to
\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\geq\sqrt{pq}
. Since
|x-y|\leq|x^{2}-y^{2}|/|y|
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|(\|\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-p)_{+}^{1/2}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}}\mathbb{E}\left[|(\|\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}-p)_{+}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{2}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3(pq)^{1/4}+4\sqrt{q}+c^{\prime}(p/q)^{1/4}\lesssim(pq)^{1/4}\ ,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of lemma
8.1
.

Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{U}^{T}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}
denote a singular value decomposition of
\mathbf{A}
. Since the distribution of
\mathbf{E}
is invariant by left and right orthogonal transformation, it follows that
\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}
follows the same distribution as
\|\mathbf{V}\mathbf{D}^{T}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}=\|\mathbf{D}^{T}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}
. We shall bound the expectation of this last random variable. Since
\mathbf{D}^{T}
is a
q\times p
diagonal matrix,
\mathbf{D}^{T}\mathbf{E}
does not depend on the entries
\mathbf{E}_{ij}
with
i\geq q
. Write
\overline{\mathbf{D}}
and
\overline{\mathbf{E}}
for the restriction of
\mathbf{D}
and
\mathbf{E}
to their
q
first rows. We obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\overline{\mathbf{D}}\overline{\mathbf{E}}\|_{\infty}\right]\leq\|\overline{\mathbf{D}}\|_{\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\overline{\mathbf{E}}\|_{\infty}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\sqrt{q}\ ,

by Lemma
A.1
and since
\|\overline{\mathbf{D}}\|_{\infty}=\|\mathbf{D}\|_{\infty}=\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}
.

∎

Proof of lemma
8.2
.

Observe that
\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}\|_{\infty}=\max(\sigma^{2}_{1}(\mathbf{E})-p,p-\sigma_{q}^{2}(\mathbf{E}))
. We deduce from Lemma
A.2
(taken from
[
11
]
) that, for any
t>0
, with probability higher than
1-2e^{-t}
we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{E})\leq\sqrt{p}+\sqrt{q}+\sqrt{2t}\ ;\sigma_{q}(\mathbf{E})\leq\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{q}-\sqrt{2t}\ .

Hence, with probability higher than
1-2e^{-t}
, we have

\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}\|_{\infty}\leq q+2\sqrt{pq}+2t+4\sqrt{2pt}
, which implies that

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}\|_{\infty}-q-2\sqrt{pq}\geq x\right]\leq 2\exp\left[-\frac{1}{4}\left(x\wedge\frac{x^{2}}{16p}\right)\right]\ .

for any
x>0
. Integrating this inequality we conclude that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}\|_{\infty}\right]\leq 3\sqrt{pq}+\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}}e^{-x^{2}/(64p)}+2\int_{16p}^{\infty}e^{-x/4}=3\sqrt{pq}+8e^{-4p}+\sqrt{64\pi p}\ .

∎

8.2
Minimax lower bound

Proof of Proposition
2.2
.

We follow the general Le Cam’s approach of fuzzy hypotheses as may be found in
[
37
, Sec.2.7.4]
. Given a matrix
\mathbf{A}
, we denote in the section of
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{A}}
for the distribution of
\mathbf{Y}
. Consider a prior distribution
\mu
on
\mathbf{A}
, such that, for some
s>0
,
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}\geq s
,
\mu
almost surely. Define the set
\Theta
as the union of the support of
\mu
and the null matrix
0
. Denoting
\mathbf{P}=\int_{\mathbf{A}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{A}}\mu(d\mathbf{A})
for the integrated distribution, it follows from Theorem 2.15 in
[
37
]
that

\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{A}}[|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}|\geq s/2]\geq\frac{1-\sqrt{\chi^{2}(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{0},\mathbf{P})/2}}{2}\ .

If we further assume that
\Theta
only contains matrices of
\rank
less or equal to one, this implies that

\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A},\ \rank(\mathbf{A})\leq 1}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{A}}[|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}|]\geq\frac{s}{4}\left[1-\sqrt{\chi^{2}(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{0},\mathbf{P})/2}\right]\ .

(37)

It remains to choose
\mu
and bound the corresponding
\chi^{2}
distance.

Let
s>0
be a positive quantity that will be fixed later. Let
\nu_{1}
be the uniform distribution over the vectors of the form
p^{-1/2}(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{p})
where the
\eta_{i}
’s belong to
\{-1,1\}
. Let
\nu_{2}
be corresponding distribution where
q
is replaced by
p
. Finally, let
\mu
be the distribution of
\mathbf{A}=suv^{T}
where
u
and
v
are sampled independently from
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
. By construction,
\mu
almost surely,
\mathbf{A}
is a rank-one matrix and
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}=s
.

The main part of the proof amounts to upper bounding the
\chi^{2}
discrepancy between
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
and
\mathbf{P}
. Writing
L=d\mathbf{P}/\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
the likelihood ratio between
\mathbf{P}
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
, we have by definition
[
37
]
that

\chi^{2}(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{0},\mathbf{P})=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[(L-1)^{2}]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[L^{2}]-1\ .

(38)

Lemma 8.3
.

Taking
s=(pq/4)^{1/4}
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{0}\left[L^{2}\right]\leq\frac{5}{3}
.

Equipped with this choice of
s
, we have
\chi^{2}(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{0},\mathbf{P})\leq 2/3
and we conclude thanks to (
37
) that

\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A},\ \rank(\mathbf{A})\leq 1}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{A}}[|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}|]\geq\frac{(pq/4)^{1/4}}{4}(1-\sqrt{1/3})\geq\frac{(pq)^{1/4}}{20}\ .

∎

Proof of Lemma
8.3
.

In this proof, we write,
\langle,\rangle
for the inner product associated to the the Frobenius norm.
First, we work out the likelihood ratio
L
. Since the density of
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{A}}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure is
(2\pi)^{-(pq)/2}e^{-\|\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}/2}
, it follows that

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{\mathbf{Y}}-\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\mathbf{\mathbf{Y}}\|_{2}^{2}\right]\mu(d\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2}_{2}+\langle\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{A}\rangle\right]\mu(d\mathbf{A})\ .

As a consequence, the second moment of the likelihood writes as

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[L^{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[\int\int\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{A}_{1}\|^{2}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{A}_{2}\|^{2}_{2}+\langle\mathbf{Y},(\mathbf{A}_{1}+\mathbf{A}_{2})\rangle\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{A}_{1}\|^{2}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{A}_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+\langle\mathbf{Y},(\mathbf{A}_{1}+\mathbf{A}_{2})\rangle\right]\right]\mu(d\mathbf{A}_{1})\mu(d\mathbf{A}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{A}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{A}_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\mathbf{A}_{1}+\mathbf{A}_{2}\|^{2}/2\right]\mu(d\mathbf{A}_{1})\mu(d\mathbf{A}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int\exp\left[\langle\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathbf{A}_{2}\rangle\right]\mu(d\mathbf{A}_{1})\mu(d\mathbf{A}_{2})\ ,

where we used the Laplace transform of the normal random distribution in the third line. In view of the definition of
\mu
, this integral further decomposes as

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[L^{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int\exp\left[s^{2}\tr[v_{1}u_{1}^{T}u_{2}v_{2}^{T}\right]\nu_{1}(du_{1})\nu_{1}(du_{2})\nu_{2}(dv_{1})\nu_{1}(dv_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int\exp\left[s^{2}(v_{1}^{T}v_{2})(u_{1}^{T}u_{2})\right]\nu_{1}(du_{1})\nu_{1}(du_{2})\nu_{2}(dv_{1})\nu_{1}(dv_{2})\ .

Since
Z_{1}=pu_{1}^{T}u_{2}
and
Z_{2}=qv_{1}^{T}v_{2}
are respectively distributed as sums of
p
and
q
independent Rademacher random variables, arrive at

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[L^{2}]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{s^{2}Z_{1}Z_{2}}{pq}\right)\right]

For
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
X
a Rademacher random variable, we have
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[e^{aX}]=\cosh(ax)\leq e^{a^{2}x^{2}/2}
(compare the power series). We obtain by integration with respect to
Z_{1}
,

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[L^{2}]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cosh\left(\frac{s^{2}Z_{2}}{pq}\right)^{p}\right]\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{s^{4}Z_{2}^{2}}{2pq^{2}}\right)\right]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{1}{8q}Z_{2}^{2}\right)\right]\ ,

since we fixed
s=(pq/4)^{1/4}
. Since
Z_{2}
is a sum of Rademacher random variables, we can apply Hoeffding’s inequality
[
6
]
, which leads us to
\mathbb{P}\left(|Z_{2}|\geq u\right)\leq 2e^{\frac{-u^{2}}{2q}}
. As a consequence,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[L^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{1}{8q}Z_{2}^{2}\right)\right]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\exp\left(\frac{Z_{2}^{2}}{8q}\right)\geq t\right]dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 1+\int_{1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\exp\left(\frac{Z_{2}^{2}}{8q}\right)\geq t\right]dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 1+2\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{4}}dt=5/3\ .

The result follows.
∎

8.3
Proof for general even norms
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}

We start with a lemma summarizing important properties of the Hermite polynomials (
8
). It is a slight variation of Lemma 3 in
[
7
]
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(x,1)
. Then,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H^{2}_{r}(Z)]\leq[e(x^{2}\vee r)]^{r}
and
\operatorname{Var}\left(H_{r}(Z)\right)\leq er[e(x^{2}\vee r)]^{r-1}
. Besides,

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H^{2}_{r}(Z)]=r!\ ,\text{ and }\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{r}(Z)H_{l}(Z)]=0\ ,\text{ for }r\neq l\ .

(39)

Proof of Lemma
8.4
.

The identity (
39
) is classical. The bound
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H^{2}_{r}(Z)]\leq[e(x^{2}\vee r)]^{r}
is taken from Lemma 3 in
[
7
]
. Since
\operatorname{Var}\left(H_{r}(Z)\right)\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H^{2}_{r}(Z)]
, we only have to prove that,
for
x^{2}>r
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{r}^{2}(Z)]\leq x^{2r}+r(ex^{2})^{r-1}
. We know from the proof of Lemma 3 in
[
7
]
that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{r}^{2}(Z)]-x^{2k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r!\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\binom{r}{j}\frac{x^{2j}}{j!}\leq r!\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\binom{r}{j}\frac{x^{2(r-1)}}{(r-1)!}\leq rx^{2r-2}2^{r}\ .

The result follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

Let us upper bound
\operatorname{Var}({U_{k}})=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\left(U_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sigma_{i}^{2k}(\mathbf{A})\right)^{2}]
. Recall that we assume without loss of generality that
\mathbf{A}
is of singular value form, that is

\mathbf{A}_{rs}=\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{A}){\mathbf{1}}_{r=s}
. Given two sequences
i=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\in[p]^{k}
and
j=(j_{1},\ldots j_{k})\in[q]^{k}
we write
W_{ij}=\prod_{r,s}H_{N_{rs}(i,j)}(\mathbf{Y}_{rs})
.
Relying on the Hermite decomposition of
U_{k}
, we observe that

\operatorname{Var}\left(U_{k}\right)=\sum_{i,j}\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\underset{=T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}}{\underbrace{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left[W_{ij}-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left(W_{ij}\right)\right]\left[W_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left(W_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\right)\right]\right]}}\ .

(40)
