In the remainder of this proof, we use combinatorial arguments to count the number of such non-zero
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
and to bound each of these non-zero terms.
The expectation
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left(W_{ij}\right)
is non-zero if and only if
N_{rs}(i,j)=0
for all
r\neq s
. This is possible only if
i_{1}=i_{2}=\ldots=i_{k}=j_{1}=\ldots=j_{k}
. As a consequence, only terms of the form
H_{2k}(Y_{rr})
have a non-zero expectation. To control
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
, we consider two cases depending on the nullity of
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[W_{ij}]
.

Case 1
:
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[W_{ij}]\neq 0
. It follows from the above discussion that, for some
r\in[q]
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[W_{ij}]=\mathbb{E}[H_{2k}(Y_{rr})]=\sigma_{r}^{2k}(\mathbf{A})
. Then,
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
is non-zero only if
r
occurs in both sequences
i^{\prime}
and
j^{\prime}
. If
i^{\prime}\neq i
or if
j^{\prime}\neq j
, this implies that
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
is not constant so that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[W_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}]=0
. Besides, if
i^{\prime}\neq i
or if
j^{\prime}\neq j
, there exist
s_{1}\neq s_{2}
such that
N_{s_{1}s_{2}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})>0
. Since
\mathbf{A}_{s_{1}s_{2}}=0
and
N_{s_{1}s_{2}}(i,j)=0
this implies that
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}=0
by independence of the noise components. If
(i^{\prime}=i)
and
(j^{\prime}=j)
, then Lemma
8.4
ensures that

T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}=\operatorname{Var}{H_{2k}(\mathbf{Y}_{rr})}\leq(2ke)[e(\sigma^{2}_{r}(\mathbf{A})\vee 2k)]^{2k-1}\ .

(41)

In summary, if
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[W_{ij}]\neq 0
or
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[W_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}]\neq 0
, we have
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}=0
unless
(i=i^{\prime})
and
(j^{\prime}=j)
. In the latter case,
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
satisfies (
41
).

Case 2
:
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left(W_{ij}\right)=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left(W_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\right)=0
. Then,
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
simplifies as

T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[W_{ij}W_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}]=\prod_{r=1}^{p}\prod_{s=1}^{q}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{N_{rr}(i,j)}(\mathbf{Y}_{rs})H_{N_{rr}(i,j)}(\mathbf{Y}_{rs})]

Since
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{Y}_{rs}]=\mathbf{A}_{rs}=0
for any
r\neq s
, it follows from Lemma
8.4
that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{l}(\mathbf{Y}_{rs})H_{l^{\prime}}(\mathbf{Y}_{rs})]=l!{\mathbf{1}}_{l=l^{\prime}}
for
r\neq s
. We deduce that
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\neq 0
only if
N_{rs}(ij)=N_{rs}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})
for all
r\neq s
. By definition of
N_{rs}
, we have
\sum_{r=1}^{p}\sum_{s=1}^{q}N_{rs}(ij)=\sum_{r=1}^{p}\sum_{s=1}^{q}N_{rs}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})=2k
. Together with the previous property, we deduce
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\neq 0
only if
\sum_{r}N_{rr}(ij)=\sum_{r}N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})
.
As explained in the proof of Proposition
3.1
below, we also have
\sum_{s=1}^{q}N_{rs}(ij)\equiv 0[2]
for each
r\in[p]
, because each entry in the sequence
i
appears twice in the monomial
W_{ij}
. Hence,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}]\neq 0
implies that, for each
r\in[q]

N_{rr}(ij)+N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})=\underset{\equiv 0[2]}{\underbrace{\sum_{s=1}^{q}N_{rs}(ij)+N_{rs}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})}}-\sum_{s=1,\ s\neq r}^{q}\underset{\equiv 0[2]}{\underbrace{N_{rs}(ij)+N_{rs}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})}}\kern 5.0pt,

which implies that
N_{rr}(ij)+N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})\equiv 0[2]
.
Furthermore, if for some
r\in[q]
,
N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})>0
, then there exists either
s\neq r\in[q]
such that
N_{rs}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})>0
or
s\neq r\in[p]
such that
N_{rs}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})>0
–otherwise
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[W_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}]\neq 0
. Hence, we have
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\neq 0
unless either
N_{rs}(ij)>0
for some
s\in[q]
or
N_{sr}(ij)>0
for some
s\in[p]
. Let us summarize our findings. When
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left(W_{ij}\right)=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left(W_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\right)=0
, we have
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\neq 0
only if the four following properties are satisfied

(a)

N_{rs}(ij)=N_{rs}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})
for all
(r,s)\in[p]\times[q]
such that
r\neq s
.

(b)

\sum_{r=1}^{q}N_{rr}(ij)=\sum_{r=1}^{q}N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})
.

(c)

N_{rr}(ij)+N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})\equiv 0[2]
for all
r\in[q]
.

(d)

For any
l=1,\ldots,k
, there exists
l^{\prime}
such that either
i_{l}=i^{\prime}_{l^{\prime}}
or
i_{l}=j^{\prime}_{l^{\prime}}
. Similarly, for any
l=1,\ldots,k
, there exists
l^{\prime}
such that either
j_{l}=i^{\prime}_{l^{\prime}}
or
j_{l}=j^{\prime}_{l^{\prime}}
.

The last property does not ensure that
i
and
i^{\prime}
take same value, but if
i
takes some value that does not appear in
i^{\prime}
, then this value must appear in
j^{\prime}
. When
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\neq 0
, then Lemma
8.4
yields

\displaystyle T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{rs}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_{N_{rs}(ij)}(\mathbf{Y}_{rs})H_{N_{rs}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})}(\mathbf{Y}_{rs})\right]

(42)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\prod_{r\neq s}[N_{rs}(ij)]!\prod_{r=1}^{q}\left[e^{1/2}\left(\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{A})\vee\sqrt{N_{rr}(ij)\vee N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})}\right)\right]^{N_{rr}(ij)+N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\prod_{r\neq s}[N_{rs}(ij)]!\prod_{r=1}^{q}\left[e^{1/2}\left(\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{A})\vee\sqrt{2k}\right)\right]^{N_{rr}(ij)+N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})}\ ,

by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

Based on the above observations, we shall now bound
\operatorname{Var}\left(U_{k}\right)
by suitably partitioning the sequences
(i,j)
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
. Let us introduce some notation.
Given a
k
-tuple
i
, we introduce the
2k
-tuples
i_{ev}=(i_{1},i_{1},i_{2},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k},i_{k})
and
i_{od}=(i_{1},i_{2},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k},i_{k},i_{1})
. Given two
k
-tuples
i
and
j
, we write
i\oplus j\in(\mathbb{N}^{2})^{2k}
by

i\oplus j=((i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{1},j_{2}),(i_{2},j_{2}),\ldots,(i_{k},j_{k}),(i_{k},j_{1}))\kern 5.0pt.

(43)

Finally, we define
S^{(id)}[i,j]=\cup_{r=1}^{q}\{(i\oplus j)^{-1}[\{(r,r)\}]\}
as the collection of indices
t
such that
(i\oplus j)_{t}=(r,r)
for some
r\in[q]
.
For any two subsets
S
and
S^{\prime}
of
[2k]
, we consider
R_{S,S^{\prime}}
as the sum of
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
over all sequences
(i,j)
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
satisfying
S^{(id)}[i,j]=S
and
S^{(id)}[i^{\prime},j^{\prime}]=S^{\prime}
. Since, for such sequences
(i,j)
, we have
\sum_{r=1}^{q}N_{rr}(ij)=|S|
and
\sum_{r=1}^{q}N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})=|S^{\prime}|
, it follows from Property (b) above that

R_{S,S^{\prime}}=0
if
|S|\neq|S^{\prime}|
. This leads us to the following decomposition

\operatorname{Var}\left(U_{k}\right)=\sum_{s=0}^{k}\quad\sum_{S,S^{\prime}\text{ s.t.}|S|=|S^{\prime}|=s}R_{S,S^{\prime}}

Observe that no sequence
(i,j)
satisfies
|S^{(id)}[i,j]|=k-1
. Besides, in view of Case 1 above and (
41
), we have

R_{[k],[k]}\leq 2ke\sum_{u=1}^{q}[e(\sigma^{2}_{r}(\mathbf{A})\vee 2k)]^{2k-1}\ .

Denoting
\mathbf{A}^{+}
the modification of
\mathbf{A}
where each singular value has been replaced by
\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{A})\vee\sqrt{2k}
, we deduce that

\operatorname{Var}\left(U_{k}\right)\leq\sum_{s=0}^{k-2}\quad\sum_{S,S^{\prime}\text{ s.t.}|S|=|S^{\prime}|=s}R_{S,S^{\prime}}+2ke^{2k}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|_{2k-1}^{2k-1}\ .

(44)

(a)
Sequence
(i,j)

(b)
Sequence
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})

Fig 1:
Examples of sequences two sequences
(i,j)
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
such that
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\neq 0
. Identical values for
i
,
i^{\prime}
,
j
, and
j^{\prime}
are depicted using the same colour.
The first rows stands for
i_{ev}
(resp.
i^{\prime}_{ev}
) and the corresponding partition
\mathcal{P}[i_{ev}]
(resp.
\mathcal{P}[i^{\prime}_{ev}]
). The second rows stands for
j_{od}
(resp.
j^{\prime}_{od}
) and the corresponding partition
\mathcal{P}[j_{od}]
(resp.
\mathcal{P}[j^{\prime}_{od}]
). The last row depicts the sets
S^{(id)}[i,j]
and
S^{(id)}[i^{\prime},j^{\prime}]
.

Consider two sequences
(i,j)
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
such that
S^{(id)}[i,j]=S
,
S^{(id)}[i^{\prime},j^{\prime}]=S^{\prime}
, and
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\neq 0
. Since
N_{rs}(ij)=N_{rs}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
for any
r\neq s
, it follows that the restriction of
(i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})
to
(S^{{}^{\prime}})^{c}
is a permutation of the restriction of
(i\oplus j)
to
S^{c}
. Define
\mathcal{P}[i_{ev}]
(resp.
\mathcal{P}[j_{od}]
) the partition of
[2k]
corresponding to identical values of
i_{ev}
(resp.
j_{od}
). There exist unique maps
F_{1}:([2k]\setminus S^{\prime})\mapsto\mathcal{P}[i_{ev}]
and
F_{2}:([2k]\setminus S^{\prime})\mapsto\mathcal{P}[j_{od}]
such that, for any
s\notin S^{\prime}
,
(i^{\prime}_{ev})_{s}=(i_{ev})_{t}
for all
t
in
F_{1}(s)
and
(j^{\prime}_{od})_{s}=(j_{od})_{t}
for all
t
in
F_{2}(s)
. As a consequence of these definitions, given
(S,S^{\prime},i,j,\mathcal{P}[i_{ev}],\mathcal{P}[j_{od}],F_{1},F_{2})
, the values of
(i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})
outside
S^{\prime}
are uniquely defined. Furthermore, it follows from the definition (
43
) of
(i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})
and of
S^{(id)}[i^{\prime},j^{\prime}]
that
(i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})
is uniquely defined by its values outside
S^{\prime}=S^{(id)}[i^{\prime},j^{\prime}]
, unless
S^{\prime}=[2k]
. Indeed, if
t\in[2k]\in S^{\prime}
is even, then one has

(i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})_{t}=(i^{\prime}_{t/2},i^{\prime}_{t/2})=\big(((i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})_{t-1})_{1},((i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})_{t-1})_{1}\big)\kern 5.0pt.

Besides, if
t\in[2k]\in S^{\prime}
is odd, then one easily checks

(i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})_{t}=(i^{\prime}_{(j^{\prime}+1)/2},j^{\prime}_{(t+1)/2})=\big(((i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})_{t-1})_{2},((i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})_{t-1})_{2}\big)\kern 5.0pt.

with the convention that
t-1=2k
when
t=0
. As a consequence, for
t\in S^{\prime}
,
(i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})_{t}
is uniquely defined by
(i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})_{t-1}
and, unless
(S^{\prime})^{c}=\emptyset
, the vector
(i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})
is uniquely defined by its restriction to
S^{\prime c}
. In summary, given
(S,S^{\prime},i,j,\mathcal{P}[i_{ev}],\mathcal{P}[j_{od}],F_{1},F_{2})

such that
S=S^{(id)}[i,j]
,
\mathcal{P}_{1}=\mathcal{P}[i_{ev}]
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}=\mathcal{P}[i_{od}]
.
There exists at most one
2
-tuple of sequences
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
satisfying
S^{\prime}=S^{(id)}[i^{\prime},j^{\prime}]
, and

\displaystyle(i^{\prime}_{ev})_{s}=(i_{ev})_{t}\text{ for any }s\in S^{\prime c}\text{ and }t\in F_{1}(s)\kern 5.0pt;

(45)

\displaystyle(j^{\prime}_{od})_{s}=(j_{od})_{t}\text{ for any }s\in S^{\prime c}\text{ and }t\in F_{2}(s)\kern 5.0pt.

(46)

Since all terms
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
are non-negative, this allows us to further decompose the expression
R_{S,S^{\prime}}

\displaystyle R_{S,S^{\prime}}\leq\sum_{\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2},F_{1},F_{2}}R_{S,S^{\prime};\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2};F_{1},F_{2}}\ ,\quad\text{ where }\quad R_{S,S^{\prime};\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2};F_{1},F_{2}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,j),\,S^{id}[i,j]=S\\
\mathcal{P}[i_{ev}]=\mathcal{P}_{1}\ ,\,\mathcal{P}[j_{od}]=\mathcal{P}_{2}\end{subarray}}T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\ ,

(47)

where, in the rhs,
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
is the unique (if it exists)
2
-tuple of sequences satisfying
S^{\prime}=S^{(id)}[i^{\prime},j^{\prime}]
, (
45
), and (
46
).

Fix any
S
,
S^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}
,
F_{1}
, and
F_{2}
and let us upper bound the expression
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
for sequences
(i,j,i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
satisfying the constraints in (
47
). Define

\gamma=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}}{\mathbf{1}}_{P\cap S\neq\emptyset}=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}{\mathbf{1}}_{P\cap S\neq\emptyset}\ ,

(48)

as the number of groups of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
(or equivalently
\mathcal{P}_{2}
) that intersect
S
.
For any any sequence
(i,j)
satisfying
S=S^{(id)}[i,j]
,
\mathcal{P}_{1}=\mathcal{P}[i_{ev}]
, and
\mathcal{P}_{2}=\mathcal{P}[j_{od}]
, one easily checks that
\gamma=\sum_{r=1}^{q}{\mathbf{1}}_{N_{rr}(ij)>0}
and therefore also corresponds to the number of distinct values of
(i\oplus j)
on
S^{(id)}[i,j]
. For instance, in Figure
1
, we have
\gamma=2
for this specific value
\mathcal{P}[i_{ev}]
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Consider any
S
,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. For any sequences
(i,j)
such that
\mathcal{P}[i_{ev}]=\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}[j_{od}]=\mathcal{P}_{2}
, we have

\prod_{r\neq s}[N_{rs}(ij)]!\leq(2k)^{2k-|S|-|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-|\mathcal{P}_{2}|+\gamma+1}\ ,

From (
42
) and Lemma
8.5
we deduce that

T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\leq(2k)^{2k-|S|-|P_{1}|-|P_{2}|+\gamma+1}\prod_{r=1}^{q}\left[e^{1/2}\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{A}^{+})\right]^{N_{rr}(ij)+N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})}\kern 5.0pt,

(49)

where we recall that
\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{A}^{+})=\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{A})\vee\sqrt{2k}
. To work out the quantity
R_{S,S^{\prime};\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2};F_{1},F_{2}}
, we need to introduce a few additional notation. Since
\mathcal{P}_{1}\neq\{[2k]\}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}\neq\{[2k]\}
, one easily checks that no element of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
are equal. Indeed, any
P
in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
is an union of sets of the form
\{2t,2t+1\}
whereas any
P\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
in an union of sets of the form
\{2t+1,2t+2\}
. Hence
P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}\cap\mathcal{P}_{2}
implies that
P=[2k]
. As a consequence, one can unambiguously define the collection
\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}
of subsets of
[2k]
. Then, we can extend the maps
F_{1}:[2k]\setminus S^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
F_{2}:[2k]\setminus S^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{2}
as maps
F_{1,ext}:[2k]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
F_{2,ext}:[2k]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}
as follows. Recall that the values of
(i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})
on
S^{\prime}
are uniquely defined by its values outside
S^{\prime}
. Besides, for
t\in S^{\prime}
,
(i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})_{t}
is of the form
(r,r)
where
r
is either equal to
i_{t^{\prime}}
for some
t^{\prime}
or
j_{t^{\prime}}
for some
t^{\prime}
– see Property (d) in Page
8.3
. Hence, we define
F_{1,ext}(t)
as the unique
P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
(if there exists one) such that for
t^{\prime}\in P
,
(i^{\prime}_{ev})_{t}=(i_{e}v)_{t^{\prime}}
. In case there is no such
P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
, we define
F_{1,ext}(t)
as the unique
P\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
such that for
t^{\prime}\in P
,
(i^{\prime}_{ev})_{t}=(j_{od})_{t^{\prime}}
. In Figure
1
, we observe for instance that
F_{1,ext}(7)=F_{1,ext}(8)
is a group of
\mathcal{P}[j_{od}]
.
We define similarly
F_{2,ext}
be reversing the role of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. One easily checks that the definitions of
F_{1,ext}
and
F_{2,ext}
only depend on
S
,
S^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}
,
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
but not on the specific values of
i
and
j
.

Finally, we introduce
\gamma^{\prime}
,
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
, and
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
. First,
\gamma^{\prime}
corresponds to the number values of the form
(r,r)
that occur in
(i^{\prime}\oplus j^{\prime})
but not in
(i\oplus j)
. This quantity does not depend on the specific values of
(i,j)
and can equivalently be defined by

\gamma^{\prime}=\sum_{P\in F_{1,ext}(S^{\prime})}{\mathbf{1}}_{P\cap S=\emptyset}=\sum_{P\in F_{2,ext}(S^{\prime})}{\mathbf{1}}_{P\cap S=\emptyset}\kern 5.0pt.

Then,
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
(resp.
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
) is the number of groups
P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
that do not intersect
S
but whose inverse image through
F_{1,ext}
(resp.
F_{2,ext}
) intersects
S^{\prime}
. Equivalently,
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
is the number of distinct values
r
of
i^{\prime}_{ev}
such that
N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})>0
,
N_{rr}(ij)=0
, and
N_{rs}(ij)>0
for some
s\neq r
.

\gamma^{\prime}_{1}=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}}{\mathbf{1}}_{P\cap S=\emptyset}{\mathbf{1}}_{(F_{1,ext}^{-1}(P))\cap S^{\prime}\neq\emptyset}\ ;\quad\gamma^{\prime}_{2}=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{2}}{\mathbf{1}}_{P\cap S=\emptyset}{\mathbf{1}}_{(F_{2,ext}^{-1}(P))\cap S^{\prime}\neq\emptyset}\kern 5.0pt.

One easily checks that
\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma^{\prime}_{1}+\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
. In fact, we even have
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime}_{1}+\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
. For instance, in Figure
1
, we have
\gamma^{\prime}=1
,
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}=1
, and
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}=1
.

Among the
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|
groups of the partition
\mathcal{P}_{1}
of
[2k]
of distinct values of
i_{ev}
,
\gamma
groups intersect
S
and correspond to values
r
such that
N_{rr}(ij)>0
,
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
groups correspond to values
r
such that
N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})>0
and
N_{rr}(ij)=0
, and
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
groups correspond to values
r
such that
N_{rr}(ij)+N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})=0
. Among the
|\mathcal{P}_{2}|
groups of the partition
\mathcal{P}_{2}
of
[2k]
of distinct values of
j_{od}
,
\gamma
groups intersect
S
and correspond to values
r
such that
N_{rr}(ij)>0
,
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
groups correspond to values
r
such that
N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})>0
and
N_{rr}(ij)=0
, and
|\mathcal{P}_{2}|-\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
groups correspond to values
r
such that
N_{rr}(ij)+N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})=0
.

The upper bound (
49
) of
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
does not depend on the specific values of
(i,j)
in these
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
and
|\mathcal{P}_{2}|-\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
groups. Recall that there are
\gamma+\gamma^{\prime}
distinct values
r
of such that
N_{rr}(ij)+N_{rr}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})>0
. In fact, there exists a vector of positive integers
\alpha_{(1)}\geq\alpha_{(2)}\geq\ldots\geq\alpha_{(\gamma+\gamma^{\prime})}>0
satisfying
\sum_{l=1}^{\gamma+\gamma^{\prime}}\alpha_{(l)}=|S|+|S^{\prime}|=2|S|
and a
\gamma+\gamma^{\prime}
-tuple
l_{1},\ldots,l_{\gamma+\gamma^{\prime}}
of distinct values (that only depend on the values of
i_{ev}
in the
\gamma+\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
groups of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and on the values of
j_{od}
in the
\gamma+\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
groups in
\mathcal{P}_{2}
) such that

T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\leq(2k)^{2k-|S|-|P_{1}|-|P_{2}|+\gamma+1}\prod_{s=1}^{\gamma+\gamma}\left[e^{1/2}\sigma_{l_{s}}(\mathbf{A}^{+})\right]^{\alpha_{(l)}}\kern 5.0pt.

Summing over all possible sequences
(i,j)
, we conclude that

\displaystyle R_{S,S^{\prime};\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2};F_{1};F_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{k}(2k)^{2k-|S|-|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-|\mathcal{P}_{2}|+\gamma+1}p^{|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}_{1}}q^{|\mathcal{P}_{2}|-\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}_{2}}\sum_{l_{1},\ldots,l_{\gamma+\gamma^{\prime}}=1}^{q}\prod_{s=1}^{\gamma+\gamma^{\prime}}\sigma_{l_{s}}(\mathbf{A}^{+})^{\alpha_{(s)}}

(50)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{k}(2k)^{2k-|S|-|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-|\mathcal{P}_{2}|+\gamma+1}p^{|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}_{1}}q^{|\mathcal{P}_{2}|-\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}_{2}}\prod_{s=1}^{\gamma+\gamma^{\prime}}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|_{\alpha_{(s)}}^{\alpha_{(s)}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{k}(2k)^{2k-|S|-|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-|\mathcal{P}_{2}|+\gamma+1}p^{|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}_{1}}q^{|\mathcal{P}_{2}|+\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime}_{2}-1}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|_{2|S|}^{2|S|}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{k}(2k)^{2k-|S|-|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-|\mathcal{P}_{2}|+\gamma+1}p^{|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}_{1}}q^{|\mathcal{P}_{2}|+\gamma^{\prime}_{1}-1}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|_{2|S|}^{2|S|}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{k}(2k)^{2k-|S|-|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-|\mathcal{P}_{2}|+\gamma+1}p^{|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-\gamma}q^{|\mathcal{P}_{2}|-1}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|_{2|S|}^{2|S|}\kern 5.0pt,

where we used Hölder’s inequality in the third line,
\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma^{\prime}_{1}+\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
in the fourth line and
p\geq q
in the last line. This upper bound does not depend on the values of
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
. Given
S
,
S^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, let us count the number of possible maps
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
such that
R_{S,S^{\prime};\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2};F_{1};F_{2}}>0
.

Lemma 8.6
.

Given
S
,
S^{\prime}
with
|S^{\prime}|=|S|
and partitions
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
of
[2k]
, there are at most

(2e)^{2k}k^{2k-|S|+1-|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-|\mathcal{P}_{2}|+\gamma}

(51)

possible maps
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
such that
R_{S,S^{\prime};\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2};F_{1};F_{2}}>0
.

Upon defining
R_{S,S^{\prime};\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}}=\sum_{F_{1},F_{2}}R_{S,S^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2},F_{1},F_{2}}
, we derive that

R_{S,S^{\prime};\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}}\leq 2^{4k}e^{3k}k^{2(2k-|S|-|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-|\mathcal{P}_{2}|+\gamma+1)}p^{|\mathcal{P}_{1}|-\gamma}q^{|\mathcal{P}_{2}|-1}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|_{2|S|}^{2|S|}\kern 5.0pt.

(52)

Now, we shall sum these expressions
R_{S,S^{\prime};\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}}
over the partitions
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Fix
l_{1}
,
l_{2}
, and
r\leq l_{1}\wedge l_{2}
and define
R_{S,S^{\prime};r,l_{1},l_{2}}=\sum_{\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}\ |\mathcal{P}_{1}|=l_{1},\ |\mathcal{P}_{2}|=l_{2},\ \gamma=r}R_{S,S^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}}
the sum over partitions
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
with sizes
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
and where
\gamma
is equal to
r
.

Lemma 8.7
.

The number of partitions
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}
with respective sizes
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
and common number of groups
r
is at most

e^{3k}k^{2k-|S|-l_{1}-l_{2}+r}\kern 5.0pt.

We deduce that

\displaystyle R_{S,S^{\prime};r,l_{1},l_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle k^{2}(2^{4k}e^{6k})k^{3[2k-|S|-l_{1}-l_{2}+r]}p^{l_{1}-r}q^{l_{2}-1}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|_{2|S|}^{2|S|}

(53)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle k^{2}(2^{4k}e^{6k})k^{3(2k-|S|)}\left(\frac{\overline{p}}{k^{3}}\right)^{l_{1}-r}\left(\frac{\overline{q}}{k^{3}}\right)^{l_{2}-1}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|_{2|S|}^{2|S|}\ ,

where we denote
\overline{p}=p\vee k^{3}
and
\overline{q}=q\vee k^{3}
.
Since
\overline{p}\geq\overline{q}\geq k^{3}
, the above rhs is maximized for
l_{1}-r
and
l_{2}
the largest possible. For
|S|=0
, we simply use
l_{1}\leq k
and
l_{2}\leq k
and
r=0
.

For
|S|=s>0
, we first observe that each of the
l_{1}-r
groups of the partition
\mathcal{P}_{1}
outside
S
must contain at least two points. This implies that

2l_{1}-2r\leq 2k-s
. Since
l_{1}-r
is an integer, we deduce that
l_{1}-r\leq k-\lceil s/2\rceil
.

Furthermore, we denote
\Delta_{S,(-)}=\{t\in S^{c}:t+1\in S\}
and
\Delta_{S,(+)}=\{t\in S^{c}:t-1\in S\}
the left and right boundaries of
S
. Since the subsets of the form
\{2t+1,2t+2\}
are in the same group in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and the subsets of the form
\{2t,2t+1\}
are in the same group in
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, it follows that each
t\in\Delta_{S,(-)}\cup\Delta_{S,(+)}
either belongs to a group of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
that intersects
S
or a group in
\mathcal{P}_{2}
that intersects
S
. Besides, each
t\in\Delta_{S,(-)}\cap\Delta_{S,(+)}
belongs to a group of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
that intersects
S
and a group in
\mathcal{P}_{2}
that intersects
S
.
Since each of the
l_{1}-r
groups of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
l_{2}-r
groups of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
that do not intersect
S
are at least of size
2
, we deduce that
2(l_{1}-r+l_{2}-r)\leq 4k-2s-|\Delta_{S,(-)}|-|\Delta_{S,(+)}|
which implies that

l_{1}-r+l_{2}-1\leq 2k-s-\frac{|\Delta_{S,(-)}|+|\Delta_{S,(+)}|}{2}+r-1\leq 2k-s-1\kern 5.0pt,

since both
|\Delta_{S,(-)}|
and
|\Delta_{S,(+)}|
equal the number of connected components of
S
which is larger or equal to
r
. Together with the bound
l_{1}-r\leq k-\lceil s/2\rceil
and the assumption
k^{3}\leq\overline{q}\leq\overline{p}
, we deduce from (
53
) that

R_{S,S^{\prime};r,l_{1},l_{2}}\leq k^{5}(2^{4k}e^{6k})\overline{p}^{k-\lceil|S|/2\rceil}\overline{q}^{k-\lfloor|S|/2\rfloor-1}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|_{2|S|}^{2|S|}\kern 5.0pt.

There are at most
2^{4k}
possible sets
(S,S^{\prime})
and at most
k^{3}
possible values for
r
,
l_{1}
, and
l_{2}
. Hence, together with (
44
) we conclude that

\operatorname{Var}\left(U_{k}\right)\leq k^{8}c_{0}^{k}\left[(\overline{p}\overline{q})^{k}+\sum_{s=1}^{2k-1}\overline{p}^{k-\lceil s/2\rceil}\overline{q}^{k-\lfloor s/2\rfloor-1}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|_{2s}^{2s}\right]\kern 5.0pt,

for
c_{0}=(2^{8}e^{6})
.
Applying again Hölder’s inequality, we get
\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|_{2s}^{2s}\leq[\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|^{2s}_{4k-4}q^{1-s/(2k-2)}]\wedge[\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|^{2s}_{4k-2}q^{1-s/(2k-1)}]
for
s\leq 2k-2
. Hence,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\left(U_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle k^{8}c_{0}^{k}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{k-1}\overline{p}^{k-t}\overline{q}^{k-t\frac{k}{k-1}}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|^{4t}_{4k-4}+\overline{p}^{k-t-1}\overline{q}^{k-t-\frac{2t+1}{2k-1}}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|^{4t+2}_{4k-2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle k^{9}c_{0}^{k}\left[(\overline{p}\overline{q})^{k}+\overline{p}^{k-1}\overline{q}^{k-\frac{1}{2k-1}}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|^{2}_{4k-2}+\overline{p}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|^{4k-4}_{4k-4}+\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|^{4k-2}_{4k-2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle k^{9}c_{0}^{k}\left[(\overline{p}\overline{q})^{k}+[(\overline{p}\overline{q})^{k}]^{1-\frac{1}{2k-1}}\left[\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|^{4k-2}_{4k-2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2k-1}}+\overline{p}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|^{4k-4}_{4k-4}+\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|^{4k-2}_{4k-2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2k^{9}c_{0}^{k}\left[(\overline{p}\overline{q})^{k}+\overline{p}\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|^{4k-4}_{4k-4}+\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|^{4k-2}_{4k-2}\right]\ ,

where we used that the sequences are monotonic with respect to
t
in the second line, that
\overline{p}\geq\overline{q}
in the third line, and that
x^{a}y^{1-a}\leq x\vee y
for any
a\in[0,1]
. Since
\|\mathbf{A}^{+}\|^{2s}_{2s}\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2s}^{2s}+q(2k)^{s}
, we conclude that

\operatorname{Var}\left(U_{k}\right)\leq k^{c_{1}}(c^{\prime}_{1})^{k}\left[(pq)^{k}+p\|\mathbf{A}\|_{4k-4}^{4k-4}+\|\mathbf{A}\|_{4k-2}^{4k-2}+p^{k}k^{3k}+k^{6k}\right]\kern 5.0pt\ ,

for some
c_{1}
and
c^{\prime}_{1}>0
, which concludes the proof.

∎

Proof of Lemma
8.5
.

Since
S^{id}[i,j]=S
, we have
\sum_{r\neq s}N_{rs}(ij)=2k-|S|
. Write
d=\sum_{r\neq s}{\mathbf{1}}_{N_{rs}(ij)>0}
. We have therefore

\prod_{r\neq s}[N_{rs}(ij)]!\leq(2k)^{2k-|S|-d}\ ,

and it suffices to prove that
d\geq|\mathcal{P}_{1}|+|\mathcal{P}_{2}|-\gamma-1
. Write
d^{\prime}=\sum_{r,s}{\mathbf{1}}_{N_{rs}(ij)>0}
. By definition of
\gamma
, we have
d+\gamma\geq d^{\prime}
and we shall prove that
d^{\prime}\geq|\mathcal{P}_{1}|+|\mathcal{P}_{2}|-1
. Exploring iteratively the sequence
(i\oplus j)
in order, we have
N_{i_{1},j_{1}}(ij)>0
. Then, each time we visit the first element of one group of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
or one group of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, we discover a new
(r,s)
such
N_{rs}(ij)>0
. Since, except at the first coordinate of
(i\oplus j)
, we cannot simultaneously visit for the first time an element of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and element of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, we deduce that
d^{\prime}\geq|P_{1}|+|P_{2}|-1
and the result follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.6
.

The two partitions
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
induce a partition of
[2k]\setminus S
into groups of the form
(r,s)
where
r\in[|\mathcal{P}_{1}|]
and
s\in[|\mathcal{P}_{2}|]
.
Note that the number of groups of this partition is exactly
d=\sum_{r\neq s}{\mathbf{1}}_{N_{rs}(ij)>0}
, where
i
and
j
are any sequence that are compatible with
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
.
The maps
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
uniquely induce a partition of
[2k]\setminus S^{\prime}
. By property (a) in Page
8.3
,
R_{S,S^{\prime};\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2};F_{1};F_{2}}>0
only if this partition of
[2k]\setminus S^{\prime}
is, up a permutation, the same as one induced
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
on
[2k]\setminus S^{\prime}
. In particular, the number of possible
(F_{1},F_{2})
is less than the Stirling number of the second kind with parameters
n=(2k-|S^{\prime}|)
and
d
, which in turn is smaller than

\binom{2k-|S^{\prime}|}{d}d^{2k-|S^{\prime}|-d}\leq e^{2k}(2k)^{2k-|S^{\prime}|-d}\leq(2e)^{2k}k^{2k-|S^{\prime}|-d}\ .

We have shown in the proof of Lemma
8.5
that
d\geq|\mathcal{P}_{1}|+|\mathcal{P}_{2}|-\gamma-1
. Since
|S^{\prime}|=|S|
, the result follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.7
.

Let us count the number of possible partitions
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
of
[2k]
with respective size
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
.
Write
K
the number of connected components of
S
when one consider
[2k]
as a torus. Among those
K
connected components, denote
K_{1}
(resp.
K_{2}
) the number of connected components whose most left entry lies at an even (resp. odd) position. As explained earlier, if the most left entry
t
of a connected component
T
of
S
is an even integer than
t-1
belongs to the same group as
t
in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. Besides, all the points of a connected component
T
of
S
belong to the same group.
Since there are
r
groups of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
that intersect
S
, we start by considering the total number of induced partitions on
S
with
r
groups which is at most
r^{K}/r!
. Given this induced partition it remains to count the number of possible partitions
\mathcal{P}_{1}
outside
S
. Since
\mathcal{P}_{1}
is completely defined by its restrictions to odd integers of
[2k]
, it remains to define the partition
\mathcal{P}_{1}
on
k-K_{1}-|S_{1}|
points, where
S_{1}
(resp.
S_{2}
) is the intersection of
S
with the set of odd (resp. even) integers. Arguing similarly for
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, we derive that that there are at most

\frac{r^{K}l_{1}^{k-|S_{1}|-K_{1}}l_{2}^{k-|S_{2}|-K_{1}}}{r!(l_{1}-r)!(l_{2}-r)!}\kern 5.0pt,

possible partitions
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Using Stirling’s lower bound
r!\geq(r/e)^{r}
, that
K_{1}+K_{2}=K
,
|S_{1}|+|S_{2}|=|S|
and that
l_{1}\vee l_{2}\leq k
, we conclude that the total number of partitions it at most

e^{r}\frac{r^{K-r}l_{1}^{k+r-|S_{1}|-K_{1}}l_{2}^{k+r-|S_{2}|-K_{2}}}{l_{1}!l_{2}!}\leq e^{3k}k^{2k-|S|-l_{1}-l_{2}+r}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

8.4
Proof of Proposition
3.1

The result is a consequence of the following lemma.

Lemma 8.8
.

There exist coefficients
\alpha_{s}
such that

V_{k}=\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{k}]+\alpha_{0}+\sum_{l=1}^{k-1}\sum_{s=(s_{1},\ldots,)\ ,1\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq s_{3};\sum s_{i}=l}\alpha_{s}\prod\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})]^{s_{i}}

(54)

satisfies
\mathbb{E}[U_{k}]=\mathbb{E}[V_{k}]
for all
\mathbf{A}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times q}
.

Obviously, both
\mathbb{E}[V_{k}]
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[U_{k}]
are polynomials with respect to the entries of
\mathbf{A}
. Since these two polynomials take the same value for all
\mathbf{A}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times q}
and since the polynomial evaluation morphism is injective (see Corollary 1.6 in
[
22
]
), this implies that both
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[U_{k}]
and
\mathbb{E}[V_{k}]
correspond to the same polynomial in
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}[X_{ij},1\leq i\leq p;1\leq j\leq q]
.

We now deduce that
U_{k}=V_{k}
. Let
Y\sim\mathcal{N}(x,1)
. For any non-negative integer
\beta
, there exist a polynomial
G_{\beta}
of degree
\beta
such that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{\beta}]=G_{\beta}(x)
. Besides, its term of degree
\beta
is exactly
x^{\beta}
. To see this, it suffices to observe to use the binomial formula on
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{\beta}]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[(x+(Y-x))^{\beta}]
.

Consider the vector space morphism
\psi:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}[(X_{ij})]\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}[(X_{ij})]
such that
\psi[\prod_{i=1,j=1}^{p,q}X_{ij}^{\beta_{ij}}]=\prod_{i=1,j=1}^{p,q}G_{\beta_{ij}}[X_{ij}]
. Then,
\mathbb{E}(V_{k})=\psi[(V_{k})](\mathbf{A})
and
\mathbb{E}(U_{k})=\psi[(U_{k})](\mathbf{A})
. This implies that
\psi(U_{k})=\psi(V_{k})
. We claim that
\psi
is a injective. As a consequence,
U_{k}=V_{k}
and the result follows.

Let us show that
\psi
is injective. Consider any monomial of the form
Z=\prod_{i=1,j=1}^{p,q}X_{ij}^{\beta_{ij}}
. Observe that
\psi(Z)-Z
has a total degree less than
\sum_{ij}\beta_{ij}
. Given a polynomial
P
and the sum
P_{l}
of monomials of largest total degree, we derive
\psi(P)-P_{l}
has a total degree less than that of
P
. Thus,
\psi(P)
has the same total degree as
P
and
\psi
is therefore injective.

Proof of Lemma
8.8
.

Given a sequence of integers
1\leq\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\alpha_{r}
, we claim that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t=1}^{r}\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{\alpha_{t}}]\right]-\prod_{t=1}^{r}\tr[(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})^{\alpha_{t}}]

(55)

is a symmetric polynomial with total degree less than
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}
with respect to (
\sigma^{2}_{i}(\mathbf{A})
,
i=1,\ldots,q
). The proof of this claim is postponed to the end of the proof. Since the space of symmetric polynomials is spanned by the Newton sums,
there exist coefficients
\beta_{s}
such that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t=1}^{r}\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{\alpha_{t}}]\right]=\prod_{t=1}^{r}\tr[(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})^{\alpha_{t}}]+\sum_{s:\sum s_{t}<\sum\alpha_{t}}\beta_{s}\prod_{t=1}^{l(s)}\tr[(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})^{s_{t}}]\ .

Then, we prove the existence of
V_{k}
by induction. First,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{k}]]-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}=Z_{1}
where
Z_{1}
is of the form

Z_{1}=\sum_{s:\sum s_{t}<k}\beta^{(1)}_{s}\prod_{t=1}^{l(s)}\tr[(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})^{s_{t}}]
. Then, considering each term of total degree
k-1
, we can estimate them by
\beta^{(1)}_{s}\prod_{t=1}^{l(s)}\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{s_{t}}]
. The resulting bias has a total degree less or equal to
k-2
. At each step, one can correct the estimator to decrease the total degree of the bias. The result follows.

Proof of the claim in (
55
)
.
Write
Z=\prod_{t=1}^{r}\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{\alpha_{t}}]
. Since
Z
is invariant by left and right orthogonal transformation of
\mathbf{Y}
, we assume without loss of generality that
\mathbf{A}
has null non-diagonal entries. As a consequence,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Z]
is a polynomial with respect to
\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})
. Since
Z
is invariant by permutation of the rows and columns of
\mathbf{Y}
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Z]
is symmetric. It remains to prove that (i)
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Z]-\prod_{t=1}^{r}\prod\tr[(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})^{\alpha_{t}}]
has a total degree less than
2k
and (ii) that, for all
i=1,\ldots,q
, the order of
\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})
in each monomial is even:

For
X\sim\mathcal{N}(x,1)
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[X^{r}]-x^{r}
is polynomial of degree less than
r
. Since the decomposition of
Z
leads to a sum of monomials in
\mathbf{Y}_{ij}
of total degree equals to
\sum_{t=1}^{r}\alpha_{t}
, it follows that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Z]
contains the exact same monomial in
A_{ij}
of total degree
\sum_{t=1}^{r}\alpha_{t}
and the remaining terms have a total degree less than that. (i) follows.

Turning to (ii), we decompose again
Z
into monomials.
Z
writes as

Z=\sum_{(i^{(1)},j^{(1)})\in p^{\alpha_{1}}\times q^{\alpha_{1}}}\ldots\sum_{(i^{(r)},j^{(r)})\in p^{\alpha_{r}}\times q^{\alpha_{r}}}\prod_{l=1}^{\alpha_{1}}\mathbf{Y}_{i^{(1)}_{l}j^{(1)}_{l}}\mathbf{Y}_{i^{(1)}_{l+1}j^{(1)}_{l}}\ldots\prod_{l=1}^{\alpha_{r}}\mathbf{Y}_{i^{(r)}_{l}j^{(r)}_{l}}\mathbf{Y}_{i^{(r)}_{l+1}j^{(r)}_{l}}

As a consequence, for any index
r\in[p]
, the set
\{\mathbf{Y}_{rs};s=1,\ldots,q\}
is visited an even number of times in each monomial. Besides, the expectation of one monomial is non-zero only if each non diagonal element
\mathbf{Y}_{rs}
is visited an even number of times since
\mathbf{A}_{rs}=0
and the normal distribution is symmetric. As a consequence of these two observations, each
\mathbf{Y}_{ss}
must be visited an even number of times in the monomial so that its expectation is non-zero. Since, for
X\sim\mathcal{N}(x,1)
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[X^{2r}]
is an even polynomial in
x
, this implies that the expectation of each monomial of
Z
only involves terms of the form
\sigma^{2r}_{i}(\mathbf{A})
and (ii) follows.

∎

Proof of Corollary
3.4
.

Since
l_{r}
norms of vectors are non increasing with respect to
r
, we have
\|\mathbf{A}\|^{4k-2}_{4k-2}\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|^{4k-2}_{2k}
and
\|\mathbf{A}\|^{4k-4}_{4k-4}\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|^{4k-4}_{2k}
. Hence, we derive from Theorem
3.3
and Chebychev inequality that

\operatorname{Var}\left(U_{k}\right)^{1/2}\leq c^{k}\left[(pq)^{k/2}+p^{1/2}\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2k-2}_{2k}+\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2k-1}_{2k}+(pk^{3})^{k/2}+k^{3k}\right]\ .

(56)

We consider two cases depending on
\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2k}_{2k}
. If
\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2k}_{2k}\leq[(p\vee k^{3})(q\vee k^{3})]^{k/2}
, then the above risk bound simplifies in

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[|(U_{k})-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}|\right]\leq 5c^{k}[(p\vee k^{3})(q\vee k^{3})]^{k/2}
. Then, together with the inequality
|x^{1/(2k)}-y^{1/(2k)}|\leq|x-y|^{1/(2k)}
and Hölder’s inequality, we have

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[|(U_{k})_{+}^{1/(2k)}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}|\right]\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[|U_{k}-\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2k}_{2k}|\right]^{1/2k}\lesssim[(p\vee k^{3})(q\vee k^{3})]^{1/4}\ .

(57)

Let us turn to the case where
\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2k}_{2k}\geq[(p\vee k^{3})(q\vee k^{3})]^{k/2}
. Since

|(1+x)^{1/(2k)}-1|\leq 2|x|
for any
x\geq-1/2
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[|(U_{k})_{+}^{1/(2k)}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}|\right]

(58)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}\big|[U_{k}/\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2k}_{2k}]^{\frac{1}{2k}}-1\big|\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[U_{k}\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}/2\right]+2\|\mathbf{A}\|^{1-2k}_{2k}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[|U_{k}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}\left[4\frac{\operatorname{Var}\left(U_{k}\right)}{\|\mathbf{A}\|^{4k}_{2k}}+2\frac{\operatorname{Var}\left(U_{k}\right)^{1/2}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2k}_{2k}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c^{\prime}c^{k}\left[\frac{(p\vee k^{3})(q\vee k^{3})]^{k}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|^{4k-1}_{2k}}+\frac{(p\vee k^{3})(q\vee k^{3})]^{k/2}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2k-1}_{2k}}+\frac{p}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{3}}+\frac{p^{1/2}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}}+\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}}+1\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle c^{k}[(p\vee k^{3})(q\vee k^{3})]^{1/4}\ ,

where we used (
56
) in the fourth line and the condition
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}\geq(p\vee k^{3})(q\vee k^{3})^{1/4}
in the last line.

∎

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

The definition of the estimators
U_{2}
and
U_{3}
is a consequence of the following moment identities

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\tr(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}+pq\ ;

(59)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\tr((\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{2})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}\|_{4}^{4}+2(p+q+1)\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}+pq(1+q+p)\ ;

(60)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\tr((\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{3})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}\|_{6}^{6}+3(p+q+1)\|\mathbf{A}\|_{4}^{4}+3(p^{2}+q^{2}+3pq+3p+3q+4)\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}

(61)

\displaystyle+pq[p^{2}+q^{2}+3pq+3p+3q+4]\ .

After some tedious computations, we see that the expressions in the right-hand side in Proposition
3.2
are unbiased estimators of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}
and
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{4}^{4}
respectively.

In the remainder of the proof, we show (
59
–
61
).
The first identity has already been proved to analyze
U_{1}
. In order to derive the second and the third identity, we decompose
\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{Y}^{T}
into a sum involving its expectation.

\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}=\left(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}+p\mathbf{I}_{q}\right)+\left(\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}+\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}\right)=:\mathbf{S}+\mathbf{N}\ .

Since
\mathbf{S}
is deterministic and
\mathbf{N}
is centered, we have

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{2}]]=\tr(\mathbf{S}^{2})+\tr[\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}(\mathbf{N}^{2})]\ .

Since the Gaussian distribution is symmetric, we obtain by straightforward computation that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{N}^{2}]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[(\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q})^{2}]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}+\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{A})^{2}]=(q+1)\left[p\mathbf{I}_{q}+2\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\right]\ .

(62)

Combining the two previous identities leads to (
60
). Turning to (
61
), we have

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\tr[(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{2}]]=\tr(\mathbf{S}^{3})+\tr[\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}(\mathbf{N}^{3})]+3\tr[\mathbf{S}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}(\mathbf{N}^{2})]\ ,

(63)

since
\mathbf{N}
is centered. For
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\tr(\mathbf{N}^{3})]
, we use a again that odd moments of centered normal distributions are null to derive

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\tr(\mathbf{N}^{3})]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\tr[(\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q})^{3}]]+3\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\tr[(\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q})(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}+\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{A})^{2}]\right]\ .

(64)

Starting with the first expression, we derive from moments of standard Wishart distribution (see e.g.
[
25
]
) that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\tr[(\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q})^{3}]]=pq[q^{2}+3q+4]\ .

Tedious computations also lead us to

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\tr[(\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q})(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}+\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{A})^{2}]\right]=\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}[(q-1)(q-2)+3(q-1)2+8]=\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}[q^{2}+3q+4]\ .

Combining the two previous identities in (
64
), we obtain

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\tr(\mathbf{N}^{3})]=[q^{2}+3q+4](pq+3\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2})\ .

Then, coming back to (
63
), and relying on (
62
), we get

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\tr((\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y})^{3})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tr[(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}+p\mathbf{I}_{q})^{3}]+[q^{2}+3q+4](pq+3\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle+3(q+1)\tr[(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}+p\mathbf{I}_{q})(2\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}+p\mathbf{I}_{q})]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}\|_{6}^{6}+3(p+q+1)\|\mathbf{A}\|_{4}^{4}+3(p^{2}+q^{2}+3pq+3p+3q+4)\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+p^{3}q+q^{3}p+3p^{2}q^{2}+3p^{2}q+3q^{2}p+4pq\ ,

which is exactly (
61
).

∎

8.5
Proofs for general norms
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}

Given a symmetric matrix
\mathbf{B}
, we denote
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B})\geq\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{B})\geq\ldots
its sequence of eigenvalues.
We start with a technical lemma.

Lemma 8.9
.

Define
\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}
.
Write
I(\mathbf{A})
for the image of
\mathbf{A}
and
\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}
for any orthogonal projection matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
onto
I(\mathbf{A})
. For any
i=1,\ldots,q
, we have

|\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{W})-\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{A})|\leq 2\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})\|\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}+\|\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}\mathbf{E}\|^{2}_{\infty}+\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|_{\infty}\ .

(65)

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Define
\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}
, then
\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{Y})-p=\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{W})
so that
T_{s}=[\sum_{i=1}^{q}(\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{W}))_{+}^{s/2}]^{1/s}
. We first consider the case
s/2\geq 1
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have
|(x+y)_{+}^{s/2}-x^{s/2}|\leq s|y|(2x)^{s/2-1}+(2|y|)^{s/2}
. It then follows from the triangular inequality and Lemma
8.9
that

\displaystyle|T^{s}_{s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{q}|[\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{W})]_{+}^{s/2}-\sigma^{s}_{i}(\mathbf{A})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{s/2}s\sum_{i=1}^{q}\left[\|\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}\mathbf{E}\|^{2}_{\infty}+\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|_{\infty}+2\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})\|\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}\right]\sigma^{s-2}_{i}(\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+3^{s/2-1}\left[\|\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}\mathbf{E}\|^{s}_{\infty}+\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|^{s/2}_{\infty}+2^{s/2}\sigma^{s/2}_{i}(\mathbf{A})\|\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}\mathbf{E}\|^{s/2}_{\infty}\right]\ .

Since
\mathbf{E}
is distribution invariant by a left orthogonal transformation
\|\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}
is distributed as the operator norm of a
\mathrm{dim}(I(\mathbf{A}))\times q
noise matrix. By integration, we deduce from Lemma
A.2
that, for any
s\geq 1
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\|\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}\mathbf{E}\|^{s}_{\infty}]\leq c^{s}(q\vee s)^{s/2}
. Similarly, arguing as in the proof of Lemma
8.2
and integrating the tail distribution of
\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|_{\infty}
, we derive that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|^{s}_{\infty}]\leq c^{s}[(p\vee s)(q\vee s)]^{s/2}
for any
s\geq 1
. This allows us to conclude that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[|T^{s}_{s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c^{s}\left[\sqrt{pq}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s-2}^{s-2}+\sqrt{q}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s-1}^{s-1}+q[(p\vee s)(q\vee s)]^{s/4}+(q\vee s)^{s/4}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s/2}^{s/2}\right]

(67)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c^{s}\left[\sqrt{pq}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s-2}^{s-2}+\sqrt{q}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s-1}^{s-1}+q[(p\vee s)(q\vee s)]^{s/4}\right]\ ,

where we used Hölder’s inequality and
xy\leq x^{\alpha}+y^{\beta}
for
1/\alpha+1/\beta=1
.

For
s\in[1,2)
, we use the inequality
|(x+y)_{+}^{s/2}-x^{s/2}|\leq|y|^{s/2}\wedge(2|y||x|^{s/2-1})
. We then deduce from Lemma
8.9
that

|T^{s}_{s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}|=\sum_{i=1}^{q}|[\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{W})]_{+}^{s/2}-\sigma^{s}_{i}(\mathbf{A})|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{q}\|\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}\mathbf{E}\|^{s}_{\infty}+\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|^{s/2}_{\infty}+4\sigma^{s-1}_{i}(\mathbf{A})\|\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}\ .

(68)

Bounding the moments as above, we conclude that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[|T^{s}_{s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}|\right]\lesssim\left[q(pq)^{s/4}+\sqrt{q}\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s-1}_{s-1}\right]\ .

(69)

The first result follows from (
67
) and (
69
).
Regarding the second result, we use again that, for
s>1
,

|(x+y)_{+}^{1/s}-x^{1/s}|\leq|y|^{1/s}\wedge(2|y||x|^{1/s-1})
. First, assume that
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}\leq q^{1/s}[(p\vee s)(q\vee s)]^{1/4}
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[|T_{s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\left[q^{1/s}[(p\vee s)(q\vee s)^{1/4}+q^{1/(2s)}\|\mathbf{A}\|^{1-1/s}_{s-1}+{\mathbf{1}}_{s\geq 2}(pq)^{1/(2s)}\|\mathbf{A}\|^{1-2/s}_{s-2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle cq^{1/s}[(p\vee s)(q\vee s)]^{1/4}\ ,

where we used Hölder’s inequality. If
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}\geq q^{1/s}[(p\vee s)(q\vee s)]^{1/4}
, then we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[|T_{s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c^{s}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}^{1-s}\left[q[(p\vee s)(q\vee s)]^{s/4}+\sqrt{q}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s-1}^{s-1}+{\mathbf{1}}_{s\geq 2}\sqrt{pq}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s-2}^{s-2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c^{s}q^{1/s}(pq)^{1/4}\ ,

where we used again Hölder’s inequality.

∎

Proof of Lemma
8.9
.

Without loss of generality, assume that the matrix
\mathbf{A}
is in diagonal form, that is
\mathbf{A}_{ii}=\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})
for
i=1,\ldots,q
and
\mathbf{A}_{ij}
is zero otherwise. Then, we deduce from the interlacing inequality (Corollary III. 1.5 in
[
5
]
) that the
i
-th largest eigenvalues of
\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}
is less of or equal to to the first eigenvalue of the restriction of
\mathbf{W}
to indices in
[i,\ldots,q]\times[i,\ldots,q]
. Writing
\mathbf{W}_{[i:q]}
for this restriction, we arrive at

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{W})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{W}_{[i:q]})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}_{i}(\mathbf{A})+\|[\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}+\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}]_{[i:q]}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}_{i}(\mathbf{A})+\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|_{\infty}+2\|[\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{A}]_{[i:q]}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}_{i}(\mathbf{A})+\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|_{\infty}+2\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})\|[\mathbf{E}_{[1:q]}\|_{\infty}\ .

Turning the lower bound of
\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{W})
, we define
V_{i}
the subspace of dimension
i
spanned by the
i
-th largest eigenvectors of
\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}
(pick any such subspace if it is not unique). It follows from Courant-Fischer min-max theorem that

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{W})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in V_{i}}(x^{T}\mathbf{W}x)/|x|^{2}_{2}

(70)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{x:|x|_{2}=1}\sum_{j=1}^{i}x_{j}^{2}\sigma_{j}^{2}(\mathbf{A})+x^{T}(\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q})x+2\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{i}x_{j_{1}}x_{j_{2}}\sigma_{j_{1}}(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{E}_{j_{1}j_{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{x:|x|_{2}=1}\sum_{j=1}^{i}x_{j}^{2}\sigma_{j}^{2}(\mathbf{A})-2[\sum_{j=1}^{i}x_{j}^{2}\sigma_{j}^{2}(\mathbf{A})]^{1/2}\|\mathbf{E}_{[1:q]}\|_{\infty}-\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|_{\infty}\ ,

where we applied Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and used that
\inf f+g\geq\inf f+\inf g
. The quantity
\sum_{j=1}^{i}x_{j}^{2}\sigma_{j}^{2}(\mathbf{A})
lies in
[\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{A});\sigma_{1}^{2}(\mathbf{A})]
.
The function
x\mapsto x^{2}-2xz
is decreasing for
x\leq z
and increasing for
x\geq z
and its minimum equals
-z^{2}
. When
\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})\geq\|\mathbf{E}_{[1:q]}\|_{\infty}
, the minimum of the left-hand side expression in (
70
) is achieved at
\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})
and we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{W})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{A})-2\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})\|\mathbf{E}_{[1:q]}\|_{\infty}-\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{A})-2\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})\|\mathbf{E}_{[1:q]}\|_{\infty}-\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|_{\infty}-\|\mathbf{E}_{[1:q]}\|^{2}_{\infty}\ .

When
\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})<\|\mathbf{E}_{[1:q]}\|_{\infty}
, this implies that
\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{A})-2\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})\|\mathbf{E}_{[1:q]}\|_{\infty}<0
, and we also have

\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{W})\geq\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{A})-2\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})\|\mathbf{E}_{[1:q]}\|_{\infty}-\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|_{\infty}-\|\mathbf{E}_{[1:q]}\|^{2}_{\infty}\ .

All in all, we have proved that

\big|\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{W})-\sigma^{2}_{i}(\mathbf{A})\big|\leq\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|_{\infty}+2\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})\|[\mathbf{E}_{[1:q]}\|_{\infty}+\|\mathbf{E}_{[1:q]}\|^{2}_{\infty}\ .

Since
\mathbf{E}_{[1:q]}
is a submatrix of
\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}(\mathbf{E})
(recall that
\mathbf{A}
is assumed to be diagonal in the proof), we get the desired result.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

We only have to gather some arguments of Proposition
4.3
. Indeed, we have established in (
68
) that

\sum_{i=1}^{q}|[\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{W})]_{+}^{1/2}-\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})|\leq q(1+2\sqrt{2})\|\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}+q\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|^{1/2}_{\infty}\ .

Besides, we have shown that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\|\Pi_{I(\mathbf{A})}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}]\lesssim q^{1/4}
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|^{1/2}_{\infty}]\lesssim(pq)^{1/4}
. The result follows.

∎

Proof of Proposition
4.4
.

Since
U_{k}
is an unbiased estimator of
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[G_{s}[P;\mathbf{Y}]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M^{s}(pq)^{s/4}\left[a_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{\deg(P)}a_{k}\left\|\frac{\mathbf{A}}{M(pq)^{1/4}}\right\|^{2k}_{2k}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M^{s}(pq)^{s/4}\sum_{j=1}^{q}P\big[\lambda_{i}\big(M^{-2}(pq)^{-1/2}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\big)\Big]\ .

Assuming that
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq M(pq)^{1/4}
, we deduce that the bias of
G_{s}[P;\mathbf{Y}]
satisfies

\displaystyle|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[G_{s}[P;\mathbf{Y}]]-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}^{s}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle M^{s}(pq)^{s/4}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\left|\psi_{s}\left[\lambda_{i}\left(\frac{\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}}{M^{2}(pq)^{1/2}}\right)\right]-P\left[\lambda_{i}\left(\frac{\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}}{M^{2}(pq)^{1/2}}\right)\right]\right|

(71)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle M^{s}(pq)^{s/4}q|\psi-P|_{[0,1],\infty}\ .

Regarding the variance of
G_{s}[P;\mathbf{Y}]
, we deduce from Theorem
3.3
that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\left(G_{s}[P;\mathbf{Y}]\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle M^{2s}(pq)^{s/2}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}a_{k}^{2}\frac{\operatorname{Var}\left(U_{k}\right)}{M^{4k}(pq)^{k}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle M^{2s}(pq)^{s/2}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}c^{k}\frac{a_{k}^{2}}{M^{4k}}[1+p^{1-k}q^{1-k}\lambda_{1}^{2k-2}(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})+q(pq)^{-k}\lambda_{1}^{2k-1}(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})]\ ,

where we used that
k\leq K\leq q^{1/3}
. Assuming that
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq M(pq)^{1/4}
, this simplifies in

\frac{\operatorname{Var}\left(G_{s}[P;\mathbf{Y}]\right)}{M^{2s}(pq)^{s/2}}\leq K\sum_{k=1}^{K}c^{k}a_{k}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{M^{4k}}+\frac{1}{M^{4}}+\frac{\sqrt{q}}{\sqrt{p}M^{2}}\right)\lesssim\frac{K}{M^{4}\wedge[(p/q)^{1/2}M^{2}]}\sum_{k=1}^{K}c^{k}a_{k}^{2}\ ,

which, together with (
71
), leads to the desired result.
∎

The following simple result is used several times throughout the proofs.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
f
be a symmetric function defined on
[-1,1]
. For any positive integer
n
, we have

E_{\mathcal{P}_{n}}\left(f;[-1;1]\right)=E_{\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{n}}\left(f;[0;1]\right)

Proof of Lemma
8.10
.

First, we prove that
E_{\mathcal{P}_{n}}\left(f;[-1;1]\right)
is achieved by a symmetric polynomial. Consider any Polynomial
P
. By symmetry of
f
,

\sup_{x\in[-1,1]}|[P(x)+P(-x)]/2-f(x)|\leq\sup_{x\in[-1;1]}|f(x)-P(x)|
and the symmetric polynomial
[P(x)+P(-x)]/2
achieves a no higher approximation error. This implies that
E_{\mathcal{P}_{n}}\left(f;[-1;1]\right)=E_{\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{n}}\left(f;[-1;1]\right)=E_{\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{n}}\left(f;[0;1]\right)
, where we used again the symmetry of
f
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

The first result is equivalent to
||x|-B_{K}(x)|_{\infty,[-1,1]}\leq\frac{2}{\pi(2K+1)}
which has been proved in Bernstein’s original paper
[
3
]
. The bound
|a^{*}|_{\infty}\leq 2^{3K}
is proved in Lemma 2 of
[
7
]
.
Regarding the last result, we first apply Lemma
8.10
to deduce that

E_{\mathcal{P}_{2K}}[|x|;[-1,1]]=E_{\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{2K}}[|x|;[0,1]]

Since any polynomial
P\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
satisfies
\sup_{x\in[0,1]}|P(x^{2})-|x||=\sup_{x\in[0,1]}|P(x)-\sqrt{x}|
and since any symmetric polynomial of degree at most
2K
can be represented as
P(x^{2})
where
P\in\mathcal{P}_{K}
, we deduce that

E_{\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{2K}}[|x|;[0,1]]=E_{\mathcal{P}_{K}}[\sqrt{x};[0,1]]

Then, the result follows again from Bernstein’s paper
[
3
]
on the approximation of the absolute function.

∎

Proof of Proposition
4.6
.

Taking
K^{*}=\lfloor 0.5(\log^{-1}((8c)\vee 2))\log(q)\rfloor

where
c
is the constant in (
15
), we have
(c2^{3})^{K^{*}}\leq\sqrt{q}
. Then, we deduce from Proposition
4.4
and Lemma
4.5
that

\mathbb{E}\left[|G_{1}[P^{*}_{K^{*}};\mathbf{Y}]-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}|\right]\leq M(pq)^{1/4}\left[\frac{2q}{\pi(2K^{*}+1)}+c_{1}\sqrt{q}\right]\lesssim M\frac{q}{\log(q)}(pq)^{1/4}\ .

∎

Proof of Proposition
4.8
.

Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
4.5
, we deduce that
E_{\mathcal{P}_{K}}[x^{s/2};[0,1]]=E_{\mathcal{P}_{2K}}[|x|^{s};[-1,1]]
.
Then, combining Proposition
4.4
with Lemma
4.7
, we deduce that, for any positive integer
K

\mathbb{E}\left[|G_{s}[P^{*}_{K;s};\mathbf{Y}]-\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}|\right]\lesssim M^{s}(pq)^{s/4}\left[\frac{q}{K^{s}}+\frac{c^{K}}{M^{2}\wedge[M(p/q)^{1/4}]}|a|_{\infty}\right]\ ,

where
|a|_{\infty}
refers to the largest coefficient of
P^{*}_{K;s}
. Then, we rely on Theorem E of
[
30
]
to bound
|a|_{\infty}
:

Lemma 8.11

(
[
30
]
)
.

Let
P_{n}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{k}x^{k}
be a polynomial of degree at most
n
such that
|P_{n}(x)|\leq 1
for all
x\in[-1,1]
. Then,

|a_{n-2\nu}|
is bounded above by the modulus of the corresponding
coefficient of
L_{n}
for
\nu=0,1,\ldots,\lfloor n/2\rfloor
, and
|a_{n-1-2\nu}|
is
bounded above by the modulus of the corresponding coefficient
of
L_{n-1}
for
\nu=0,1,\ldots,\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor
. Here, we recall that
L_{n}(x)
is the
n
-th Chebychev polynomial.

Since
\sup_{x\in[-1,1]}|P^{*}_{K;s}(x^{2})-|x|^{s}|\leq 1
for
K
large enough and since the largest coefficient of the Chebychev polynomial is less or equal to
2^{3K}
(Lemma
4.5
), it follows that
|a|_{\infty}\leq c^{\prime K}
and

\mathbb{E}\left[|G_{s}[P^{*}_{K;s};\mathbf{Y}]-\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}|\right]\lesssim M^{s}(pq)^{s/4}\left[\frac{q}{K^{s}}+\frac{c^{K}}{M^{2}\wedge[M(p/q)^{1/4}]}\right]\ .

Taking
K=\lfloor\log^{-1}(c\vee 2)\log(q)\rfloor
leads to the first result. The second result is a straightforward consequence of Jensen inequality and the inequality
|x^{1/s}-y^{1/s}|\leq|x-y|^{1/s}
.

∎

8.6
Proof of Theorem
4.9

In comparison to the proof of Proposition
2.2
, we now follow a generalized Le Cam’s approach, by building two prior distributions
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
on matrices
\mathbf{A}
such that the corresponding integrated distributions of the observations
\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{i}}=\int\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{A}}\mu_{i}(d\mathbf{A})
are close in total variation distance, while the distributions of
f_{\sigma}(\mathbf{A})
under
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
are highly different. The statement of the following lemma is provided for a general functional
T
.

Lemma 8.12

(
[
37
]
, Thm. 2.15)
.

Consider any function
T:\ \mathbb{R}^{p\times q}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that there exist
s>0
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
and a collection
\mathcal{A}
of matrices
such that
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
are supported in
\mathcal{A}
and

\mu_{0}\big[\mathbf{A}:\ T(\mathbf{A})\leq\zeta-s\big]\geq 1-\beta_{0}\ ;\quad\quad\mu_{1}\big[\mathbf{A}:\ T(\mathbf{A})\geq\zeta+s\big]\geq 1-\beta_{1}\ .

If
\|\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{1}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{0}}\|_{TV}\leq\eta<1
, then

\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{A}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta}\left[|\widehat{T}-T(\mathbf{A})|\geq s\right]\geq\frac{1-\eta-\beta_{0}-\beta_{1}}{2}\ .

For a positive integer
n
, denote
\mathcal{U}(n)
the Haar measure on orthogonal matrices of size
n
. Let
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
be two probability measures supported on
[0,0.125(pq)^{1/4}]
whose definition is postponed at the end of the proof.
Given a vector
\theta
of size
\lceil q/2\rceil
, Let
\mathbf{D}(\theta)
denote the
p\times q
matrix such that
[\mathbf{D}(\theta)]_{aa}=\theta_{a}
for
a\leq\lceil q/2\rceil
and is zero otherwise.

For
i=0,1
, consider the prior distributions
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
such that, under
\mu_{i}
,
\mathbf{A}
is distributed as
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{D}(\theta)\mathbf{V}^{T}
where
\mathbf{U}
,
\mathbf{V}
, and
\theta
are independent and respectively sampled according to the Haar measures
\mathcal{U}(p)
and
\mathcal{U}(q)
and
\nu_{i}^{\otimes\lceil q/2\rceil}
. Note that, up to a permutation, the non-zero singular values of
\mathbf{A}
equal
\theta_{a}
for
a=1,\ldots,\lceil q/2\rceil
, which implies that
f_{\sigma}(\mathbf{A})=\sum_{a}f(|\theta_{a}|)
.

The measure
\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{0}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{1}}
are challenging to handle because the corresponding matrices
\mathbf{A}
have ranks
\lfloor q/2\rfloor
(if
\nu_{0}(\{0\})=0
) and involve intricate integrals with respect to the Haar measure. The following lemma reduces
\|\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{0}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{1}}\|_{TV}
to distance between distributions involving rank 1 matrices
\mathbf{A}
.

Define
q^{\prime}=q+1-\lceil q/2\rceil
,
p^{\prime}=p+1-\lceil q/2\rceil
. Let
\underline{\mu}_{0}
(resp.
\underline{\mu}_{1}
) denote the distribution on
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p^{\prime}\times q^{\prime}}
such that
\mathbf{A}=\sigma uv^{T}
where
\sigma\sim\nu_{0}
(resp.
\nu_{1}
),
u
and
v
are sampled uniformly on the unit sphere of
\mathbb{R}^{p^{\prime}}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q^{\prime}}
. Finally, we denote
\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{i}=\int\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{A}}\underline{\mu}_{i}(d\mathbf{A})
for
i=0,1
the marginal distributions of the noisy
p^{\prime}\times q^{\prime}
observations
\mathbf{Y}
when
\mathbf{A}\sim\underline{\mu}_{i}
.

Lemma 8.13
.

For any
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
, we have

\|\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{0}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{1}}\|_{TV}\leq\lceil q/2\rceil\|\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{0}-\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{1}\|_{TV}\ .

Intuitively,
\|\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{0}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{1}}\|_{TV}
quantifies the difficulty to decipher rank
\lceil q/2\rceil
matrices whose singular values are either i.i.d. distributed according to
\nu_{0}
or
\nu_{1}
, whereas
\|\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{0}-\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{1}\|_{TV}
quantifies the difficulty to decipher rank
1
matrices whose unique non-zero singular value is either distributed according to
\nu_{0}
or to
\nu_{1}
. Fortunately, the distance
\|\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{0}-\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{1}\|_{TV}
is now tractable and we are now in position to apply moment matching techniques
[
7
]
.

Define
k_{0}=k_{0}(\nu_{0},\nu_{1})
be the smallest integer such that, for all
0\leq k\leq k_{0}
,
\int x^{2k}\nu_{0}(dx)=\int x^{2k}\nu_{1}(dx)
. In other words, the
k_{0}
first even moments of
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
are matching. Note that
k_{0}\geq 0
as
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
are probability measures.

Lemma 8.14
.

If the supports of
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
are included in
[0,0.125(pq)^{1/4}]
and if the first
k_{0}
even moments of
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
are matching, then we have

\|\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{0}-\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{1}\|^{2}_{TV}\leq e^{-k_{0}}\ .

Define
k_{0}^{*}:=\lceil\log(4\lceil q/2\rceil)\rceil
. Provided that
k_{0}\geq k_{0}^{*}
, we then derive from Lemma
8.13
that

\|\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{0}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{1}}\|_{TV}\leq e^{-k_{0}}\lceil q/2\rceil\leq\frac{1}{4}\ .

(72)

Since the support of
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
are included in
[0,(pq)^{1/4}/8]
we deduce from Hoeffding inequality

\nu_{0}^{\otimes\lceil q/2\rceil}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil q/2\rceil}f(|\theta_{i}|)\geq\lceil q/2\rceil\int f(t)\nu_{0}(dx)+(\lceil q/2\rceil\log(2))^{1/2}\|f\|_{\infty,[0,(pq)^{1/4}/8]}\right]\leq\frac{1}{4}

A similar bound holds for the left deviations of
\sum_{i}f(\theta_{i})
under
\nu_{1}^{\otimes\lceil q/2\rceil}
. Using (
72
), we are now in position to apply Lemma
8.12
. This leads us to

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A};\,\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq 0.125(pq)^{1/4}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\big|\widehat{T}-f_{\sigma}(\mathbf{A})\big|\right]

(73)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{16}\left[\lceil q/2\rceil\left[\int f(t)\left(\nu_{1}(dt)-\nu_{0}(dt)\right)\right]-2(\lceil q/2\rceil\log(2))^{1/2}\|f\|_{\infty,[0,(pq)^{1/4}/8]}\right]_{+}\ ,

provided that the even moments up to
k_{0}^{*}
of
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
are matching. To conclude, it remains to consider
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
in such a way that
(a)
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
are supported in
[0,(pq)^{1/4}/8]
, (b) the first even moments up to
k_{0}^{*}
of
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
are matching, and (c)
\int f(t)(\nu_{1}(dt)-\nu_{0}(dt))
is the largest possible. Fortunately, such extremum problem is now well understood.
The following lemma is a straightforward extensions of Lemma 1 in
[
7
]
(see also Lemma 5.4 in
[
15
]
). The proof being the same as in Cai and Low
[
7
]
, we omit it.

Lemma 8.15
.

For any bounded interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, any continuous function
f
on
I
, any collection
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{q})
continuous functions on
I
with
g_{1}=1
,
there exist two probability measures
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
such that

1.

\int_{I}g_{l}(t)\nu_{0}(dt)=\int_{I}g_{l}(t)\nu_{1}(dt)\ ,\quad\text{ for all }l=1,\ldots,q

2.

\int_{I}f\nu_{1}(dt)-\int_{I}f\nu_{0}(dt)=2E_{G}[f;I]
,

where
G=\mathrm{Vect}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{q})
. Furthermore, it is not possible to built
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
satisfying (1) and
\int_{I}f\nu_{1}(dt)-\int_{I}f\nu_{0}(dt)>2E_{G}[f;I]
.

We apply this lemma with
G=\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{2k^{*}_{0}}
. Together with (
73
), this concludes the proof.

Proof of Lemma
8.14
.

Let
\phi
denote the density of the standard normal normal distribution. With a slight abuse of notation, we define
\phi(\mathbf{B})=(2\pi)^{-pq/2}e^{-\|\mathbf{B}\|_{2}^{2}/2}
for a
p^{\prime}\times q^{\prime}
matrix
\mathbf{B}
.
Relying on Fubini Theorem and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we get

\displaystyle\|\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{0}-\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{1}\|_{TV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\big|\int\phi(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{A})[\underline{\mu}_{0}(d\mathbf{A})-\underline{\mu}_{1}(d\mathbf{A})]\Big|d\mathbf{Y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\phi(\mathbf{Y})\big|\int e^{\langle\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{A}\rangle-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}/2}[\underline{\mu}_{0}(d\mathbf{A})-\underline{\mu}_{1}(d\mathbf{A})]\Big|d\mathbf{Y}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left[\int\left[\int e^{\langle\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{A}\rangle-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}/2}[\underline{\mu}_{0}(d\mathbf{A})-\underline{\mu}_{1}(d\mathbf{A})]\right]^{2}\phi(\mathbf{Y})d\mathbf{Y}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left[\sum_{j=0,1}\sum_{l=0,1}\int\left(\int\phi(\mathbf{Y})e^{\langle\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\rangle-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}/2-\|\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}/2}d\mathbf{Y}\right)(-1)^{j+l}\underline{\mu}_{j}(d\mathbf{A})\underline{\mu}_{l}(d\mathbf{A})\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left[\sum_{j=0,1}\sum_{l=0,1}(-1)^{j+l}e^{\langle\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\rangle}\underline{\mu}_{j}(d\mathbf{A})\underline{\mu}_{l}(d\mathbf{A})\right]^{1/2}\kern 5.0pt.

The inner product
\langle\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\rangle
decomposes as
\langle\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\rangle=\eta\eta^{\prime}(u^{T}u^{\prime})(v^{T}v^{\prime})
. Since
u
and
u^{\prime}
are independently sampled on the unit sphere,
(u^{T}u^{\prime})
is distributed as the first coordinate
u_{1}
. Similarly
v^{T}v^{\prime}
is distributed as the first coordinate
v_{1}
. Decomposing
e^{\langle A,A^{\prime}\rangle}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k!)^{-1}\eta^{k}\eta^{\prime k}(u^{T}u^{\prime})^{k}(v^{T}v^{\prime})^{k}
, this leads us to

\displaystyle\|\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{0}-\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{1}\|^{2}_{TV}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[u_{1}^{k}]\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[v_{1}^{k}]\left[\sum_{j=0,1}\sum_{l=0,1}(-1)^{j+l}\int\eta^{k}\nu_{j}(d\eta)\int\eta^{{}^{\prime}k}\nu_{l}(d\eta^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(2k)!}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[u_{1}^{2k}]\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[v_{1}^{2k}]\left(\int\eta^{2k}(\nu_{0}(d\eta)-\nu_{1}(d\eta))\right)^{2}\ ,

since the distributions of
u_{1}
and
v_{1}
are symmetric. Since the
k_{0}
first even moments of
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
are matching and since those measures are supported in
[0,0.125(pq)^{1/4}]
, this simplifies as

\|\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{0}-\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{1}\|^{2}_{TV}\leq\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[u_{1}^{2k}]\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[v_{1}^{2k}]}{(2k)!}\left(\frac{1}{8}\right)^{4k}(pq)^{k}\leq\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi k}}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[u_{1}^{2k}(ep)^{k}]}{2^{7k}k^{k}}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[v_{1}^{2k}(eq)^{k}]}{2^{7k}k^{k}}\ .

The random variable
u_{1}^{2}
is distributed as
X^{2}/[X^{2}+Z^{2}]
where
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
Z\sim\chi^{2}(p^{\prime}-1)
. As a consequence,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[u_{1}^{2k}]\leq\frac{4^{k}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[X^{2k}]}{(p^{\prime})^{k}}+\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}[X^{2}+Z^{2}\leq p^{\prime}/4]
. From Lemma
A.3
, we deduce that
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}[X^{2}+Z^{2}\leq p^{\prime}/4]\leq e^{-p^{\prime}/12}
, for any
p^{\prime}\geq 1
. Besides,

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[X^{2k}]=\frac{2^{k}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Gamma(k+1/2)\leq\frac{2^{k}}{\sqrt{\pi}}k!\leq e\sqrt{k}\left(\frac{2k}{e}\right)^{k}\ ,

where we used Stirling’s upper bound.
Coming back to
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[u_{1}^{2k}]
, we deduce from Stirling lower bound that

\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[u_{1}^{2k}(ep)^{k}]}{2^{7k}k^{k}}\leq e\sqrt{k}2^{-4k}\left(\frac{p}{p^{\prime}}\right)^{k}+2^{-2k}\left(\frac{pe}{2^{5}k}\right)^{k}e^{-p^{\prime}/12}\ .

By definition of
p^{\prime}
, we have
p^{\prime}\geq p/2
.
The function
x\mapsto(\tfrac{pe}{2^{6}x})^{x}
achieves its maximum for
x=p/2^{5}
. As a consequence, we derive that

\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[u_{1}^{2k}(ep)^{k}]}{2^{7k}k^{k}}\leq e\sqrt{k}2^{-3k}+2^{-2k}e^{p/32-p/24}\leq 2^{-2k}\left[1+e\sqrt{k}2^{-k}\right]\leq 2^{-2k}(e/2+1)

Arguing similarly for
v_{1}
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\|\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{0}-\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{1}\|^{2}_{TV}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{(e/2+1)^{2}}{\sqrt{4\pi k}}2^{-4k}\leq 3\cdot 2^{-4k_{0}}\leq e^{-k_{0}}\ .

∎

Proof of Lemma
8.13
.

In this proof, we interpolate the measures
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
by
\lceil q/2\rceil-1
intermediary measures.
For
j=1,\ldots,\lceil q/2\rceil-1
, define
\mu^{(j)}
the distribution on
\mathbf{A}
such that
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{D}(\theta)\mathbf{V}^{T}
where
\mathbf{U}\sim\mathcal{U}(p)
,
\mathbf{V}\sim\mathcal{U}(q)
, and
\theta\sim\nu_{0}^{\otimes(\lceil q/2\rceil-j)}\otimes\nu_{1}^{\otimes j}
, so that the distributions
\mu^{(j)}
and
\mu^{(j+1)}
only differ though the distribution of one entry. Denote
\mathbf{P}_{\mu^{(j)}}=\int\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{A}}\mu^{(j)}(d\mathbf{A})
the corresponding marginal distributions on
\mathbf{Y}
. Writing
\mu_{0}=\mu^{(0)}
and
\mu_{1}=\mu^{(\lceil q/2\rceil)}
, we derive from triangular inequality that

\|\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{0}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mu_{1}}\|_{TV}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\lceil q/2\rceil-1}\|\mathbf{P}_{\mu^{(j)}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mu^{(j+1)}}\|_{TV}\ .

We shall bound each of these total variation distance independently. Fix any integer
0\leq j\leq\lceil q/2\rceil-1
.

Define the set
S=\{1,\ldots,\lceil q/2\rceil-j-1,\lceil q/2\rceil-j+1,\ldots,\lceil q/2\rceil\}
. Denote
\mathbf{U}_{[S]}
, and
\mathbf{V}_{[S]}
the restrictions of
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
to its columns in
S
. If
\mathbf{U}
is sampled on the Haar measure, then conditionally to
\mathbf{U}_{[S]}
, the
\lceil q/2\rceil-j
-th column of
\mathbf{U}
is sampled uniformly on the intersection of the unit sphere of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
and the orthogonal subspace
U
of that induced by the columns of
\mathbf{U}_{[S]}
. The same Property holds for the
\lceil q/2\rceil-j
-th column of
\mathbf{V}
. We respectively write
u
and
v
for these two columns.

As a consequence, under
\mu^{(j)}
(resp.
\mu^{(j+1)}
),
\mathbf{A}
decomposes as

\mathbf{A}=\eta uv^{T}+\mathbf{U}_{[S]}^{T}\mathbf{D}(\theta_{-j})\mathbf{V}_{[S]}=:\eta uv^{T}+\mathbf{B}\ ,

(74)

where
\eta\sim\nu_{0}
(resp.
\nu_{1}
), conditionally to
\mathbf{U}_{[S]}
and
\mathbf{V}_{[S]}
and
\theta_{-j}\sim\nu_{0}^{\otimes\lceil q/2\rceil-j-1}\otimes\delta_{0}\otimes\nu^{\otimes j}
. Writing
\underline{\pi}
for the distribution of
(\mathbf{U}_{[S]},\mathbf{V}_{[S]},\mathbf{D}(\theta_{-j}))
and
\pi_{0}
(resp.
\pi_{1}
) for the conditional distribution of (
u
,
v
,
\eta
) given
(\mathbf{U}_{[S]},\mathbf{V}_{[S]},\mathbf{D}(\theta_{-j}))
where
\eta\sim\nu_{0}
(resp.
\nu_{1}
), we decompose the total variation distance as follows

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{\mu^{(j)}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mu^{(j+1)}}\|_{TV}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\Big|\int\phi(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{A})\mu^{(j)}(d\mathbf{A})-\int\phi(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{A})\mu^{(j+1)}(d\mathbf{A})\Big|d\mathbf{Y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\Big|\int\phi\left[\mathbf{Y}-\eta uv^{T}-\mathbf{B}\right]\pi_{0}(du,dv,d\eta)\underline{\pi}(\mathbf{U}_{[S]}^{T},\mathbf{V}_{[S]},\theta_{-j})

\displaystyle\quad-\int(\phi\left[\mathbf{Y}-\eta^{\prime}u^{\prime}v^{{}^{\prime}T}-\mathbf{B})\mathbf{V}_{[S]}\pi_{1}(du^{\prime},dv^{\prime},d\eta^{\prime}\right]\underline{\pi}(\mathbf{U}_{[S]}^{T},\mathbf{V}_{[S]},\theta_{-j})\Big|d\mathbf{Y}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int\left|\int\phi\left[\mathbf{Y}-\eta uv^{T}-\mathbf{B}\right]\pi_{0}(du,dv,d\eta)-\int\phi\left[\mathbf{Y}-\eta^{\prime}u^{\prime}v^{{}^{\prime}T}-\mathbf{B}\right]\pi_{1}(du^{\prime},dv^{\prime},d\eta^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\underline{\pi}(d\mathbf{U}_{[S]}^{T},d\mathbf{V}_{[S]},d\theta_{-j})d\mathbf{Y}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int\left|\int\phi\left[\mathbf{Y}-\eta uv^{T}\right]\pi_{0}(du,dv,d\eta)-\int\phi\left[\mathbf{Y}-\eta^{\prime}u^{\prime}v^{{}^{\prime}T}\right]\pi_{1}(du^{\prime},dv^{\prime},d\eta^{\prime})\right|d\mathbf{Y}\underline{\pi}(d\mathbf{U}_{[S]}^{T},d\mathbf{V}_{[S]},d\theta_{-j})\ ,

where in the last line we replaced
\mathbf{Y}
by
\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{B}
. The above integral does not depend on specific values of
U_{[S]}
and
V_{[S]}
since the normal distribution is invariant by orthogonal transformation.

Define
q^{\prime}=q+1-\lceil q/2\rceil
,
p^{\prime}=p+1-\lceil q/2\rceil
. Let
\underline{\mu}_{0}
(resp
\underline{\mu}_{1}
) denote the distribution on
\mathbf{A}
such that
\mathbf{A}=\sigma uv^{T}
where
\sigma\sim\nu_{0}
(resp.
\nu_{1}
),
u
and
v
are sampled uniformly on the unit sphere of
\mathbb{R}^{p^{\prime}}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q^{\prime}}
. Then, upon considering the integrated distribution
\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{i}=\int\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{A}}\underline{\mu}_{i}(d\mathbf{A})
for
i=0,1
, we have proved that

\|\mathbf{P}_{\mu^{(j)}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mu^{(j+1)}}\|_{TV}\leq\|\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{0}-\underline{\mathbf{P}}_{1}\|_{TV}\ .

The result follows.

∎

8.7
Proof of Corollary
4.10

We start with
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}^{s}
. First observe that the minimax estimation risk is non-decreasing in
q
. Indeed, for
q_{1}\leq q_{2}
one can extend each matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q_{1}}
in
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q_{2}}
by adding null column vectors. Since both
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
share the same non-zero singular values we have

\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q_{1}}:\ \|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq 0.125(pq_{1})^{1/4}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\big|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}^{s}\big|\right]\leq\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q_{2}}:\ \|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq 0.125(pq_{2})^{1/4}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\big|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}^{s}\big|\right]\ .

Besides, for
q=1
,
\mathbf{A}
has rank at most one and
\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}=\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{2}
. It then readily follows from the proof of Proposition
2.2
that, for all
q\leq p
,

\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\big|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}^{s}\big|\right]\geq cp^{s/4}

Thus, it suffices to prove (
21
) for
q
large enough. We apply Theorem
4.9
with the function
x\mapsto|x|^{s}
. Since for any
x
and
M>0
,
|Mx|^{s}=M^{s}|x|^{s}
, we have

E_{\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{2k^{*}}}(|x|^{s};I_{0}])=\left((pq)^{1/4}/8\right)^{s}E_{\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{2k^{*}}}\left(|x|^{s};[0;1]\right)
. Since this function is symmetric, it follows from Lemma
8.10
that
E_{\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{2k^{*}}}[|x|^{s};[0;1]]=E_{\mathcal{P}_{2k^{*}}}\left(|x|^{s};[-1;1]\right)
.

\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\ \|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq 0.125(pq)^{1/4}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\big|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}^{s}\big|\right]\geq c^{\prime}_{s}(pq)^{s/4}\left[qE_{\mathcal{P}_{2k^{*}}}[f;[0.1]]-q^{1/2}\right]_{+}\ .

Then, we deduce from Lemma
4.7
that, for
q
large enough,
E_{\mathcal{P}_{2k^{*}}}\left(|x|^{s};[-1;1]\right)\geq c/\log^{s}(q)
. The first result follows.

Let us prove the second result by contradiction. Assume that there exists an estimator
\widehat{T}
and a constant
c_{s}
such that

\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\ \|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq 0.125(pq)^{1/4}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\big|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}\big|\right]\leq c_{s}\frac{q^{1/s}(pq)^{1/4}}{\log^{s}(q)\vee 1}

Without loss of generality, we can also assume that
\widehat{T}\leq 0.125q^{1/s}(pq)^{1/4}
. Then, for any matrix
\mathbf{A}
with
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\leq 0.125(pq)^{1/4}
, we have

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\big|\widehat{T}^{s}-\|\mathbf{A}\|^{s}_{s}\big|\right]\leq s[q^{1/s}(pq)^{1/4}]^{s-1}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\big|\widehat{T}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{s}\big|\right]\leq c_{s}s\frac{q(pq)^{1/4}}{\log^{s}(q)}\ .

Hence, if
c_{s}
is take small enough, this contradicts the first result.

8.8
Proofs for Wasserstein estimation

We start with the key lemma in
[
21
]
that allows them to control the Wasserstein distance between the spectral measures.

Lemma 8.16
.

[
21
]
Consider two probability measures
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
supported on
[-1,1]
. Denote their first
k
moments

\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})
and
\beta=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k})
, respectively. Then,

W_{1}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\leq\frac{c}{k}+c^{\prime}3^{k}|\alpha-\beta|_{2}\ ,

where
c
and
c^{\prime}
are numerical constant.

It turns out that we can extend the above approximation bound by only considering the sequence of even moments.

Lemma 8.17
.

Consider two probability measures
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
supported on
[0,1]
. Denote their first
k

even
moments

\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})
and
\beta=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k})
, respectively. Then,

W_{1}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\leq\frac{c}{2k}+c^{\prime}3^{2k}|\alpha-\beta|_{2}\ ,

where
c
and
c^{\prime}
are the same numerical constants as in Lemma
8.16
.

.

Proof of Lemma
8.17
.

We start from Kantorovich-Rubinstein variational representation of the Wasserstein distance:

W_{1}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=\sup_{f:Lip(f)\leq 1}\int f(x)(\nu_{1}(dx)-\nu_{2}(dx))
, where
f
is supported on
[0,1]
and
Lip(f)\leq 1
means that
f
is a Lipschitz function with constant
1
. Let
\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{k}
denote the space of the symmetric polynomials of degree less or equal to
k
. As Kong and Valiant
[
21
]
, we approximate

\int f(x)(\nu_{1}(dx)-\nu_{2}(dx))dx
by
\int P(x)(\nu_{1}(dx)-\nu_{2}(dx))dx
where
P=\sum_{i=0}^{k}a_{k}x^{2k}\in\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{k}
, the twist being that we now consider symmetric polynomials.

\displaystyle\int f(x)(\nu_{1}(dx)-\nu_{2}(dx))dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int(f(x)-P(x))(\nu_{1}(dx)-\nu_{2}(dx))dx+\int P(x)(\nu_{1}(dx)-\nu_{2}(dx))dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\|f-P\|_{\infty}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}|a_{i}||\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i}|\ .

Coming back to the Wasserstein distance, we have

W_{1}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\leq\sup_{f:Lip(f)\leq 1}\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}^{sym}_{k}}2\|f-P\|_{\infty}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}|a_{i}||\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i}|\ .

(75)

Fix any such Lipschitz function
f
defined in
[0,1]
and consider the symmetric function
\overline{f}
on
[-1,1]
, such that
\overline{f}(x)=f(|x|)
. In the proof of Proposition 1 in
[
21
]
, Kong and Valiant show that there exists a polynomial
Q_{2k}(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{2k}c_{i}x^{i}

such that
\|Q-\overline{f}\|_{\infty}\leq c/(2k)
(
c
is the same universal contant as in Lemma
8.16
) and
(\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}^{2})^{1/2}\leq 3^{2k}/2
. Since
\overline{f}
is symmetric, the symmetric polynomial
Q^{sym}=\sum_{i=0}^{k}c_{2i}x^{2i}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|Q^{sym}_{2k}-\overline{f}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[-1,1]}\left|\frac{Q_{2k}(x)+Q_{2k}(-x)}{2}-\overline{f}(x)\right|=\sup_{x\in[-1,1]}\left|\frac{Q_{2k}(x)+Q_{2k}(-x)-\overline{f}(x)-\overline{f}(-x)}{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|Q-\overline{f}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{c}{2k}\ .

In particular, this implies that
\|Q-f\|_{\infty}\leq c/(2k)
. Coming back to (
75
), we have proved that

W_{1}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\leq\frac{c}{2k}+\frac{3^{2k}}{2}|\alpha-\beta|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

Proof of Theorem
5.2
.

The proof closely follows that of Theorem 2 in Kong and Valiant
[
21
]
. Define
\overline{m}
the vector of size
K
of even moments such that
\overline{m}_{k}=\frac{1}{q}\big[\frac{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}}{M(pq)^{1/4}}\big]^{2k}
, for
k=1,\ldots,K
. Since we assume that
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\leq M(pq)^{1/4}
, it follows from Proposition
2.1
and Theorem
3.3
that
