\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[|\overline{m}-\widehat{m}|_{1}\right]\leq\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}c^{k}\left[\frac{1}{M^{2}}+\left(\frac{q}{p}\right)^{1/4}\frac{1}{M}\right]\leq\frac{c^{K}}{q[M^{2}\wedge[(p/q)^{1/4}M]]}\ .

Consider a rounding
\mu_{0}
of the true measure
\mu_{\sigma(\mathbf{A})/[M(pq)^{1/4}]}
that is supported on the grid. By definition, the
2k
-th moments of
\mu_{0}
is at most
[1-(1-\zeta)^{2}k]
far from that of
\mu_{\sigma(\mathbf{A})/[M(pq)^{1/4}]}
. As a consequence, this distribution
\mu_{0}
is a feasible point of the linear program and its objective value is less or equal to
|\overline{m}-\widehat{m}|_{1}+2\zeta\sum_{k=1}^{K}k
.
As a consequence, the even moments vector
m^{+}
of the solution
p^{+}
of the linear program satisfies in expectation

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|m^{+}-\overline{m}|_{2}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[|m^{+}-\widehat{m}|_{1}]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\overline{m}-\widehat{m}|_{1}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{c^{K}}{q}\frac{1}{M^{2}\wedge[(p/q)^{1/4}M]}+c\zeta K^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\frac{c^{K}}{q}\frac{1}{M^{2}\wedge[(p/q)^{1/4}M]}\ ,

since
\zeta\leq 1/(M^{2}q)
. Denote
\mu^{+}
the measure associated to the vector
p^{+}
and
\mu^{+}_{disc}
the discrete distribution made of
q
Dirac which is derived from
p^{+}
after step
2
of the procedure. By triangular inequality, we have

W(\mu^{+}_{disc},\mu_{\sigma(\mathbf{A})})\leq W(\mu^{+},\mu_{\sigma(\mathbf{A})})+W(\mu^{+}_{disc},\mu^{+})
. Since
W(\mu^{+}_{disc},\mu^{+})\leq 1/q
, we deduce from Lemma
8.17
that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[W(\mu^{+}_{disc},\mu_{\sigma(\mathbf{A})})]\leq\frac{c}{K}+\frac{1}{q}+\frac{c^{\prime K}}{q}\frac{1}{M^{2}\wedge[(p/q)^{1/4}M]}\ .

From (
23
), we conclude that

\sum_{i=1}^{q}|\widehat{\sigma}_{i}-\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{A})|\leq qM(pq)^{1/4}\left[\frac{c}{K}+\frac{1}{q}+\frac{c^{\prime K}}{q}\frac{1}{M^{2}\wedge[(p/q)^{1/4}M]}\right]\ .

Plugging the value
K=\lfloor c_{1}\log(q)\rfloor
where
c_{1}
is chosen small enough so that
c^{\prime K}\leq\sqrt{q}
yields the desired result.

∎

8.9
Proof of Propositions
7.1
and
7.2

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

Recall that
\tr[\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}]=\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}+2\tr[\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}]+(\tr[\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}]-pq)
. The second expression follows a centered normal distribution with variance
4\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}
whereas the last expression follows a
\chi^{2}
distribution with
pq
degrees of freedom.
From Lemma
A.3
, we deduce that, with probability higher than
1-4e^{-t}
,

\big|\tr[\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}]-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}\big|\leq 2\sqrt{pqt}+2t+2\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}\sqrt{2t}\ .

Regarding the operator norm, we start from (
36
)

\big|\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{Y}^{T}\mathbf{Y}-p\mathbf{I}_{q})-\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}\big|\leq 2\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}+\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|_{\infty}\ .

From Lemma
A.2
, we deduce that, with probability higher than
1-e^{-t}
,
\|\mathbf{E}^{T}\mathbf{E}-p\mathbf{I}_{q}\|_{\infty}\leq q+2\sqrt{pq}+4\sqrt{2pt}+2t
. In the proof of Lemma
8.1
, we have shown that
\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}/\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}
is stochastically dominated by the operator norm of a
q\times q
matrix with independent normal entries. Invoking again Lemma
A.2
, we derive that
2\|\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{E}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})(2\sqrt{q}+\sqrt{2t})
with probability higher than
1-e^{-t}
. Putting everything together, this yields

\frac{1-[2\sqrt{pqt}+2t]/\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}-2\sqrt{2t}/\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}}{1+2\frac{2\sqrt{q}+\sqrt{2t}}{\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})}+\frac{3\sqrt{pq}+4\sqrt{2pt}+2t}{\sigma^{2}_{1}(\mathbf{A})}}\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}_{1}(\mathbf{A})}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}}\widehat{\mathrm{ER}}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})\leq\frac{1+[2\sqrt{pqt}+2t]/\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}+2\sqrt{2t}/\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}}{1-2\frac{2\sqrt{q}+\sqrt{2t}}{\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})}-\frac{3\sqrt{pq}+4\sqrt{2pt}+2t}{\sigma^{2}_{1}(\mathbf{A})}}\ ,

which, assuming that both
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}
and
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}
are large enough, implies that

\frac{\big|\widehat{\mathrm{ER}}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})-\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})\big|}{\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})}\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{pqt}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}}+\frac{\sqrt{pt}+\sqrt{pq}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2}_{\infty}}+\frac{\sqrt{q}+\sqrt{t}}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

Proof of Proposition
7.2
.

The first bound is a straightforward consequence of (
32
), so we focus on the minimax lower bound.
Consider any
r\geq(pq)^{1/4}
. We shall first prove that

\inf_{\widehat{R}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\geq r}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{|\widehat{R}-\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})\big|}{\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})}\geq c\frac{\sqrt{pq}}{r^{2}}\right]\geq 0.2\ .

(76)

Define the matrices
\mathbf{E}_{i,j}
by
(\mathbf{E}_{ij})_{kl}={\mathbf{1}}_{i=k}{\mathbf{1}}_{j=l}
. Fix
s\in(0,(pq)^{1/4})
. We consider the matrix
\mathbf{A}_{0}=r\mathbf{E}_{11}
and
the class
\mathcal{C}_{s}
of matrices of the form
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{0}+s\mathbf{B}
, where
\mathbf{B}
is a rank-one matrix whose only non-zero singular is equal to one and whose first row and first column are zero. Obviously, the effective rank of
\mathbf{A}_{0}
is
1
, whereas that of
\mathbf{A}
in
\mathcal{C}_{s}
is
1+s^{2}/r^{2}
. This allows us to reducing the problem of estimating the effective rank to that of testing whether
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{0}
versus
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{C}_{s}
.

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{R}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\geq r}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{|\widehat{R}-\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})\big|}{\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})}\geq\frac{s^{2}}{4r^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{T}\in\{0,1\}}\left[\max\left(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{A}_{0}}[\widehat{T}=1],\max_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{C}_{s}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{A}}[\widehat{T}=0]\right)\right]\ .

Since the first row and the first column of
\mathbf{Y}
are uninformative for this test, this reduces to the problem of testing whether a
(p-1)\times(q-1)
matrix
\mathbf{A}
is null versus
\mathbf{A}
is a rank-one matrix with non-zero singular value
s
. We rely again on Le Cam’s method of fuzzy hypotheses
[
37
]
. Considering again the same mixture distribution
\mu
as in the proof of Proposition
2.2
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{R}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\geq r}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{|\widehat{R}-\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})\big|}{\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})}\geq\frac{s^{2}}{4r^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1-\sqrt{\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0},\mathbf{P})}}{2}.

By Lemma
8.3
, the latter expression is larger than
0.2
if we take
s=[0.25(p-1)(q-1)]^{1/4}
. We proved (
76
).

Let us now prove that

\inf_{\widehat{R}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\geq r}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{|\widehat{R}-\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})\big|}{\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})}\geq c\frac{\sqrt{q}}{r}\right]\geq 0.2\ .

(77)

Without loss of generality, we restrict ourselves to square matrices of dimension
q
. Introduce
\mathbf{A}_{1}=r\mathbf{I}_{q}
and, for
s>0
,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{s}
the collection of matrices of the form
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{1}+suu^{T}
where
u
is a unit vector. The effective rank of
\mathbf{A}_{1}
is
q
, whereas that of matrices
\mathbf{A}
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
equals
q-(q-1)\frac{s^{2}+2sr}{(r+s)^{2}}
. As long as
r\geq 2s
and
q\geq 2
, the relative difference between these effective ranks is at least
\tfrac{4s}{9r}
. Arguing as above, we deduce that

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{R}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\geq r}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{|\widehat{R}-\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})\big|}{\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})}\geq\frac{2s}{9r}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{T}\in\{0,1\}}\left[\max\left(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{A}_{1}}[\widehat{T}=0],\max_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{s}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{A}}[\widehat{T}=0]\right)\right]\ ,

the latter quantity being equivalent to the optimal error of the test of the hypotheses “
\mathbf{A}=0
” versus “
\mathbf{A}=suu^{T}
for some unit vector
u
”.
Introduce the uniform distribution
\nu
on the hypercube
\{-1,1\}^{q}
and define
\mu
the corresponding distribution of
\mathbf{A}=s/qvv^{T}
when
v
is sampled according to
\nu
. Writing
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}
the marginal distribution of
Y
when
\mathbf{A}
is sampled according to
\mu
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{R}}\sup_{\mathbf{A}:\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})\geq r}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{|\widehat{R}-\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})\big|}{\mathrm{ER}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{A})}\geq\frac{2s}{9r}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1-\sqrt{\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0},\mathbf{P}^{\prime})}}{2}.

We claim that, for
s=\sqrt{q/16}
, we we have
\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0},\mathbf{P}^{\prime})\leq 1/3
. Before showing this claim, let us finish this proof. Since
r\geq(pq)^{1/4}\geq\sqrt{q}
, we have
r\geq 2s
and (
77
) follows from the previous inequality.

It remains to prove the claim. Recall that
\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0},\mathbf{P}^{\prime})=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[L^{2}]-1
, where
L
is the likelihood ratio.
Arguing as in the beginning of the proof of Lemma
8.3
, we deduce that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[L^{2}]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{s^{2}Z^{2}}{q^{2}}\right)\right]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{Z^{2}}{16q}\right)\right]\ ,

where
Z
is distributed as a sum of
q
independent Rademacher random variables. By Hoeffding’s inequality
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}[|Z|\geq u]\leq 2e^{-u^{2}/(2q)}
. Hence,

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[L^{2}]-1\leq\int_{1}^{\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{Z^{2}}{16q}\right)\geq t\right]dt\leq 2\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{8}}dt=\frac{2}{9}\leq\frac{1}{3}\ .

The result follows.
∎

9
Proofs for Sub-Gaussian noise

9.1
Proof of Theorem
6.3

Proof of Theorem
6.3
.

Recall that our estimator
U_{k}
is defined as

U_{k}=\sum_{i=i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}\sum_{j=j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}\prod_{r,s}H_{N_{rs}(ij)}(\mathbf{Y}_{rs})\kern 5.0pt,

so that its square risk satisfies

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(U_{k}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}\right)^{2}\right]=\sum_{i,j,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\underset{=T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}}{\underbrace{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\prod_{r,s}H_{N_{rs}(ij)}(\mathbf{Y}_{rs})-\prod_{rs}\mathbf{A}_{rs}^{N_{rs}(ij)}\right)\left(\prod_{r,s}H_{N_{rs}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})}(\mathbf{Y}_{rs})-\prod_{rs}\mathbf{A}_{rs}^{N_{rs}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})}\right)\right]}}\ .

(78)

The random variables
Y_{rs}
are independent. Besides, we have
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{1}(Y_{rs})]=\mathbf{A}_{rs}
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{2}(Y_{rs})]=\mathbf{A}_{rs}^{2}
since
\operatorname{Var}\left(E_{rs}\right)=1
. As a consequence,
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
is equal to zero if

\max_{rs}N_{rs}(ij)N_{rs}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})=0\quad\text{ and }\quad[\max_{rs}N_{rs}(ij)]\wedge[\max_{rs}N_{rs}(i^{\prime}j^{\prime})]\leq 2\kern 5.0pt.

Indeed, the first condition ensures that
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
is the expectation of a product of two independent random variables
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
whereas the second condition ensures that either
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{1}]=0
or
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{2}]=0
so that
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}=0
. In order to work out the expression of the non-zero
T_{iji^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
, we shall rely on the following lemma which control that expectation of
H_{k}(Y)
and of the products
H_{k}(Y)H_{l}(Y)
when
Y
is not normally distributed.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
Y=\theta+E
where
E
is a mean-zero random variable satisfying
\operatorname{Var}\left(E\right)=1
. There exists a universal constant
c>0
, such that the following holds. For any integer
k\geq 3
, we have

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{k}(Y)]=\theta^{k}+P_{k}(\theta)\ ,

where
P_{k}
is a polynomial of degree at most
k-3
and whose coefficients are, in absolute value, uniformly bounded by
(c\|E\|_{\psi_{2}})^{k}k^{k+1}
.
Let
k
and
l
be two non-negative integers satisfying
k+l\geq 2
. Then,

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{k}H_{l}(Y)]=\theta^{k+l}+Q_{k,l}(\theta)

where the degree of
Q_{k,l}
is at most
k+l-2
and whose coefficients are, in absolute value, uniformly bounded by
(c\|E\|_{\psi_{2}})^{k+l}(k+l)^{k+l+2}
. Besides, if
k+l=2=k\vee l
, then
Q_{k,l}=0
.

Given a partition
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{l}\}
of
[4k]
with
l
groups, we consider the function
F_{\mathcal{P}}
defined by

\displaystyle F_{\mathcal{P}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{r=1}^{l}\mathbb{E}\left[H_{|\mathcal{P}_{r}\cap[2k]|}(y_{r})H_{|\mathcal{P}_{r}\cap[2k+1:4k]|}(y_{r})\right]-\prod_{r=1}^{l}x_{r}^{|\mathcal{P}_{r}\cap[2k]|}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_{|\mathcal{P}_{r}\cap[2k+1:4k]|}(y_{r})\right]

\displaystyle-\prod_{r=1}^{l}x_{r}^{|\mathcal{P}_{r}\cap[2k+1:4k]|}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_{|\mathcal{P}_{r}\cap[2k]|}(y_{r})\right]+\prod_{r=1}^{l}x_{r}^{|\mathcal{P}_{r}|}\ ,

where, for
r=1,\ldots,l
,
y_{r}=x_{r}+\epsilon_{r}
and the
\epsilon_{r}
’s are independent and identically distributed.
By Lemma
9.1
above,
F_{\mathcal{P}}
is a polynomial of
l
variables.

\displaystyle F_{\mathcal{P}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{r:\ |\mathcal{P}_{r}|=1}x_{r}\left[\prod_{r:\ |\mathcal{P}_{r}|\geq 2}x_{r}^{|\mathcal{P}_{r}|}+\prod_{r:\ |\mathcal{P}_{r}|\geq 2}\left[x_{r}^{|\mathcal{P}_{r}|}+Q_{|\mathcal{P}_{r}\cap[2k]|,|\mathcal{P}_{r}\cap[2k+1:4k]|}(x_{k})\right]\right.

(79)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\prod_{r:\ |\mathcal{P}_{r}|\geq 2}\left[x_{r}^{|\mathcal{P}_{r}|}+x_{r}^{|\mathcal{P}_{r}\cap[2k]|}P_{|\mathcal{P}_{r}\cap[2k+1:4k]|}(x_{r})\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\left.-\prod_{r:\ |\mathcal{P}_{r}|\geq 2}\left[x_{r}^{|\mathcal{P}_{r}|}+x_{r}^{|\mathcal{P}_{r}\cap[2k+1:4k]|}P_{|\mathcal{P}_{r}\cap[2k]|}(x_{r})\right]\right]\ .

Given
m=(i,j,i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
, we define the following sequence
\underline{m}
of
2
-tuples of size
4k

\displaystyle\underline{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{1},j_{2}),\ldots,(i_{k},j_{1}),(i^{\prime}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,(i^{\prime}_{k},j^{\prime}_{1}))\ .

(80)

Then, we define
\mathcal{P}[m]
as the partition of
[4k]
that groups identical 2-tuples in
\underline{m}
.
Besides, we consider the two sequences
\underline{m}^{ev}
and
\underline{m}^{od}
defined by

\displaystyle\underline{m}^{ev}=(i_{1},i_{1},i_{2},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k},i_{k},i^{\prime}_{1},i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{k},i^{\prime}_{k})\ ;\,\,\,\underline{m}^{od}=(j_{1},j_{2},j_{2},\ldots,j_{k},j_{1},j^{\prime}_{1},j^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,j^{\prime}_{k},j^{\prime}_{1})\ .

Given a partition
\mathcal{P}
of
[4k]
into
l
groups, we pick representatives
s_{1},\ldots,s_{l}
for each group. Then,
for all
m
satisfying
\mathcal{P}[m]=\mathcal{P}
, we have

T_{m}=F_{\mathcal{P}}[\mathbf{A}_{\underline{m}_{s_{1}}},\ldots,\mathbf{A}_{\underline{m}_{s_{l}}}]\ .

For short, we write henceforth
F_{\mathcal{P}}[m]
for
F_{\mathcal{P}}[\mathbf{A}_{\underline{m}_{s_{1}}},\ldots,\mathbf{A}_{\underline{m}_{s_{l}}}]
.
Write
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{k}
for the collection of all partitions
\mathcal{P}
of
[4k]
. Equipped with this notation, we group the expressions
T_{m}
according to their partition
\mathcal{P}[m]
.

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(U_{k}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}\right)^{2}\right]=\sum_{\mathcal{P}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{k}}\quad\sum_{m:\ \mathcal{P}[m]=\mathcal{P}}F_{\mathcal{P}}(m)\ .

(81)

Unfortunately, it is not simple to enumerate the sequences such that
\mathcal{P}[m]=\mathcal{P}
and these sums
\sum_{m:\ \mathcal{P}[m]=\mathcal{P}}F_{\mathcal{P}}(m)
do not simplify much. For this reason, we prefer to reformulate the expressions using sums over sequences
m
such that
\mathcal{P}[m]
is coarser than
\mathcal{P}

For two partitions
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}
, we write
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\trianglelefteq\mathcal{P}
if
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is finer or equal to
\mathcal{P}
. First, we extend the definition of
F_{\mathcal{P}}(m)
to any sequence
m
whose partition
\mathcal{P}[m]
is coarser than
\mathcal{P}
, that is
\mathcal{P}\trianglelefteq\mathcal{P}[m]
. Then, we define a new functional
G_{\mathcal{P}}
for
\mathcal{P}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{k}
by recursion.
If
\mathcal{P}
is the finest partition (with
4k
groups), we fix
G_{\mathcal{P}}=F_{\mathcal{P}}=0
. Otherwise, we define

G_{\mathcal{P}}(m)=F_{\mathcal{P}}(m)-\sum_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{k}:\ \mathcal{P}^{\prime}\triangleleft\mathcal{P}}G_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}(m)\ ,

where
F_{\mathcal{P}}(m)
is defined in (
79
).
One then easily deduce from this definition that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(U_{k}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}\right)^{2}\right]=\sum_{\mathcal{P}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}_{k}}W_{\mathcal{P}}\quad\text{ where }\quad W_{\mathcal{P}}=\sum_{m:\ \mathcal{P}\trianglelefteq\mathcal{P}[m]}G_{\mathcal{P}}(m)\ .

(82)

In the remainder of the proof, we shall carefully control
W_{\mathcal{P}}
. To do this, we need to evaluate the functional
G_{\mathcal{P}}
, which is done using
Möbius inversion formula for posets (see the proof of the next lemma). Equipped with this formula, we first establish some structural properties of
G_{\mathcal{P}}
.

Lemma 9.2
.

G_{\mathcal{P}}
can be represented as a polynomial in
|\mathcal{P}|
variables that satisfies the two following properties:

(a)

if
\mathcal{P}
contains at least one group, say
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, of size
2
such that either
\mathcal{P}_{1}\subset[2k]
or
\mathcal{P}_{1}\subset[2k+1:4k]
, then
G_{\mathcal{P}}=0
.

(b)

For any
l=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{P}|
such that
|\mathcal{P}_{l}|\geq 2
, the degree of
\mathcal{P}
in
x_{l}
is at most
|\mathcal{P}_{l}|-3
if
\mathcal{P}_{l}\subset[2k]
or
\mathcal{P}_{l}\subset[2k+1:4k]
and at most
|\mathcal{P}_{l}|-2
otherwise.

Writing
\kappa
the number of groups of
\mathcal{P}
of size higher than one, we order the groups of the partition
\mathcal{P}
in such a way that the
\kappa
first groups are of size at least
2
.
We define
the collection
\mathrm{deg}(\mathcal{P})
of non-negative integer-valued vectors
D
of size
\kappa
satisfying the two following properties for all
1\leq r\leq\kappa
,

(a)

D_{r}\leq|\mathcal{P}_{r}|-2
if
|\mathcal{P}_{r}|\geq 2
;

(b)

D_{r}\leq|\mathcal{P}_{r}|-3
if
|\mathcal{P}_{r}|\geq 3
and
\mathcal{P}_{r}\subset[2k]
or
\mathcal{P}_{r}\subset[2k+1:4k]
.

Define the monomial,
G_{\mathcal{P},D}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{|\mathcal{P}|}]=\prod_{l=\kappa+1}^{|\mathcal{P}|}x_{l}\prod_{l=1}^{\kappa}x_{l}^{D_{l}}
for any
D\in\mathrm{deg}(\mathcal{P})
. By Lemma
9.2
, we deduce that there exists a collection of numbers
\alpha_{D,\mathcal{P}}
for
D\in\mathrm{deg}(\mathcal{P})
such that

G_{\mathcal{P}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{|\mathcal{P}|}]=\sum_{D\in\mathrm{deg}(\mathcal{P})}\alpha_{D,\mathcal{P}}G_{\mathcal{P},D}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{|\mathcal{P}|}]\ .

The next lemma provides an uniform bound for the coefficients
\alpha_{D,\mathcal{P}}
. It is based on Möbius inversion formula (as the previous lemma) together with Lemma
9.1
.

Lemma 9.3
.

For any partition
\mathcal{P}
, we have

\sum_{D\in\mathrm{deg}(\mathcal{P})}|\alpha_{D,\mathcal{P}}|\leq(c^{\prime}\|E\|_{\psi_{2}})^{4k}k^{12k+2}

where
c^{\prime}
is a numerical constant.

Now define the associated sums

W_{\mathcal{P},D}=\sum_{m:\ \mathcal{P}\trianglelefteq\mathcal{P}[m]}G_{\mathcal{P},D}[(\mathbf{A}_{\underline{m}_{s_{1}}},\ldots,\mathbf{A}_{\underline{m}_{s_{l}}})]\ ,

(83)

where we recall that
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{l})
are representatives of the partition
\mathcal{P}
.
In light of the last lemma, we deduce from (
82
)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(U_{k}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{D\in\mathrm{Deg}(\mathcal{P})}|\alpha_{D,\mathcal{P}}||W_{\mathcal{P},D}|

(84)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(c^{\prime\prime}\|E\|_{\psi_{2}})^{4k}k^{16k+2}\max_{\mathcal{P},\ D}|W_{\mathcal{P},D}|\kern 5.0pt.

Hence, it remains to upper bound
W_{\mathcal{P},D}
defined in (
83
) to conclude.

Consider any partition
\mathcal{P}
and any
D\in\mathrm{deg}(P)
such that
W_{\mathcal{P},D}\neq 0
. In particular,
\mathcal{P}
contains a least a group of size larger than one and
\mathcal{P}
does not contain any group of size
2
which is either included in
[2k]
or in
[2k+1,4k]
–see Lemma
9.2
.

Define
\mathcal{P}_{ev}
as the finest partition of
[4k]
that is coarser than
\mathcal{P}
and coarser than
\{\{1,2\},\{3,4\},\ldots,\{4k-1,4k\}\}
.
Similarly, we define
\mathcal{P}_{od}
the as the finest partition of
[4k]
that is coarser than
\mathcal{P}
and coarser than
\{\{2k,1\},\{2,3\},\ldots,\{2k-2,2k-1\},\{4k,2k+1\},\{2k+2,2k+1\},\ldots,\{4k-2,4k-1\}\}
. Denote
l_{1}
the number of groups of
\mathcal{P}_{ev}
and
l_{2}
the number of groups of
\mathcal{P}_{od}
. Equipped with this notation, we deduce that there are
p^{l_{1}}q^{l_{2}}
sequences
m=(i,j,i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
satisfying
\mathcal{P}\trianglelefteq\mathcal{P}[m]
.

We denote
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2}
the subcollection of
\mathcal{P}
made of groups of size at least
2
. By definition of
\kappa
above, we have
\kappa=|\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2}|
.
Finally, we define
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,ev}\subset\mathcal{P}_{ev}
the subcollection of
\mathcal{P}_{ev}
made of groups that intersect at least one group of
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2}
. We denote
\kappa_{1}\leq\kappa
the number of groups of
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,ev}
. Analogously, we define
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,od}
and we denote
\kappa_{2}\leq\kappa
the corresponding number of groups. In order to evaluate
W_{\mathcal{P},D}
, we first fix the values of
\underline{m}_{ev}
,
\underline{m}_{od}
on
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,ev}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,od}
respectively –recall the definitions of
\underline{m}_{ev}
and
\underline{m}_{od}
in (
9.1
).
Consider any
s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{\kappa_{1}})\in[p]^{\kappa_{1}}
and
t=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{\kappa_{2}})\in[q]^{\kappa_{2}}
, where
s_{r}
(resp.
t_{r}
) corresponds to the value of
(i_{ev},i^{\prime}_{ev})
(resp.
(j_{od},j^{\prime}_{od})
) in the
r
-th group of
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,ev}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,od})
. Besides denote
\lambda_{1}
(resp.
\lambda_{2}
) the number of groups of
\mathcal{P}_{1,ev}=\mathcal{P}_{ev}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,ev}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}_{1,od}=\mathcal{P}_{od}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,od}
).

By Definition (
83
) of
W_{\mathcal{P};D}
, there exist sequences
(h_{r})_{r=1,\ldots,\kappa}
in
[\kappa_{1}]^{\kappa}
and
(h^{\prime}_{r})_{r=1,\ldots,\kappa}
in
[\kappa_{2}]^{\kappa}
,
(h_{r})_{r=\kappa+1,\ldots,l}
in
[\kappa_{1}+\lambda_{1}]^{l-\kappa}
and
(h^{\prime}_{r})_{r=\kappa+1,\ldots,\lambda}
in
[\kappa_{2}+\lambda_{2}]^{l-\kappa}
such that

W_{\mathcal{P};D}=\sum_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{\kappa_{1}}=1}^{p}\sum_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{\kappa_{2}}=1}^{q}\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\kappa}{\mathbf{A}}_{s_{h_{r}},t_{h^{\prime}_{r}}}^{D_{r}}\right]\sum_{s_{\kappa_{1}+1},\ldots,s_{\kappa_{1}+\lambda_{1}}=1}^{p}\quad\sum_{t_{\kappa_{2}+1},\ldots,t_{\kappa_{2}+\lambda_{2}}=1}^{q}\,\prod_{r=\kappa+1}^{l}\mathbf{A}_{s_{h_{r}},t_{h^{\prime}_{r}}}\kern 5.0pt.

(85)

Besides, the sequence
(h_{r})_{r=\kappa+1,\ldots,l}
contains exactly twice the value
u
for all
u\in[\kappa_{1}+1,\kappa_{1}+\lambda_{1}]
. Similarly, the sequence
(h^{\prime}_{r})_{r=\kappa+1,\ldots,l}
contains exactly twice the value
u
for all
u\in[\kappa_{2}+1,\kappa_{2}+\lambda_{2}]
. The sequence
(h_{r})_{r=1,\ldots,\kappa}
contains at least once each value in
[\kappa_{1}]
and the sequence
(h^{\prime}_{r})_{r=1,\ldots,\kappa}
contains at least once each value in
[\kappa_{2}]
. Since the indices
s_{\kappa_{1}+1},\ldots,s_{\kappa_{1}+\lambda_{1}}
and
t_{\kappa_{2}+1},\ldots,t_{\kappa_{2}+\lambda_{2}}
occur exactly twice in the sum (
85
), we can express the corresponding sums in (
85
) as products of matrices
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{A}^{T}
. This leads us to the following expression for
W_{\mathcal{P};D}
(the proof is omitted).

Lemma 9.4
.

There exists a positive integer
z_{\mathcal{P}}
only depending on the partition
\mathcal{P}
such that

W_{\mathcal{P};D}=\sum_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{\kappa_{1}}=1}^{p}\sum_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{\kappa_{2}}=1}^{q}\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\kappa}{\mathbf{A}}_{s_{h_{r}},t_{h^{\prime}_{r}}}^{D_{r}}\right]\prod_{r=1}^{z_{\mathcal{P}}}\mathbf{B}^{(r)}_{f_{r},f^{\prime}_{r}}

(86)

where
\mathbf{B}^{(r)}=\mathbf{A}^{\omega_{r}}(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})^{\omega^{\prime}_{r}}(\mathbf{A}^{T})^{\omega^{\prime\prime}_{r}}
with
\omega_{r}
and
\omega^{\prime\prime}_{r}
in
\{0,1\}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{r}
is a non-negative integer. If
\omega_{r}=1
, we have
f_{r}=s_{v_{r}}
for some
1\leq v_{r}\leq\kappa_{1}
, otherwise we have
f_{r}=t_{v_{r}}
for some
1\leq v_{r}\leq\kappa_{2}
. If
\omega^{\prime\prime}_{r}=1
, we have
h^{\prime}_{r}=s_{v^{\prime}_{r}}
for some
1\leq v^{\prime}_{r}\leq l
, otherwise we have
h_{r}=t_{v^{\prime}_{r}}
for some
1\leq v_{r}\leq l^{\prime}
.

Now that we have expressed
W_{\mathcal{P};D}
as a sum indexed by the group in
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,ev}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,od}
in (
86
), we cannot simply express
W_{\mathcal{P};D}
as the trace of a matrix or even the product matrix traces. Indeed, the indices
s_{i}
or
t_{j}
may occur more than two times in (
86
). The next bound is the crux of the proof.

Proposition 9.5
.

Upon denoting
a=\sum_{r=1}^{\kappa}(|\mathcal{P}_{r}|-D_{r})
, we have

\big|W_{\mathcal{P};D}\big|\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a}p^{\lfloor a/4\rfloor}q^{\lceil a/4\rceil}&\text{ if $a$ is even}\\
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a}p^{(a-1)/4}q^{(a-1)/4}&\text{ if $a$ is odd}\end{array}\right.

We first show how to conclude the proof from this bound before establishing Proposition
9.5
.
Recall that
a=\sum_{r=1}^{\kappa}(|\mathcal{P}_{r}|-D_{r})
lies in
[2,4k]
. If
a\equiv 3[4]
, then Young’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a}p^{(a-1)/4}q^{(a-1)/4}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a-1}p^{(a-3)/4}q^{(a-3)/4}\left[\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{2}+qp\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a-1}p^{\lfloor(a+1)/4\rfloor}q^{\lceil(a+1)/4\rceil}+\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a+1}p^{\lfloor(a-1)/4\rfloor}q^{\lceil(a-1)/4\rceil}\ .

If
a\equiv 1[4]
, then Young’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a}p^{(a-1)/4}q^{(a-1)/4}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a-1}p^{(a-1)/4}q^{(a-1)/4}\left[\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{2}+1\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a-1}p^{\lfloor(a+1)/4\rfloor}q^{\lceil(a+1)/4\rceil}+\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a+1}p^{\lfloor(a-1)/4\rfloor}q^{\lceil(a-1)/4\rceil}\ .

This allows us to derive

\big|W_{\mathcal{P};D}|\leq\max_{s=1}^{2k}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-2s}p^{\lfloor s/2\rfloor}q^{\lceil s/2\rceil}\leq\max\left[\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-2}q,\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-4}pq,(pq)^{k}\right]

Then, together with (
84
), we conclude that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(U_{k}-\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2k}^{2k}\right)^{2}\right]\leq(c^{\prime\prime}\|E\|_{\psi_{2}})^{4k}k^{16k+2}\max\left[\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-2}q,\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-4}pq,(pq)^{k}\right]\ .

The result follows.

∎

9.2
Proof of Proposition
9.5

First, we factorize the expression the operator norm of
\mathbf{A}
in order to consider matrices with operator norm at most one.

W_{\mathcal{P};D}={\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-\sum_{r=1}^{\kappa}(|\mathcal{P}_{r}|-D_{r})}}\sum_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{\kappa_{1}}=1}^{p}\sum_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{\kappa_{2}}=1}^{q}\prod_{r=1}^{\kappa}\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{s_{h_{r}},t_{h^{\prime}_{r}}}^{D_{r}}\sum_{r=1}^{z_{\mathcal{P}}}\overline{\mathbf{B}}^{(r)}_{f_{r},f^{\prime}_{r}}\kern 5.0pt,

(87)

where
\overline{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{A}/\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}
and
\overline{\mathbf{B}}^{(r)}=\mathbf{B}^{(r)}/\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{\omega_{r}+\omega^{\prime}_{r}+\omega^{\prime\prime}_{r}}
. In order to bound the latter expression, we shall introduce a multigraph
G
and bound
W_{\mathcal{P},D}
relying on the topology of
G
.

Before explaining how we translate
(\mathcal{P},d)
into a multigraph, we introduce some notation for a multigraph
G=(V,E)
. Since there are possibly multiple edges between the same two vertices, the edges
e\in E
are defined as a
3
-tuple
(i,j,a)
where
i
and
j
are two vertices and
a
is a positive integer corresponding to the index of
e
among all the edges between
i
and
j
. Besides, we respectively denote
e_{1}=i
and
e_{2}=j
the vertices incident to
e
. Although the edges are directed, we shall most often ignore their direction and the degree of vertex
i
henceforth corresponds to the number of edges that are incident to
i
. Finally, each vertex of
G
is either labelled as type ’
r
’ (for row) or as type ’
c
’ (for column). We write
R\subset V
for the collection of vertices of type ’
r
’ and
C=V\setminus R
for collection of vertices of type ’
c
’. We write
E_{\mathrm{lo}}\subset E
for the collection of self-loops, that is edges satisfying
e_{1}=e_{2}
and
E_{0}=E\setminus E_{\mathrm{lo}}
for the collection of remaining edges.
In the sequel, the external degree
d_{i}
of a vertex
i\in V
is the number of edges
e\in E_{0}
that are incident to
i
. We also
d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}
for the number of self-loops of
i
.

A matrix
\mathbf{C}
is said to satisfy Condition
(\mathcal{B}_{0})
if its operator norm is at most one and if its rank is at most
q
. A real vector
b
is said to satisfy Condition
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{lo}})
if
|b|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
|b|_{1}\leq q
. Finally, a real vector
b
is said to satisfy the slightly weaker condition
(\mathcal{B}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}})
if
|b|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
|b|_{2}\leq\sqrt{q}

For a multigraph
G=(V,E)
, we consider a collection
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{B}^{(e)})_{e\in E_{0}}
of matrices and a collection
\beta=(\beta^{(e)})_{e\in E_{\mathrm{lo}}}
of vectors. The matrix
\mathbf{B}^{(e)}
has
p
(resp.
q
) rows if
e_{1}\in R
(resp.
e_{1}\in C
). Similarly,
\mathbf{B}^{(e)}
has
p
(resp.
q
) columns if
e_{2}\in R
(resp.
e_{2}\in C
). The size of
\beta^{(e)}
is
p
(resp.
q
) if
e_{1}=e_{2}\in R
(resp.
\in C
). Finally,
(G,\mathbf{B},\beta)
is said to satisfy Property
(\mathcal{A}_{0})
if:

(i)

all matrices
\mathbf{B}^{(e)}
with
e\in E_{0}
satisfy Condition
(\mathcal{B}_{0})
.

(ii)

all vectors
\beta^{(e)}
with
e\in E_{\mathrm{lo}}
satisfy Condition
(\mathcal{B}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}})
.

(iii)

all vectors
\beta^{(e)}
with
e\in E_{\mathrm{lo}}
where the self-loop
e
is incident to a vertex
i
such that
d_{i}\leq 1
satisfy Condition
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{lo}})
.

Given a multigraph
G
, a collection
\mathbf{B}
of matrices and a collection
\beta
of vectors, we define the quantity
W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}
by

W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}:=\sum_{(\omega_{i})_{i\in V}}\prod_{e\in E_{0}}\mathbf{B}^{(e)}_{\omega_{e_{1}},\omega_{e_{2}}}\prod_{e\in E_{\mathrm{lo}}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega_{e_{1}}}\ ,

(88)

where the sum runs over
\omega_{i}
runs from
1
to
p
if
i\in R
and from
1
to
q
is
i\in C
.

Denote
V_{r,0,0}
the subset of isolated vertices
i
in
G
that have a label
r
and satisfy
d_{i}=d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0
. Similarly,

V_{*,0,*}
stands for the collection of vertices
i
satisfying
d_{i}=0
, whereas
V_{r,1,*}
is the collection of vertices of type ’
r
’ that satisfy
d_{i}=1
. Finally,

V_{r,\geq 2,*}
is the collection of vertices of type ’
r
’ with
d_{i}\geq 2
.

Proposition 9.6
.

If
(G,\mathbf{B},\beta)
satisfies Property
(\mathcal{A}_{0})
, then

\big|W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}\big|\leq p^{|V_{r,0,0}|+|V_{r,1,0}|/2}q^{|V_{*,0,*}|-|V_{r,0,0}|+[|V|-|V_{r,1,0}|/2-|V_{*,0,*}|]/2]}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{(|V_{r,\geq 2,*}|-1)_{+}/2}\kern 5.0pt.

Let us now apply explicit how we can apply this result to the expression (
87
) of
W_{\mathcal{P};D}
. We build a multigraph with
\kappa_{1}
vertices of type ’r’ and
\kappa_{2}
vertices of type ’c’. For
r=1,\ldots,\kappa
, we add
D_{r}
edges between the vertex
h_{r}
of type ’r’ and the vertex
h^{\prime}_{r}
of type ’c’ and we associate the matrix
\overline{\mathbf{A}}
to this edge. Obviously, we have
\|\overline{\mathbf{A}}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\mathrm{Rank}(\mathbf{A})\leq q
so that
\overline{\mathbf{A}}
satisfies
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. For
r=1,\ldots,z_{\mathcal{P}}
, we also add an edge between the vertices
v_{r}
and
v^{\prime}_{r}
where
v_{r}
and
v^{\prime}_{r}
are defined in Lemma
9.4
. If
v_{r}\neq v^{\prime}_{r}
, we associate the matrix
\overline{\mathbf{B}}^{(r)}
which satisfies also
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. If
v_{r}=v^{\prime}_{r}
, then we associate the vector
(\mathbf{B}^{(r)}_{a,a})
to this self-loop. Since
\|\mathbf{B}^{(r)}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and its rank is at most
q
, one readily checks that it satisfies
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{lo}}
. We are therefore in position to apply Proposition
9.6
to
W_{\mathcal{P},D}
.

|W_{\mathcal{P},D}|\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a}p^{|V_{r,0,0}|+|V_{r,1,0}|/2}q^{|V_{*,0,*}|-|V_{r,0,0}|+[|V|-|V_{r,1,1}|-|V_{*,0,*}|]/2]}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{(|V_{r,\geq 2,*}|-1)_{+}/2}\kern 5.0pt,

where we recall that
a=\sum_{r=1}^{\kappa}(|\mathcal{P}_{r}|-D_{r})
. For
r=1,\ldots,\kappa
, define
a_{r}=|\mathcal{P}_{r}|-D_{r}
so that
a=\sum_{r=1}^{\kappa}a_{r}
. From Lemma
9.2
, we know that
a_{r}\geq 2
and that
a_{r}\geq 3
if
|\mathcal{P}_{r}|\subset[2r]
or
|\mathcal{P}_{r}|\subset[2r+1:4r]
.

Let us consider any vertex
i
of the
\kappa_{2}
vertices of type ’
c
’. The vertex
i
corresponds to a group
\mathcal{P}_{i,od}
of
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,od}
and we write
s_{i}
for the number of groups of
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2}
whose intersection with this group of
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,ev}
is non-empty. By definition of
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2,ev}
, we have
s_{i}\geq 1
and
\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa_{2}}s_{i}=\kappa
. If
s_{i}=1
, we denote
r_{i}
for the unique group
\mathcal{P}_{r_{i}}
which is included in
\mathcal{P}_{i,ev}
.

Lemma 9.7
.

Any node
i
of type ’
c
’ such that
s_{i}=1
and
a_{r_{i}}\leq 3
has an external degree
d_{i}
satisfying
d_{i}\geq 1
.

Similarly, we define for any vertex
i=1,\ldots,\kappa_{1}
of type ’
r
’ the group
\mathcal{P}_{i,ev}
, the number
s^{\prime}_{i}
of groups of
\mathcal{P}_{\geq 2}
that are included in
\mathcal{P}_{i,ev}
and, if
s^{\prime}_{i}=1
, the index
r^{\prime}_{i}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{r^{\prime}_{i}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{i,ev}
.

Lemma 9.8
.

Any node
i
of type ’
r
’ such that
s^{\prime}_{i}=1
and
a_{r^{\prime}_{i}}\leq 3
has an external degree
d_{i}
satisfying
d_{i}\geq 1
. Besides, if
s^{\prime}_{i}=1
and
a_{r^{\prime}_{i}}=2
, then
d_{i}\geq 2
.

Since
p\geq q
, we deduce that

|W_{\mathcal{P},D}|\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a}p^{T_{1}-T_{3}}q^{T_{2}+T_{3}}\ ,

where

T_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa_{1}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{s^{\prime}_{i}>1}+{\mathbf{1}}_{s^{\prime}_{i}=1}{\mathbf{1}}_{a_{r^{\prime}_{i}}\geq 4}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{1}}_{s^{\prime}_{i}=1}{\mathbf{1}}_{a_{r^{\prime}_{i}}\in[2,3]}\right]\kern 5.0pt;

T_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa_{2}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{s_{i}>1}+{\mathbf{1}}_{s_{i}=1}{\mathbf{1}}_{a_{r_{i}}\geq 4}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{1}}_{s_{i}=1}{\mathbf{1}}_{a_{r_{i}}\in[2,3]}\right]\ ,

and
T_{3}=\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathbf{1}}_{\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa_{1}}{\mathbf{1}}_{s^{\prime}_{i}=1}{\mathbf{1}}_{a_{r^{\prime}_{i}}=2}>0}
. Since
\sum_{r=1}^{\kappa}a_{r}=a
and
a_{r}\geq 2
, we derive that

T_{2}\leq\frac{1}{4}a-\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa_{2}}\frac{1}{2}(s_{i}-2)_{+}-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{r=1}^{\kappa}(a_{r}-2)_{+}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa_{2}}{\mathbf{1}}_{s_{i}=1}{\mathbf{1}}_{a_{r_{i}}\geq 4}

and similarly that

T_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4}a-\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa_{1}}\frac{1}{2}(s^{\prime}_{i}-2)_{+}-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{r=1}^{\kappa}(a_{r}-2)_{+}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa_{1}}{\mathbf{1}}_{s^{\prime}_{i}=1}{\mathbf{1}}_{a_{r^{\prime}_{i}}\geq 4}\ .

(89)

To finish, we consider four different cases depending on the value of
a
. If
a\equiv 0[4]
, then one readily checks that
T_{1}\leq a/4
and
T_{2}\leq a/4
which leads to

|W_{\mathcal{P},D}|\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a}p^{a/4}q^{a/4}
. If
a\equiv 1[2]
, then at least one
a_{r}
is odd and we deduce that
T_{1}\leq(a-1)/4
and
T_{2}\leq(a-1)/4
so that

|W_{\mathcal{P},D}|\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a}p^{(a-1)/4}q^{(a-1)/4}
. It remains to consider the case
a\equiv 2[4]
. We observe again that
T_{2}\leq a/4
so that
T_{2}+T_{3}\leq\lceil a/4\rceil
. If
T_{3}=1/2
, we can use the simple bound
T_{1}\leq a/4
so that
T_{1}-T_{3}\leq\lfloor a/4\rfloor
. If
T_{3}=0
, this implies that no index
i
satisfies
s^{\prime}_{i}=1
and
a_{r^{\prime}_{i}}=2
, and we claim that
T_{1}\leq\lfloor a/4\rfloor=(a-2)/4
which implies that
|W_{\mathcal{P},D}|\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}^{4k-a}p^{\lfloor a/4\rfloor}q^{\lceil a/4\rceil}
.

It remains to establish the claim.
Since we know that
T_{1}\leq a/4
, and that 4 times the rhs of (
89
) is an integer, it suffices to prove that the rhs of (
89
) is neither equal to
a/4
nor to
(a-1)/4
. First, this expression cannot be equal to
(a-1)/4
because this would imply that

2\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa_{1}}\frac{1}{2}(s^{\prime}_{i}-2)_{+}-\sum_{r=1}^{\kappa}(a_{r}-2)_{+}+2\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa_{1}}{\mathbf{1}}_{s^{\prime}_{i}=1}{\mathbf{1}}_{a_{r^{\prime}_{i}}\geq 4}=1\ ,

which in turn would imply that
\sum_{r=1}^{\kappa}(a_{r}-2)_{+}
is odd which contradicts that the fact that
a=\sum_{r=1}^{\kappa}a_{r}
is even (recall that
a_{r}\geq 2
). Next, the only way of having the rhs of (
89
) equal to
a/4
is that (i) all
s^{\prime}_{i}
are either equal to
1
or
2
, (ii) that, for all
i
such that
s^{\prime}_{i}=1
, we have
a_{r^{\prime}_{i}}=4
, and (iii) that all the remaining values of
a_{r}
are equal to
2
.
This implies that
a\equiv 0[4]
which contradicts the hypothesis
a\equiv 2[4]
.

Proof of Lemma
9.7
.

In order to have
d_{i}=0
, we need that
a_{r_{i}}=|\mathcal{P}_{r_{i}}|
, otherwise the graph contains at least
D_{r_{i}}=|\mathcal{P}_{r_{i}}|-a_{r_{i}}
external edges incident to
i
(see (
87
)). If
a_{r_{i}}=|\mathcal{P}_{r_{i}}|=2
, then this implies that
|\mathcal{P}_{r_{i}}\cap[2k]|=1
and
|\mathcal{P}_{r_{i}}\cap[2k+1:4k]|=1
, otherwise
W_{\mathcal{P},d}=0
(Lemma
9.2
). Hence, we have
|\mathcal{P}_{i,od}|=4
with
|\mathcal{P}_{i,od}\cap[2k]|=2
and
|\mathcal{P}_{i,od}\cap[2k+1;4k]|=2
. From the construction of the graph
G
, we deduce that
d_{i}=2
(see (
85
)).

Now assume that
a_{r_{i}}=|\mathcal{P}_{r_{i}}|=3
.Since
|\mathcal{P}_{i,od}|
is an even integer, and since the number of half edges incident to
i
in the graph is
|\mathcal{P}_{i,od}|-a_{r_{i}}
, it follows that the number of half edges is odd and at least one of these half edges is connected to a distinct vertex. Hence, we have
d_{i}\geq 1
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
9.8
.

The first claim is proved analogously to Lemma
9.7
. We focus on the second claim.
Also, we have already shown in the previous proof that
d_{i}=2
if
|a_{r^{\prime}_{i}}|=|\mathcal{P}_{r^{\prime}_{i}}|=2
. If
|\mathcal{P}_{r^{\prime}_{i}}|\geq 4
and
a_{r^{\prime}_{i}}=2
, then there are
D_{r_{i}}=|\mathcal{P}_{r_{i}}|-a_{r_{i}}\geq 2
external edges incident to
i
(see (
87
)). It remains to consider the case where
|\mathcal{P}_{r^{\prime}_{i}}|=3
and
a_{r^{\prime}_{i}}=2
. Then,
D_{r_{i}}=|\mathcal{P}_{r_{i}}|-a_{r_{i}}=1
and there is one external edge which is associated to
D_{r_{i}}
(see (
87
)). Besides,
|\mathcal{P}_{i,ev}|-|\mathcal{P}_{r^{\prime}_{i}}|
is odd and is the number of half-edges corresponding to the right-hand side term in (see (
87
)). Since this number of half-edges is odd, at least one them corresponds to an external edge. Hence, we have proved that
d_{i}\geq 2
.
∎

9.3
Proof of Proposition
9.6

The proof is based on an induction-like approach. We shall first iteratively prune the graph
G
by removing small external degree vertices and we will show that the functional
\big|W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}\big|
is not much larger than that of the pruned graph. We start with the following lemma that summarizes the pruning operations that we are able to consider.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
(G,\mathbf{B},\beta)
satisfying
(\mathcal{A}_{0})
and let
i\in V
be a vertex satisfying
d_{i}\leq 2
. If
d_{i}\leq 1
, we define
G^{(-i)}
as the subgraph induced by
V\setminus\{i\}
. If
d_{i}=2
and
i
has two distinct neighbors, then
G^{(-i)}
is defined as the subgraph induced by
V\setminus\{i\}
where we add an edge
e
connecting the two neighbors of
i
. If
d_{i}=2
and
i
is connected twice to the same neighbor, say
j
, then
G^{(-i)}
is defined as the subgraph induced by
V\setminus\{i\}
such that
j
is incident to a single self-loop.
Then, the following holds:

(a)

If
d_{i}=0
, then, for some
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
,
\beta^{\prime}
such that (
G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}
) satisfies
(\mathcal{A}_{0})
, we have

\big|W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}\big|\leq\big|W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})}\big|[q+(p-q){\mathbf{1}}_{i\in R}{\mathbf{1}}_{d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0}]\ .

(b)

If
d_{i}=2
, then, for some
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
,
\beta^{\prime}
such that (
G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}
) satisfies
(\mathcal{A}_{0})
, we have

W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}=W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\ .

(c)

If
d_{i}=1
and the unique neighbor of
j
satisfies
d_{j}\geq 3
, then, for some
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
,
\beta^{\prime}
such that (
G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}
) satisfies
(\mathcal{A}_{0})
, we have

|W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}|\leq\left|W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\right|\left[\sqrt{q}+[\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{q}]{\mathbf{1}}_{i\in R}{\mathbf{1}}_{d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

(d)

If
d_{i}=1
and the unique neighbor of
j
satisfies
d_{j}=1
, then, for some
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
,
\beta^{\prime}
such that (
G^{(-i,-j)},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}
) satisfies
(\mathcal{A}_{0})
, we have

\left|W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}\right|\leq\left|W_{G^{(-i,-j)},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\right|\left[(\sqrt{q}+[\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{q}]{\mathbf{1}}_{i\in R}{\mathbf{1}}_{d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0})(\sqrt{q}+[\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{q}]{\mathbf{1}}_{i\in R}{\mathbf{1}}_{d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0})\right]\kern 5.0pt.

In view of this lemma, we iteratively prune the graph
G
by removing vertex with external degree
0
(case (a) above), or a vertex with external degree 2 (case (b) above) if no vertex with external degree 0 is left, or a vertex with external degree 1(cases (c) and (d) above) if no vertices with external degrees
0
or
2
vertices are left. Then, the pruning process stops when the resulting graph
G^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
is either empty or when all its vertices have an external degree higher or equal to
3
. Below, we use the convention that
W_{G^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}=1
if
G^{\prime}
is empty

Denote
V_{r,0,0}\subset R
the subset of vertices
i
in
G
that have a label
r
and satisfy
d_{i}=d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0
. Define
V_{*,0,*}\subset V
the subset of vertices
i
in
G
satisfying
d_{i}=0
.
Denote
V_{r,1,0}\subset R
the subset of vertices
i
in
G
that have a label
r
and satisfy
d_{i}=1
and
d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0
. Denote
R^{\prime}=R\cap V^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}=C\cap V^{\prime}
the vertices of
G^{\prime}
of type
r
and
c
respectively. The following lemma is an application of Lemma
9.9
above and controls
|W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}|
in terms of
|W_{G^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}|
.

Lemma 9.10
.

We have
V^{\prime}\subset V\setminus(V_{*,0,*}\cup V_{r,1,0})
and

|W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}|\leq|W_{G^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}|p^{|V_{r,0,0}|+|V_{r,1,0}|/2}q^{|V_{*,0,*}|-|V_{r,0,0}||+[|V|-|V^{\prime}|-|V_{*,0,*}|-|V_{r,1,0}|]/2]}\kern 5.0pt,

(90)

where
(G^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
satisfy
(\mathcal{A}_{0})
.

It remains to control quantities of the form
|W_{G^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}|
for graphs
G^{\prime}
whose vertices have all an external degree at least
3
. This is slightly more tricky than the previous arguments. See the proof of the following dedicated result.

Proposition 9.11
.

Consider any multi-graph
G^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
whose vertices have an external degree at least
3
. Let
(\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
be any matrices and vectors such that
(G^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
satisfies Property
(\mathcal{A}_{0})
. Then, we have

W_{G^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\leq q^{|V^{\prime}|/2}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{(|R^{\prime}|-1)_{+}/2}\ ,

(91)

where we recall that
[x]_{+}=\max(x,0)
and
R^{\prime}\subset V^{\prime}
is the subset of vertices of type ’
r
’.

Gathering the two previous lemmas leads to

|W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}|\leq p^{|V_{r,0,0}|+|V_{r,1,0}|/2}q^{|V_{*,0,*}|-|V_{r,0,0}||+[|V|-|V_{*,0,*}|-|V_{r,1,0}|]/2]}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{(|R^{\prime}|-1)_{+}/2}\kern 5.0pt.

To conclude, we need to upper bound
|R^{\prime}|
. Since all vertices of with external degree
0
or
1
have been pruned in the above iterative algorithm, the vertex set
V^{\prime}
is only made of vertices of degree at least
2
. Hence,
R^{\prime}\subset V_{r,2,*}
, the collection of vertices of type ’
r
’ with external degree at least
2
. The result follows.

9.4
Proof of Lemmas
9.9
and
9.10

Proof of Lemma
9.10
.

Let us explain how we obtain (
90
). As we first prune all vertices with external degree
0
, it follows from Cases (a) and (b) in Lemma
9.9
that

|W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}|\leq|W_{G^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}}|p^{|V_{r,0,0}|}q^{|V_{*,0,*}|-|V_{r,0,0}|}\kern 5.0pt,

(92)

where
G^{\prime\prime}
is subgraph of
G
induced by
V\setminus V_{*,0,*}
and
(G^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime})
satisfies
(\mathcal{A}_{0})
. Then, we observe that no pruning step in Lemma
9.9
increases the external degree of any vertex. Therefore, at some point, the procedure prunes all the vertices
i
satisfying
d_{i}=1
in the original graph
G
.

If, at some point of the pruning procedure, we have decreased the degree
d_{j}
of a vertex to
0
, then this implies that, in the previous step, we have pruned its unique neighbor, which was therefore an external degree
2
vertex (case (b) in Lemma
9.9
), so that the vertex
j
must have now a self-loop. When we prune this vertex
j
with external degree
0
, we pay a total price of
q
for having pruned two vertices (cases (a) and (b) in Lemma
9.9
).

If, at some point of the pruning procedure, we have decreased the degree
d_{j}
of a vertex to
1
, then this vertex must now contain a self-loop. Indeed, the external degree of
j
was at least
2
in the previous step. It is not possible that it was equal to
2
, because this would imply that we have pruned an external degree one vertex before an external degree 2 vertex. Similarly, it is not possible that the external degree of
j
was larger than
3
, otherwise its pruned neighbor would have been of external degree at least
3
. Finally, if the external degree of
j
was equal to
3
, than its pruned neighbor
i
was satisfying
d_{i}=2
and pruning
i
creates a self-loop in
j
(see case (b) in Lemma
9.9
). Since
j
has a self-loop, it follows from cases (c) and (d) in Lemma
9.9
), we pay a price
\sqrt{q}
when we prune
j
.

In summary, in the pruning process, all vertices with external degree at most
2
have been pruned. We pay a price
p
only when pruning vertices
i\in R
with
d_{i}=d_{i,lo}=0
in the original graph
G
. We pay a price
\sqrt{p}
only when pruning vertices
i\in R
with
d_{i}=1
and
d_{i,lo}=0
in the original graph
G
. We pay a price
q
only for vertices satisfying
d_{i}=0
and
i\in C
or
d_{i,lo}>0
in the original graph
G
. Otherwise, we pay a
\sqrt{q}
price for all the other pruned vertices. The result follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
9.9
.

We start with a straightforward property that will be used several times through the manuscript.

Lemma 9.12
.

If a matrix
\mathbf{B}
satisfies
(\mathcal{B}_{0})
, then
\|\mathbf{B}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{q}
.

Proof of lemma
9.12
.

Indeed,
\|\mathbf{B}\|^{2}_{2}=\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{B})\leq\sigma_{1}^{2}(\mathbf{B})q=q
because
\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\infty}=1
and the rank of
\mathbf{B}
is at most
q
.
∎

If
(G,\mathbf{B},\beta)
satisfies
(\mathcal{A}_{0})
and if
G
contains a vertex
i
with external degree
d_{i}=0
. Then, we consider
G^{(-i)}
as the graph induced by
V\setminus\{i\}
,
\mathbf{B}^{(-i)}=\mathbf{B}
, and
\beta^{(-i)}
as the restriction of
\beta
to self-loops that not incident to
i
.
We start from the decomposition

W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}=W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)},\beta^{(-i)}}\sum_{\omega_{i}}\prod_{e\in E_{lo,i}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega_{i}}\ ,

where
E_{lo,i}
is the subset of self-loops that are incident to
i
and where we use the convention
\prod_{e\in\emptyset}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega_{i}}=1
. Assume first that
E_{lo,i}=\emptyset
, then the above sum over
\omega_{i}
equals
p
if
i\in R
and
q
if
i\in C
. If
E_{lo,i}\neq\emptyset
, then one deduces from Condition (
\mathcal{A}_{0}
) that the vector
\gamma=(\prod_{e\in E_{lo,i}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega_{i}})
satisfies
|\gamma|_{1}\leq q
, which yields

\big|W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}\big|\leq\big|W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)},\beta^{(-i)}}\big|[q+(p-q){\mathbf{1}}_{i\in R}{\mathbf{1}}_{d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0}]\ .

(93)

If
G
contains a vertex
i
with external degree
d_{i}=2
, then we define

G^{(-i)}
as the subgraph
G
induced by
V\setminus\{i\}
where we have added a new edge
e^{\prime}
between the neighbors of
i
. If these neighbors, say
j
and
k
, are distinct, then the new matrix
\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}
is defined by

\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}=\sum_{\omega_{i}}\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,1)}_{\omega,\omega_{i}}\mathbf{B}^{(k,i,1)}_{\omega_{i},\omega^{\prime}}\prod_{e\in E_{lo,i}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega_{i}}\ .

This matrix
\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}
satisfies
(\mathcal{B}_{0})
. Indeed,
\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}
is a product of the matrices
\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,1)}
and
\mathbf{B}^{(k,i,1)}
and of
|E_{lo,i}|
diagonal matrices with diagonal entries given by the
\beta^{(e)}
. By Conditions
(\mathcal{B}_{0})
and
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{lo}}^{-})
, the operator norm of all these matrices is smaller or equal to one, whereas the rank of the two first matrices in the product is smaller or equal to
q
. Hence,
\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}
satisfies
(\mathcal{B}_{0})
. We have

W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}=W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)},\beta^{(-i)}}
, where
\mathbf{B}^{(-i)}
is the collection
\mathbf{B}
of matrices where we have removed
\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,1)}
and
\mathbf{B}^{(k,i,1)}
and added
\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}
and where
\beta^{(-i)}
is the collection
\beta
of vectors where we have removed all vectors corresponding to self-loops incident to
i
. Since the external degrees of the vertices
j
and
k
are left unchanged, this implies that
(G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)},\beta^{(-i)})
satisfies (
\mathcal{A}_{0}
).

If
i
has a unique neighbor, say
j
, then the new edge
e^{\prime}
is a self-loop incident to
j
. The new vector
\beta^{(e^{\prime})}
is defined by

\beta^{(e^{\prime})}_{\omega}=\sum_{\omega_{i}}\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,1)}_{\omega,\omega_{i}}\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,2)}_{\omega,\omega_{i}}\prod_{e\in E_{lo,i}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega_{i}}\ .

Applying Young’s inequality and relying on the fact that the Frobenius norms of
\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,1)}
and
\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,2)}
are at most
\sqrt{q}
(Lemma
9.12
), we derive that
|\beta^{(e^{\prime})}|_{1}\leq q
. Besides,
|\beta^{(e^{\prime})}|_{\infty}\leq\|\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,1)}\|_{\infty}\|\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,2)}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. As a consequence,
\beta^{(e^{\prime})}
satisfies
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{lo}})
. If the vertex
j
had no previous self-loop, we consider
\mathbf{B}^{(-i)}
as the collection
\mathbf{B}
of matrices where we have removed
\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,1)}
and
\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,2)}
. Besides, we consider
\beta^{(-i)}
as the collection
\beta
of vectors where we have removed all vectors corresponding to self-loops incident to
i
and we have added
\beta^{(e^{\prime})}
. Hence,
(G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)},\beta^{(-i)})
satisfies (
\mathcal{A}_{0}
) and we have
W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}=W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)},\beta^{(-i)}}
. It remains to consider the case where
j
had already at least one self-loop. We cannot simply use
(G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)},\beta^{(-i)})
as before because the external degree of
j
is possibly smaller than one while some of the vectors
\beta^{(e)}
associated to self-loops incident to
j
do not necessarily satisfy (
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{lo}}
). To resolve this small issue, we merge all the self-loops of
j
together and replace them by a single self-loop, say
e^{\prime\prime}
whose associated vector
\beta^{(e^{\prime\prime})}
is the coordinate-wise product of the vectors, i.e.
\beta^{(e^{\prime\prime})}_{\omega}=\beta^{(e^{\prime})}_{\omega}\prod_{e\in E_{l_{0},j}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega}
. One easily checks that
\beta^{(e^{\prime\prime})}
satisfied (
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{lo}}
). The result follows.

Finally, we consider the case where
G
contains a vertex
i
with external degree
d_{i}=1
and we denote
j
its unique neighbor.

•

If
d_{j}=1
. Then, we have

|W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}|\leq\left|W_{G^{(-i,-j)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i,-j)},\beta^{(-i,-j)}}\right|\left[(\sqrt{q}+[\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{q}]{\mathbf{1}}_{i\in R}{\mathbf{1}}_{d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0})(\sqrt{q}+[\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{q}]{\mathbf{1}}_{i\in R}{\mathbf{1}}_{d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0})\right]\kern 5.0pt,

where
G^{(-i,-j)}
is the subgraph induced by
V\setminus\{i,j\}
.
Indeed, denoting
e^{\prime}
the unique edges between
i
and
j
, we have the decomposition

W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}=W_{G^{(-i,-j)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i,-j)},\beta^{(-i,-j)}}\sum_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}\prod_{e\in E_{lo,i}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega}\prod_{e\in E_{lo,j}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega^{\prime}}\ ,

and we only have to bound this sum over
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
. Since
\|\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, we have

\left|\sum_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}\prod_{e\in E_{lo,i}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega}\prod_{e\in E_{lo,j}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega^{\prime}}\right|\leq\Bigg[\underset{=A}{\underbrace{\sum_{\omega}\left(\prod_{e\in E_{lo,i}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega}\right)^{2}}}\Bigg]^{1/2}\Bigg[\sum_{\omega^{\prime}}\left(\prod_{e\in E_{lo,j}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\Bigg]^{1/2}\ .

By symmetry, we only need to consider the sum over
\omega
. If
d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0
, then we see that
A=q
if
i\in C
and
A=p
if
i\in R
. If
d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}>0
, then, we
deduce from Condition
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{lo}})
that

A\leq\sum_{\omega}\left|\prod_{e\in E_{lo,i}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega}\right|\leq q\kern 5.0pt,

which concludes the proof.

•

If
d_{j}\geq 3
then we consider the subgraph
G^{(-i)}
induced by
V\setminus\{i\}
where we have added a self-loop
e^{\prime}
incident to
j
. We have

W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}=\sum_{(\omega_{l})_{l}\in V\setminus\{i,j\}}\sum_{\omega_{j}}\prod_{e\in E_{0}^{(-i)}}\mathbf{B}^{(e)}_{\omega_{e_{1}},\omega_{e_{2}}}\prod_{e\in E_{\mathrm{lo}}^{(-i)}}\beta^{e}_{\omega_{e_{1}}}\left[\sum_{\omega_{i}}\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,1)}_{\omega_{j},\omega_{i}}\prod_{e\in E_{lo,i}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega_{j}}\right]\ ,

where
E_{\mathrm{lo}}^{(-i)}
is the set of self-loops that are not incident to
i
and
E_{0}^{(-i)}
is the subset of edges not incident to
i
.
Define the vector
\beta^{e^{\prime}}
by

\beta^{e^{\prime}}_{\omega}=\left[\sqrt{q}+[\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{q}]{\mathbf{1}}_{i\in R}{\mathbf{1}}_{d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0}\right]^{-1}\sum_{\omega_{i}}\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,1)}_{\omega,\omega_{i}}\prod_{e\in E_{lo,i}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega_{j}}\ .

Obviously we have

|W_{G,\mathbf{B},\beta}|\leq\left|W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)},\beta^{(-i)}}\right|\left[\sqrt{q}+[\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{q}]{\mathbf{1}}_{i\in R}{\mathbf{1}}_{d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0}\right]\kern 5.0pt,

Since the external degree of
j
is still larger or equal to
2
after the pruning step, we only have to prove that
\beta^{(e^{\prime})}
satisfies
(\mathcal{B}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}})
.
Indeed, define the vectors
\gamma
and
\delta
by

\gamma_{\omega}=\sum_{\omega_{i}}\mathbf{B}^{(j,i,1)}_{\omega,\omega_{i}}\prod_{e\in E_{lo,i}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega_{i}}\ ;\quad\delta_{\omega}=\prod_{e\in E_{lo,i}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega}.

Since the
\beta^{(e)}
’s satisfy
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{lo}}
(as
d_{i}=1
), we have
|\delta|_{\infty}=1
and
|\delta|_{1}=\sqrt{q}+[\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{q}]{\mathbf{1}}_{i\in R}{\mathbf{1}}_{d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0}
. Since
\mathbf{B}^{(i,j,1)}
satisfies (
\mathcal{B}_{0}
), it follows that the vector
\gamma
satisfies

|\gamma|_{2}=|\mathbf{B}^{(i,j,1)}\delta|_{2}\leq\|\mathbf{B}^{(i,j,1)}\|_{\infty}|\delta|_{2}\leq\sqrt{q}+[\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{q}]{\mathbf{1}}_{i\in R}{\mathbf{1}}_{d_{i,\mathrm{lo}}=0}\ .

This implies that the
|\beta^{(e^{\prime})}|_{2}\leq 1
and
\beta^{(e^{\prime})}
therefore satisfies
(\mathcal{B}^{-}_{\mathrm{lo}})
.

∎

9.5
Proof of Proposition
9.11

Proof of Proposition
9.11
.

We shall establish the desired bound under different conditions and then show how to transform
(G^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
to satisfy these new conditions. A multigraph
G=(V,E)
is said to satisfy Property
(\mathcal{G}^{*})
if:

(a)

G
does not contain any self-loop.

(b)

Any pair of vertices is connected by at most 2 edges.

(c)

The minimum degree is larger or equal to
2
.

(d)

If the degree of a vertex is
2
, the this vertex has only one neighbor. In other words, this vertex shares two edges with another vertex.

A matrix
\mathbf{C}
is said to satisfy Condition
(\mathcal{B}^{*})
if (a) the entries of
\mathbf{C}
are non-negative, if (b) the
l_{2}
norm of each row and each column of
\mathbf{C}
is at most one, and if (c) the Frobenius norm of
\mathbf{C}
is at most
\sqrt{q}
.

Finally, we say that
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{B})
where
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{B}^{(e)})
is a collection of matrices indexed by the edges of
G
satisfies Property
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
is
G
satisfies
(\mathcal{G}^{*})
and all matrices
\mathbf{B}^{(e)}
satisfy
(\mathcal{B}^{*})
.

Proposition 9.13
.

For any
(G,\mathbf{B})
satisfying Condition (
\mathcal{A}^{*}
), we have

W_{G,\mathbf{B}}\leq q^{|V|/2}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{(|R|-1)_{+}/2}\ ,

(94)

where we recall that
[x]_{+}=\max(x,0)
and
R\subset V
is the subset of vertices of type ’
r
’.

Let us show how to apply this result to
W_{G^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
. First, we transform
G^{\prime}
into a multigraph
G^{\uparrow}
by erasing the self-loops of
G^{\prime}
. Recall that the external degree of each vertex in
G^{\prime}
is at least
3
. For any vertex
i\in V
, we pick an external edge
e=(k,i,a)
incident to
i
and we modify the corresponding matrix
(\mathbf{B}^{\prime})^{(e)}
into
(\mathbf{B}^{\uparrow})^{(e)}
by setting
(\mathbf{B}^{\uparrow})^{(e)}_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}=(\mathbf{B}^{\prime})^{(e)}_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}\prod_{e\in E_{l_{0},i}}\beta^{(e)}_{\omega}
. One checks that the resulting matrix
(\mathbf{B}^{\uparrow})^{(e)}
still satisfies
(\mathcal{B}_{0})
. For all the other external edges
e
, we set

(\mathbf{B}^{\uparrow})^{(e)}=\mathbf{B}^{(e)}
.
As a consequence, the resulting multigraph
G^{\uparrow}=(V,E^{\uparrow})
is such that
(G^{\uparrow},\mathbf{B}^{\uparrow})
satisfies
(\mathcal{A}_{0})
and

W_{G^{\uparrow},\mathbf{B}^{\uparrow}}=W_{G^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
. Next, we modify
G^{\uparrow}
by erasing some edges in such a way that any pair of vertices is connected by at most twice edges. We call
G^{\ddagger}
the resulting multigraph. Since the degree of any vertex in

G^{\uparrow}
is larger or equal to
3
, one easily checks that
G^{\ddagger}
satisfies Condition (
\mathcal{G}^{*}
). Then, we define the collection of matrix
\mathbf{B}^{\ddagger}
as follows. For any pair of vertices that are connected by at most 2 edges, we simply define
(\mathbf{B}^{\ddagger})^{(e)}
by

(\mathbf{B}^{\ddagger})^{(e)}_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}=\big|(\mathbf{B}^{\uparrow})^{(e)}_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}\big|
for the corresponding edges. If there are more than
2
edges, then the first matrix
(\mathbf{B}^{\ddagger})^{(e)}
is defined as above, whereas the second matrix
(\mathbf{B}^{\ddagger})^{(e)}
corresponding to the second edge
is defined by the product of the absolute values of the entries of the remaining matrices
(\mathbf{B}^{\uparrow})^{(e^{\prime})}
. Equipped with these notation, we straightforwardly have

\big|W_{G^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\big|=\big|W_{G^{\uparrow},\mathbf{B}^{\uparrow}}\big|\leq W_{G^{\ddagger},\mathbf{B}^{\ddagger}}\ .

(95)

It remains to show that the matrices
(\mathbf{B}^{\ddagger})^{e}
all satisfy Condition
(\mathcal{B}^{*})
. Since the maximum entry (in absolute value value) of all matrices
(\mathbf{B}^{\uparrow})^{e}
is at most one because
\|(\mathbf{B}^{\uparrow})^{e}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, it suffices to establish the following claim: if a matrix
\mathbf{C}
satisfies Condition
(\mathcal{B}_{0})
, then the matrix
\overline{\mathbf{C}}
defined by
\overline{\mathbf{C}}_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}=|\mathbf{C}_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}|
satisfies
(\mathcal{B}^{*})
. Indeed, it follows from Lemma
9.12
that
\|\overline{\mathbf{C}}\|_{2}=\|\mathbf{C}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{q}
. Besides, the
l_{2}
norm of each row and each column of
\overline{\mathbf{C}}
is upper bounded by the operator norm of
\mathbf{C}
and is therefore smaller than one. The claim follows. Since
(G^{\ddagger},\mathbf{B}^{\ddagger})
satisfies
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
, we are in position to apply Proposition
9.13
and the result follows from (
95
).

∎

Before turning to the proof Proposition
9.13
, we state a simple result that will be used several times.

Lemma 9.14
.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{1}
and
\mathbf{C}_{2}
be any two
r\times s
and
s\times t
matrices that satisfy Property
(\mathcal{B}^{*})
. Then the product matrix
q^{-1/2}\mathbf{C}_{1}\mathbf{C}_{2}
also satisfies Property
(\mathcal{B}^{*})
.

Proof of Proposition
9.13
.

We prove this result by induction on the number
|V|
of vertices. We start by considering any pair
(G,\mathbf{B})
with
|V|=2
vertices that satisfies
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
. Since the two vertices are connected by exactly two edges, we derive from Young’s inequality that

\displaystyle W_{G,\mathbf{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}=1}^{p}\prod_{e\in E}\mathbf{B}^{(e)}_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{e\in E}\sum_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}=1}^{p}(\mathbf{B}^{(e)}_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}})^{2}\leq q\leq q^{|V|/2}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{(|R|-1)_{+}/2}\ ,

where we used that the Frobenius norms of the matrices
\mathbf{B}^{(e)}
is at most
\sqrt{q}
by Condition (
\mathcal{B}
).

(a)
Case 1.a

(b)
Case 1.b.i

(c)
Case 1.b.ii

(d)
Case 3

Fig 2:
Examples of pruned graphs in the proof of Proposition
9.13
.
Vertices and edges in red are removed, whereas edges in dotted green are inserted.

Let us now consider any
(G,\mathbf{B})
satisfying
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
with
|V|\geq 3
.
If the graph
G
is not connected, we write
G_{1}=(V_{1},E_{1})
,
G_{2}=(V_{2},E_{2})
,…,
G_{K}=(V_{K},E_{K})
its decomposition into connected components and
\mathbf{B}_{1}
, …,
\mathbf{B}_{K}
the corresponding collection of matrices. Obviously, each
(G_{j},\mathbf{B}_{j})
satisfies the property
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
. Besides, we have
W_{G,\mathbf{B}}=\prod_{j=1}^{K}W_{G_{j},\mathbf{B}_{j}}
. By induction, we conclude that
W_{G,\mathbf{B}}\leq q^{|V|/2}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{(|R|-1)_{+}/2}
. We focus henceforth on the situation where
G
is connected. Since
|V|\geq 3
, this implies that at least one vertex has a degree larger or equal to
3
. The analysis is divided into three cases.

CASE (1)
: there exists a least one vertex whose degree is exactly
3
. Then, we pick any degree
3
vertex
i
of type
r
if there exists one and otherwise we pick any degree
3
vertex
i
of type
c
. There are two sub-cases depending on the number of distinct neighbors of
i
.

CASE (1.a)
: the vertex
i
has three distinct neighbors
j
,
k
, and
l
in
G
–see Figure
2
. Since
W_{G,\mathbf{B}}
is decreasing with the number of edges, we start by removing the edge between
i
and
l
from
E
and the corresponding matrix
\mathbf{B}^{(i,l,1)}
from
\mathbf{B}
. We write
G^{p}
the corresponding pruned graph and
\mathbf{B}^{p}
the corresponding collection of matrices. Focusing on the sum over
w_{i}
in
W_{G,\mathbf{B}}
, we have

\displaystyle W_{G,\mathbf{B}}\leq W_{G^{p},\mathbf{B}^{p}}=\sum_{\omega_{t},\ t\in V\setminus\{i\}}\prod_{e\in E^{(-i)}}\mathbf{B}^{(e)}_{\omega_{e_{1}},\omega_{e_{2}}}\sum_{\omega_{i}}\prod_{e\in E^{p}_{i}}\mathbf{B}^{(e)}_{\omega_{e_{1}},\omega_{e_{2}}}\ ,

where
E^{p}_{i}
is the set of edges incident to
i
.
We denote
e^{\prime}=(i,j,1)
and
e^{\prime\prime}=(i,k,1)
the two edges incident to
i
in
G^{p}
. Then, we introduce the matrix
\mathbf{C}
defined by

\mathbf{C}_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}=\sum_{\omega_{i}}\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}_{\omega_{i},\omega}\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime\prime})}_{\omega_{i},\omega^{\prime}}
,
where the sum over
\omega_{i}
runs over
[p]
if
i
is of type ’
r
’ and over
[q]
if
j
is of type ’
c
’. From Lemma
9.14
, we deduce that

\mathbf{C}/\sqrt{q}
satisfies Condition
(\mathcal{B}^{*})
, so that

W_{G^{p},\mathbf{B}}\leq\sum_{\omega_{t},\ t\in V\setminus\{i\}}\prod_{e\in E^{(-i)}}\mathbf{B}^{(e)}_{\omega_{e_{1}},\omega_{e_{2}}}\mathbf{C}_{\omega_{j},\omega_{k}}\ .

We now consider the modification
\overline{G}
and
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
of
G^{p}
and
\mathbf{B}^{p}
by removing the vertex
i
and, if there are at most one edge between
i
and
k
, we insert a new edge
\overline{e}
between
j
and
k
and associate the matrix
\overline{\mathbf{B}}^{(\overline{e})}=\mathbf{C}/\sqrt{q}
. All the other matrices
\mathbf{B}^{(e)}
are left unchanged. Then, we have

W_{G,\mathbf{B}}\leq\sqrt{q}W_{\overline{G},\overline{\mathbf{B}}}\ .

Among the remaining vertices in
\overline{G}
, only the vertex
l
possibly does not meet the conditions in
(\mathcal{G}^{*})
. If
l
satisfies those condition, then (
\overline{G},\overline{\mathbf{B}}
) satisfies
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
, and by induction, we conclude that

W_{G,\mathbf{B}}\leq\sqrt{q}q^{|\overline{V}|/2}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{(|\overline{R}|-1)_{+}/2}\leq q^{|V|/2}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{(|R|-1)_{+}/2}\ ,

where we denote
\overline{R}
the collection of vertices of type ’
r
’ in
\overline{G}
. If
l
does not satisfy the degree condition in
(\mathcal{G}^{*})
, then this implies that the degree of
l
in
G^{p}
is
2
and that
l
is connected to two distinct vertices, say
m
and
n
. Denoting
e^{\prime}=(l,m,1)
and
e^{\prime\prime}=(l,n,1)
these two edges, we consider as above the matrix
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
defined by
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{a,b}=\sum_{\omega_{l}}\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}_{\omega_{l},a}\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime\prime})}_{\omega_{l},b}
.
Again, the matrix
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}/\sqrt{q}
satisfies
(\mathcal{B}^{*})
by Lemma
9.14
. Then,

W_{\overline{G},\overline{\mathbf{B}}}\leq\sum_{\omega_{s},\ s\in V\setminus\{i,l\}}\prod_{e\in\overline{E}^{-l}}\overline{\mathbf{B}}^{(e)}_{e_{1},e_{2}}\mathbf{C}_{\omega_{m},\omega_{n}}\ .

Finally, we consider the transformations
(\overline{G}^{\prime},\overline{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})
of
(\overline{G},\overline{\mathbf{B}})
where we remove the vertex
l
and, if the number of edges between
m
and
n
is at most one, we insert an edge
\tilde{e}
between
m
and
n
and introduce the corresponding matrix
\overline{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime(\tilde{e})}=\mathbf{C}/\sqrt{q}
. Then,
(\overline{G}^{\prime},\overline{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})
satisfies Condition (
\mathcal{A}^{*}
) and we conclude by induction that

W_{G,\mathbf{B}}\leq qq^{|\overline{V}^{\prime}|/2}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{(|\overline{R}^{\prime}|-1)_{+}/2}\leq q^{|V|/2}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{(|R|-1)_{+}/2}\ .

CASE (1.b)
: the vertex
i
has only two neighbors
j
and
k
in
G
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that there are two edges between
i
and
j
and only one between
i
and
k
. Since
W_{G,\mathbf{B}}
is non-decreasing with the number of edges, we start by removing the edge between
i
and
k
and call
G^{p}
the corresponding pruned graph. We consider again two sub-cases depending on whether the vertex
k
satisfies the degree condition of
(\mathcal{G}^{*})
in the pruned graph
G^{p}
.

CASE (1.b.i)
The vertex
k
does not satisfy the degree condition of
(\mathcal{G}^{*})
in the pruned graph
G^{p}
–see Figure
2
. Then, this implies that, in
G^{p}
,
d_{k}=2
and that
k
is connected to two distinct vertices, say
l
and
m
. argue as in Case 1.a by introducing the matrix
\mathbf{C}
defined by

\mathbf{C}_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}}=\sum_{\omega_{k}}\mathbf{B}^{(e)}_{\omega_{k},\omega}\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}_{\omega_{k},\omega^{\prime}}\ .

where
e
and
e^{\prime}
respectively stand for the edges
e=(k,l,1)
and
e^{\prime}=(k,m,1)
. The matrix
\mathbf{C}/\sqrt{q}
satisfies Condition
(\mathcal{B}^{*})
by Lemma
9.14
. Then, we consider the transformation
(\overline{G},\overline{\mathbf{B}})
of
(G^{p},\mathbf{B}^{p})
, where we remove the vertex
k
and, if the number of edges between
l
and
m
is at most one, we insert an edge
\overline{e}
between
l
and
m
with the matrix
\overline{\mathbf{B}}^{(\overline{e})}=\mathbf{C}/\sqrt{q}
. Then,
(\overline{G},\overline{\mathbf{B}})
satisfies Condition
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
and
W_{G,\mathbf{B}}\leq\sqrt{q}W_{\overline{G},\overline{\mathbf{B}}}
. By induction, we conclude that

W_{G,\mathbf{B}}\leq\sqrt{q}W_{\overline{G},\overline{\mathbf{B}}}\leq q^{|V|/2}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{(|R|-1)_{+}/2}\ .

CASE (1.b.ii)
In the pruned graph
G^{p}
, the vertex
k
satisfies the conditions of
(\mathcal{G}^{*})
. Hence,
(G^{P},\mathbf{B}^{P})
satisfy Condition
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
. We now focus on the vertices
i
and
j
–recall that they are connected by two edges. If the degree of
j
is equal to
2
, then
i
and
j
form a connected component of size
2
in
G^{p}
. Decomposing
G^{p}
into an union of at least two connected components, we conclude by induction as in the beginning of the proof that

W_{G,\mathbf{B}}\leq W_{G^{p},\mathbf{B}^{P}}\leq q^{|V|/2}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{(|R|-1)_{+}/2}\ .

We now assume that the degree of
j
is at most
3
in
G^{p}
–see Figure
2
. Let us denote
e=(i,j,1)
and
e^{\prime}=(i,j,2)
the two edges between
i
and
j
. We derive from Condition
(\mathcal{B}^{*})
and Young’s inequality that, for any
\omega_{j}
,

\sum_{\omega_{i}}\mathbf{B}^{(e)}_{\omega_{i},\omega_{j}}\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}_{\omega_{i},\omega_{j}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\omega_{i}}\left(\mathbf{B}^{(e)}_{\omega_{i},\omega_{j}}\right)^{2}+\left(\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}_{\omega_{i},\omega_{j}}\right)^{2}\leq 1\ .

As a consequence, the new pruned graph
G^{(-i)}
where we have removed the vertex
i
and all edges incident to
i
satisfies

W_{G,\mathbf{B}}\leq W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)}}\ .

If, in
G^{(-i)}
, the vertex
j
satisfies the degree conditions of
(\mathcal{G}^{*})
, then
(G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)})
satisfies
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
and we conclude by induction. If the vertex
j
does not satisfy the degree conditions
(\mathcal{G}^{*})
, then either
d_{j}=1
or
d_{j}=2
and
j
has two distinct neighbors. In the latter case, we have already explained (e.g. in Case (1.b.i)) how to factorize the sum with respect to
\omega_{j}
in
W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)}}
to build a multi-graph
\overline{G}
with vertex set
V\setminus\{i,j\}
and a collection of matrices
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
satisfying Condition
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
and such that

W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)}}\leq\sqrt{q}W_{\overline{G},\overline{\mathbf{B}}}\ .

Then, we conclude by induction. It remains to consider the case where
j
has exactly one neighbor in
G^{(-i)}
. Let us denote
l
this neighbor and
e=(j,l,1)
the corresponding edge. In
W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)}}
we focus on the sum

\sum_{\omega_{j}}\mathbf{B}^{(e)}_{\omega_{j},\omega_{l}}\ .

By Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Condition
(\mathcal{B}^{*})
, this sum is bounded by
\sqrt{q}
if
j
is of type ’
c
’ and by
\sqrt{p}
if
j
is of type ’
r
’. Since the degree of
j
in
G^{(-i)}
is 1, this entails that the degree of
j
in
G
is equal to 3. Hence, if
j
is of type ’
r
’, then this implies that
i
is of type ’
r
’ because we have picked
i
as one of the degree 3 vertices of type ’
r
’ if there exists at least one. Hence, considering the new pruned graph
(G^{(-i,-j)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i,-j)})
where we have also removed the vertex
j
, we arrive at

W_{G,\mathbf{B}}\leq\sqrt{q}\sqrt{\frac{p}{q}}^{{\mathbf{1}}_{i,j\in R}}W_{G^{(-i,-j)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i,-j)}}\ .

In
G^{(-i,-j)}
, only the vertex
l
possibly does not satisfy the degree condition in
(\mathcal{G}^{*})
. Hence, if
l
satisfies the degree conditions in
(\mathcal{G}^{*})
, then
(G^{(-i,-j)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i,-j)})
satisfies
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
and we conclude by induction. Otherwise, the vertex
l
has two distinct neighbors say
m
and
n
in
G^{(-i,-j)}
with corresponding edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
. As already argued several times, we focus the sum over
\omega_{l}
of
\mathbf{B}^{(e)}_{\omega_{l},\omega_{m}}\mathbf{B}^{(e^{\prime})}_{\omega_{l},\omega_{n}}
. This allows us to build a graph
\overline{G}
with vertex set
V\setminus\{i,j,l\}
and a collection
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
of matrices that satisfy Condition
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
and such that

W_{G^{(-i,-j)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i,-j)}}\leq\sqrt{q}W_{\overline{G},\overline{\mathbf{B}}}\ .

Then, we conclude by induction.

CASE
(2)
: The degree of all vertices in
G
is at least
4
. Since the sum
W_{G,\mathbf{B}}
does not decrease when we remove edges in
G
, we can iteratively prune the graph by removing some edges until one vertex has a degree
3
. If the pruned graph
G^{p}
is not connected, we conclude as in the beginning of the proof. If the pruned graph is connected, then we come back to Case
1
.

CASE
(3)
: No vertex has a degree
3
and there exists at least one vertex with degree
2
. We pick one of the degree
2
vertices, say
i
. Denoting
j
the neighbor of
i
in
G
, we observe that the degree of
j
in
G
is at least
4
since
G
is connected and since
d_{j}\neq 3
. Arguing exactly as in Case 1.ii.b, we observe that
(G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)})
where we have removed the vertex
i
and the two edges incident to
i
satisfies

W_{G,\mathbf{B}}\leq W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)}}\ .

If, in
G^{(-i)}
,
j
satisfies the degree conditions of
(\mathcal{G}^{*})
, then
(G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)})
satisfies Condition
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
and we conclude by induction. If
j
does not satisfy the degree conditions of
\mathcal{G}
in
G^{(-i)}
, then
d_{j}=2
and
j
has two distinct neighbors, say
k
and
l
–see Figure
2
. As argued several times, we prune the vertex
j
and possibly create an edge between
k
and
l
to build
(\overline{G},\overline{\mathbf{B}})
satisfying
(\mathcal{A}^{*})
and such that

W_{G^{(-i)},\mathbf{B}^{(-i)}}\leq\sqrt{q}W_{\overline{G},\overline{\mathbf{B}}}\ .

Then, we conclude by induction.

∎

Proof of Lemma
9.14
.

Let us denote
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{C}_{1}\mathbf{C}_{2}
. We only have to prove that
\|\mathbf{D}\|_{2}\leq q
and that the
l_{2}
norms of each row and column is smaller or equal to
\sqrt{q}
. Since the Frobenius norm is sub-multiplicative (as long as all Schatten norms), we deduce that

\|\mathbf{D}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathbf{C}_{1}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{C}_{2}\|_{2}\leq q\ ,

since both
\mathbf{C}_{1}
and
\mathbf{C}_{2}
satisfy
(\mathcal{B}^{*})
. Consider that
a
-th row of
\mathbf{D}
. Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{b=1}^{t}\mathbf{D}^{2}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{b=1}^{t}\left[\sum_{c=1}^{s}(\mathbf{C}_{1})_{a,c}(\mathbf{C}_{2})_{c,b}\right]^{2}\leq\sum_{b=1}^{t}\left[\sum_{c=1}^{s}(\mathbf{C}_{1})^{2}_{a,c}\right]\left[\sum_{c^{\prime}=1}^{s}(\mathbf{C}_{2})^{2}_{c^{\prime},b}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{b=1}^{t}\sum_{c^{\prime}=1}^{s}(\mathbf{C}_{2})^{2}_{c^{\prime},b}\leq q\kern 5.0pt,

where we used in the second line that the
l_{2}
norm of the
a
-th row of
\mathbf{C}_{1}
is at most one and that the Frobenius norm of
\mathbf{C}_{2}
is at most
q
.
∎

9.6
Proof of Technical Lemmas

Proof of Lemma
9.1
.

Given a positive integer
k
, we write
a_{l,k}
for the coefficients of the decomposition of the
k
-th Hermite polynomial in the canonical basis, that is
H_{k}(x)=\sum_{l=0}^{k}a_{l,k}x^{l}
. First, we consider the case where
k
is even. Since
H_{k}
is a symmetric polynomial, we derive that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{k}(Y)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{k/2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[a_{2l,k}(\theta+E)^{2l}]=\sum_{s=0}^{k}\theta^{s}\sum_{l=\lceil s/2\rceil}^{k/2}a_{2l,k}\binom{2l}{2l-s}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E^{2l-s}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{k,k}\theta^{k}+a_{k,k}\theta^{k-1}k\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E]+\theta^{k-2}\left[a_{k,k}\frac{k(k-1)}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E^{2}]+a_{k-2,k}\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{s=0}^{k-3}\theta^{s}\sum_{l=\lceil s/2\rceil}^{k/2}a_{2l,k}\binom{2l}{2l-s}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E^{2l-s}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta^{k}+\sum_{s=0}^{k-3}\theta^{s}\sum_{l=\lceil s/2\rceil}^{k/2}a_{2l,k}\binom{2l}{2l-s}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E^{2l-s}]\ ,

because
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E]=0
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E^{2}]=1
,
a_{k,k}=1
and
a_{k,k}\frac{k(k-1)}{2}+a_{k-2,k}=0
. The two latter identities are a consequence of the equality
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{k}(\theta+Z)]=\theta^{k}
when
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Similarly, if
k
is odd, we derive that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{k}(Y)]=\theta^{k}+\sum_{s=0}^{k-3}\theta^{s}\sum_{l=\lceil(s-1)/2\rceil}^{(k-1)/2}a_{2l+1,k}\binom{2l+1}{2l+1-s}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E^{2l+1-s}]\ .

Next, we upper bound the coefficients of the polynomial
P_{k}
. For
l\equiv k[2]
, it is well known that the coefficient
a_{l,k}
of the Hermite polynomial satisfies

a_{l,k}=\frac{k!}{2^{k}l![(k-l)/2]!}(-1)^{(k-l)/2}\ ,

so that

|a_{l,k}|\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}\binom{k}{l}(k-l)^{(k-l)/2}\leq(k-l)^{(k-l)/2}\leq k^{k/2}\ .

(96)

Besides, we know (e.g.
[
39
]
) that
|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E^{k}]|\leq(c\|E\|_{\psi_{2}}\sqrt{k})^{k}
for some positive numerical constant
c
.
Writing
P_{k}(x)=\sum_{l=0}^{k-3}b_{l,k}x^{k}
, we derive that
|b_{l,k}|\leq c^{k}\|E\|_{\psi_{2}}^{k}k^{k+1}2^{k}
.
Let us turn to
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{k}H_{l}(Y)]
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{k}H_{l}(Y)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{k+l}\sum_{t=(s-l)\vee 0}^{s\wedge k}a_{t,k}a_{s-t,l}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[(\theta+E)^{s}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{k+l}\theta^{r}\left[\sum_{s=r}^{k+l}\sum_{t=(s-l)\vee 0}^{s\wedge k}a_{t,k}a_{s-t,l}\binom{s}{r}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E^{s-r}]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta^{k+l}+\sum_{r=0}^{k+l-2}\theta^{r}\left[\sum_{s=r}^{k+l}\sum_{t=(s-l)\vee 0}^{s\wedge k}a_{t,k}a_{s-t,l}\binom{s}{r}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[E^{s-r}]\right]\ ,

because
a_{k,k}=a_{l,l}=1
and
a_{k-1,k}=a_{l-1,l}=0
. The rhs in the above identity corresponds to a polynomial of degree at most
k+l-2
. Arguing as above, we derive that its coefficients are uniformly bounded (in absolute value) by
(c\|E\|_{\psi_{2}})^{(k+l)}(k+l)^{2+(k+l)}
.

Finally, we consider the case where

k+l=2=k\vee l
. Then, we observe that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{k}(Y)H_{l}(Y)]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[H_{2}(Y)]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[(\theta+E)^{2}-1]=\theta^{2}
so that
Q_{k,l}=0
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
9.2
.

We prove the first result by induction on the size of
v_{\mathcal{P}}=\sum_{r}|\mathcal{P}_{r}|{\mathbf{1}}_{|\mathcal{P}_{r}|>1}
. If
v_{\mathcal{P}}=0
, then
G_{\mathcal{P}}=0
by definition. Consider any partition
\mathcal{P}
such that
v_{\mathcal{P}}>0
,
\mathcal{P}
contains a group, say
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, of size
2
satisfying
\mathcal{P}_{1}\subset[2k]
or
\mathcal{P}_{1}\subset[2k+1,4k]
. We build the finer collection
\mathcal{P}^{(-1)}\triangleleft\mathcal{P}
by breaking
\mathcal{P}_{1}
into two singletons. Then,

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{P}}(m)

\displaystyle=
